<h4>Importance</h4>Rigorous evidence for inhaled sedation using isoflurane in mechanically ventilated patients with acute hypoxemic respiratory failure (AHRF) is limited.<h4>Objective</h4>To evaluate whether an inhaled sedation strategy lowers hospital mortality compared with usual care intravenous sedation strategy in mechanically ventilated adults with AHRF.<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>Pragmatic, effectiveness, open-label, superiority, randomized clinical trial in 14 intensive care units (ICUs) in Canada. Adults (≥18 years) receiving sedation for less than 72 hours and receiving mechanical ventilation for AHRF from non-COVID respiratory failure (ratio of partial pressure of arterial oxygen [Pao2] to fraction of inspired oxygen [Fio2] <300 mm Hg) or COVID-19 were enrolled between September 2020 and March 2025 with follow-up through June 2025.<h4>Intervention</h4>Patients were randomized to an inhaled sedation strategy using isoflurane with intravenous adjuncts as clinically needed or to a usual intravenous sedation care strategy. Sedatives were titrated to achieve the desired target sedation score guided by clinical teams and continued until extubation or death.<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>Primary outcome was all-cause hospital mortality. Secondary outcomes included ICU- and ventilator-free days at day 30; hospital-free days at day 60; use of refractory hypoxemia adjunctive therapies; delirium- and coma-free days at day 14; and quality-of-life and disability scores at 3 months.<h4>Results</h4>Of 786 patients randomized, 780 were included in the primary analysis (mean age, 61.1 years; 34.0% female; 73.7% non-COVID AHRF, mean Pao2:Fio2 ratio, 152.6 mm Hg); 385 in the inhaled sedation and 395 in the intravenous sedation groups. In the inhaled sedation group, 49.9% to 55.4% of patients received concomitant intravenous sedation on any day during the first 14 days after randomization. At hospital discharge, 161 of 379 patients (42.5%) in the inhaled sedation group had died, compared with 171 of 390 patients (43.8%) in the intravenous sedation group (risk ratio, 0.98 [95% CI, 0.84 to 1.14]; P = .85). There was no effect modification in prespecified subgroup analyses for age, sex, and COVID-19 status. ICU-, ventilator-, and hospital-free days were similar between groups. The inhaled sedation group received more adjunctive therapies for hypoxemia (239 [63.9%] vs 214 [55.0%]; between-group difference, 8.89% [95% CI, 1.95% to 15.83%]) and had fewer delirium- and coma-free days (between-group difference, -0.95 [95% CI, -1.69 to -0.22]). There was no difference in quality-of-life and disability scores between groups.<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>In mechanically ventilated adults with AHRF, an inhaled sedation strategy using isoflurane with intravenous adjuncts as clinically needed did not reduce hospital mortality compared with an intravenous sedation strategy.<h4>Trial registration</h4>ClinicalTrials.gov Identifier: NCT04415060.

### [Clinician Use of an Artificial Intelligence-Enabled Clinical Information Platform.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.21868)

- Source: `jama_abstracts` · published `2026-10-15` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42879271`

<h4>Importance</h4>Despite growing interest in the use of artificial intelligence (AI) in medicine, empirical evidence on how clinicians use these tools is limited.<h4>Objective</h4>To characterize clinician use of an AI-enabled clinical information platform, the distribution of use across the clinical workforce, and the purpose of submitted queries.<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>This cross-sectional study analyzed clinicians with National Provider Identifier-verified accounts of a commonly used AI-enabled clinical information platform, OpenEvidence, from January 1, 2024, through June 30, 2026. A large language model was used to classify the clinical purpose and topic areas of a stratified random sample of 38.8 million queries.<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>The number of active users and clinical queries, the share of queries relative to representation in the national workforce, and the clinical purpose and topic of queries were examined.<h4>Results</h4>Overall, 952 008 clinicians (58.5% female), including 556 200 physicians, submitted 271 million queries during the 2.5-year study period. Monthly active users increased from 355 in January 2024 to 703 265 in June 2026, and average monthly queries per active user rose from 18.0 to 39.5. Nurse practitioners (44.5) and physician assistants (44.5) submitted more queries per active user-month than physicians (37.1). A higher share of queries relative to the national workforce was submitted by physicians within a decade of graduation (use ratio, 2.54) and residents and fellows (1.84). Use was also higher than average among physicians specializing in oncology and hematology (use ratio, 2.00), internal medicine (1.88), and family medicine (1.80), but lower in procedural and diagnostic specialties (ophthalmology, 0.23; radiology, 0.24; orthopedics, 0.24). Most queries addressed clinical decision-making (51.9%), particularly treatment and management (33.1%), whereas diagnosis accounted for 13.9% of queries. Queries addressing topics outside physicians' own specialties ranged from 35% among oncologists and hematologists to 88% among emergency department physicians.<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>Over 2.5 years, more than 950 000 US clinicians submitted greater than 270 million queries to an AI-enabled clinical information platform. Use varied across clinician types, specialties, and career stages and was frequently for treatment decisions rather than diagnostic tasks.

Medicare Program; Implementation of Sections 6219, 6220, and 6224 of the Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2026 (CMS-4214-P) Background On Oct. 16, 2026, the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS) issued a proposed rule to implement key sections of the Consolidated Appropriations Act of 2026 including section 6220 (Requiring Enhanced and Accurate Lists of (REAL) Health Providers Act) and section 6224 (Modernizing and Ensuring Pharmacy Benefit Manager Accountability). This rule also proposes a conforming change mandated by section 6219 of the CAA (Adjustments to Medicare Part D Cost-Sharing Reductions for Low-Income Individuals) that would reduce the generic drug copayment from $1 to $0 for full-benefit dual eligible individuals. Implementing Certain Provisions of Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2026 On February 3, 2026, the CAA of 2026, which included several changes to the MA and Part D programs, was signed into law. In this rule, we are proposing to implement the following sections: Adjustments to Medicare Part D Cost-Sharing Reductions for Low-Income Individuals (Section 6219): This proposed rule implements Section 6219 of the CAA that would reduce the generic drug copayment from $1 to $0 for full-benefit dual eligible individuals at or below 100% of the Federal Poverty Level, effective for plan years starting Jan. 1, 2028. Requiring Enhanced and Accurate Lists of (REAL) Health Providers Act (Section 6220): CMS proposes to codify regulations to implement the REAL Health Providers Act, which establishes new provider directory verification and accuracy requirements for MA organizations beginning in contract year 2028. Specifically, CMS proposes requirements to implement this new law related to provider directory verification and maintenance standards, annual accuracy analysis and reporting, public reporting of accuracy scores, and cost-sharing protections for enrollees who rely on inaccurate provider directory information. CMS also proposes to codify the statutory requirements for contract year 2029, including that CMS must publicly display provider directory accuracy scores in a machine-readable format, and MA organizations must prominently display those scores in their provider directories. Through these proposed codifications implementing the REAL Health Providers Act, CMS seeks to promote provider directory accuracy and accountability among MA organizations to ensure that beneficiaries are provided with clear, accurate information on the network of providers available to meet their healthcare needs. Modernizing and Ensuring Pharmacy Benefit Manager Accountability (Section 6224): CMS has several proposals to implement section 6224 of the CAA, which established requirements related to pharmacy benefit managers (PBMs) and their affiliates specific to the Medicare Part D program. These proposals include new requirements governing the remuneration that PBMs and their affiliates may receive for services in connection with the utilization of Part D drugs, data reporting by PBMs to Part D prescription drug plan (PDP) sponsors and CMS, transparency in PBM price guarantee arrangements, disclosure by PBMs to PDP sponsors of the terms of certain agreements between PBMs or their affiliates and pharmaceutical manufacturers, and other requirements added by the CAA. CMS also proposes to define key terms related to such requirements and establish compliance and enforcement mechanisms, including disgorgement of funds to CMS for violations of the remuneration requirements. The proposed rule will be open for public comment for 60 days following publication in the Federal Register. View this regulation on the Federal Register . The proposed rule will be open for public comment for 60 days following publication in the Federal Register. ### CMS News and Media Group Catherine Howden, Director Media Inquiries Form 202-690-6145

### [A Common CD36 Variant and the Genetic Landscape of Dilated Cardiomyopathy in Individuals of African Ancestry.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.18449)

- Source: `jama_abstracts` · published `2026-10-19` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42895716`

<h4>Importance</h4>Dilated cardiomyopathy (DCM) is a major cause of heart failure that disproportionately affects individuals of predominantly African ancestry, among whom familial clustering of disease is more pronounced relative to those of predominantly European ancestry. Truncating variants in TTN, which encodes the sarcomeric protein titin, are the most common established monogenic cause of DCM. A common CD36 nonsense variant enriched among individuals of African ancestry has also been shown to account for a substantial burden of DCM.<h4>Objective</h4>To compare the contribution of a CD36 nonsense variant to DCM risk with that of truncating variants in TTN and pathogenic or likely pathogenic variants in other established genes for DCM.<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>Multicohort genetic association study including participants of African and European ancestry with exome or genome sequence data and DCM case status from the following 4 US datasets: All of Us Research Program (recruitment: 2017-present; last follow-up: August 27, 2024), Million Veteran Program (recruitment: 2011-present; last follow-up: September 30, 2024), Penn Medicine Biobank (recruitment: 2013-present; last follow-up: November 14, 2022), and the DCM Precision Medicine Study (recruitment: 2016-2020; last follow-up: March 15, 2020).<h4>Exposure</h4>Carrier status for TTN truncating variants, pathogenic or likely pathogenic variants in 11 high-confidence genes for DCM, and the nonsense variant CD36 Y325* (0, 1, or 2 copies).<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>Risk of DCM, prevalence of variant carriers among DCM cases, and population attributable fraction of DCM.<h4>Results</h4>Among 82 623 individuals of predominantly African ancestry across 4 cohorts, the mean age was 53.4 years (SD, 13.9 years), 47 124 were male, and 1316 had DCM. Individuals who were homozygous for the CD36 Y325* risk allele had a higher risk of DCM (odds ratio [OR], 4.8; 95% CI, 3.1-7.3) as did those who were heterozygous (OR, 1.4; 95% CI, 1.2-1.7). TTN truncating variants also conferred an elevated risk of DCM in participants of African ancestry (OR, 8.5; 95% CI, 5.5-13.0). Among 1316 individuals of African ancestry classified as DCM cases, 33 (2.5%) were homozygous for CD36, 56 (4.3%) had TTN truncating variants, and 16 (1.2%) had pathogenic or likely pathogenic variants in other high-confidence genes for DCM. In population-level analyses incorporating both heterozygous and homozygous CD36 Y325* carriers, the population attributable fraction for CD36 (9.0% [6.2% for heterozygous carriers and 2.8% for homozygous carriers]) surpassed the population attributable fraction for TTN truncating variants (3.7%).<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>In 4 US cohorts, an ancestry-specific CD36 variant contributed more to DCM burden among individuals of African ancestry than established genes for DCM, including TTN truncating variants that are typically considered the most common genetic cause. These findings reshape the known genetic architecture of DCM in individuals of African ancestry and highlight the importance of representation in genomic research.

### [Individualized Cardiac Index-Guided Hemodynamic Therapy in High-Risk Patients Undergoing Elective Major Abdominal Surgery: The PELICAN Randomized Clinical Trial.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.18574)

<h4>Importance</h4>It remains unclear if intraoperative hemodynamic therapy should be guided by cardiac index.<h4>Objective</h4>To evaluate whether individualized cardiac index-guided hemodynamic therapy vs routine hemodynamic therapy improves outcomes in high-risk patients undergoing elective major abdominal surgery.<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>This multicenter, single-blind, superiority randomized clinical trial enrolled high-risk patients undergoing elective major abdominal surgery between October 8, 2023, and October 25, 2025, at 12 university medical centers in Austria, the Czech Republic, Denmark, Germany, and Spain. The date of last follow-up was January 15, 2026.<h4>Interventions</h4>Patients were randomized to individualized cardiac index-guided hemodynamic therapy targeting the patient's preoperative resting cardiac index vs routine hemodynamic therapy (with blinded cardiac index monitoring).<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>The primary outcome was a composite of acute kidney injury, acute myocardial injury, severe infectious complications, nonfatal cardiac arrest, or death within the first 7 postoperative days. Secondary outcomes included the individual components of the primary outcome within the first 7 postoperative days.<h4>Results</h4>Of the 1128 patients enrolled, 1111 were randomized and 1095 were included in the primary analysis (median age, 67 years [IQR, 59-74 years]; 49.4% were female). The primary outcome occurred in 243 of 549 patients (44.3%) in the individualized cardiac index-guided hemodynamic therapy group vs 256 of 546 patients (46.9%) in the routine hemodynamic therapy group (relative risk, 0.94 [95.1% CI, 0.83-1.08]; P = .38). There were no between-group differences for the incidence of the individual components of the primary outcome within the first 7 postoperative days.<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>Among high-risk patients undergoing elective major abdominal surgery, individualized cardiac index-guided hemodynamic therapy targeting the patient's preoperative resting cardiac index did not reduce the incidence of a composite outcome of acute kidney injury, acute myocardial injury, severe infectious complications, nonfatal cardiac arrest, or death within the first 7 postoperative days compared with routine hemodynamic therapy.<h4>Trial registration</h4>ClinicalTrials.gov Identifier: NCT05648279.

### [Diagnosis and Treatment of Cardiovascular Conditions in Medicare Advantage In-Home Health Risk Assessments.](https://doi.org/10.7326/annals-26-01366)

- Source: `annals_internal_abstracts` · published `2026-10-20` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42896184`

<h4>Background</h4>Medicare Advantage insurers conduct in-home health risk assessments (HRAs) to identify high-risk patients and facilitate appropriate care. Critics argue this practice increases risk-adjusted payments without improving quality. Comprehensive data on use of in-home HRAs and downstream treatment are lacking.<h4>Objective</h4>To examine trends in cardiovascular conditions diagnosed during in-home HRAs, characteristics associated with HRA-based diagnoses, and treatment rates after diagnoses.<h4>Design</h4>Cohort study.<h4>Setting</h4>Population-based.<h4>Participants</h4>Medicare Advantage beneficiaries.<h4>Measurements</h4>Using 100% Medicare claims, trends in the proportion of cardiovascular conditions diagnosed during in-home HRAs (vs. facility-based visits) were examined. Multivariable logistic regression models were fit to estimate the association between beneficiary- and plan-level characteristics and HRA-based diagnoses and compare treatment rates across settings.<h4>Results</h4>Among 21 496 506 Medicare Advantage beneficiaries diagnosed with a cardiovascular risk factor or disease, the proportion diagnosed during in-home HRAs increased between 2018 and 2022 for hypertension (45.9% to 47.7%), hyperlipidemia (57.8% to 66.7%), diabetes (47.8% to 51.6%), atrial fibrillation (30.0% to 36.0%), ischemic heart disease (37.2% to 41.6%), peripheral vascular disease (64.5% to 73.9%), and heart failure (34.7% to 42.6%). Racial and ethnic minority beneficiaries and those enrolled in lower-rated plans were more likely to receive HRA-based diagnoses. Treatment rates were substantially lower after HRA-based diagnoses across all conditions.<h4>Limitations</h4>Differences in the severity of conditions between groups could not be assessed. Prescription fills were used as a proxy for management. Observed differences in the diagnosis and treatment of conditions across encounter settings may be susceptible to unmeasured confounding.<h4>Conclusion</h4>Among Medicare Advantage beneficiaries, an increasingly large share of cardiovascular conditions are being diagnosed through in-home HRAs, but treatment rates after these diagnoses are consistently lower than after facility-based encounters.<h4>Primary funding source</h4>National Heart, Lung, and Blood Institute.

### [Effectiveness of a structured template to improve completeness of reporting randomised trial protocols: randomised controlled trial.](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj-2026-100917)

- Source: `bmj_abstracts` · published `2026-10-20` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42901609`

<h4>Objective</h4>To evaluate whether providing authors with a manuscript template structured according to the Standard Protocol Items: Recommendations for Interventional Trials (SPIRIT) 2025 reporting guideline for use during revision improves the completeness of reporting in randomised trial protocols.<h4>Design</h4>Randomised controlled trial.<h4>Setting</h4>BMJ Open.<h4>Population</h4>70 of 74 manuscripts screened between 29 April and 27 May 2025 that reported randomised controlled trial protocols with a decision of minor or major revision after the first round of peer review were randomly allocated (1:1) using an R generated sequence. Authors were not informed of the study.<h4>Interventions</h4>Handling editors asked the intervention group authors to use a SPIRIT based manuscript template when preparing their first revision. The template included structured subheadings corresponding to SPIRIT 2025 items, along with guidance on reporting. Editorial decisions were not conditional on use of the template. Control group manuscripts underwent standard peer review.<h4>Main outcome measures</h4>The primary outcome was completeness of reporting score in the revised manuscript (adjusted for baseline), calculated as the mean of 11 SPIRIT items that are frequently inadequately reported (each scored 0, 0.5, or 1) and linearly rescaled to a 0-10 score. After submission of their first revision, authors in the intervention group completed an online survey. The survey included both fixed response and open ended questions designed to capture the authors' perceptions of ease of use, helpfulness, and any challenges encountered when using the template. Secondary outcomes included mean completeness of reporting scores for each item, median time to submission of the revised manuscript, and authors' experiences of using the template. Outcome assessors, but not editors or investigators, were masked.<h4>Results</h4>Of the 70 manuscripts randomised (35 in each group), all were analysed, with no loss to follow-up. In the intervention group, completeness of reporting in randomised trial protocols improved by 0.61 points (95% confidence interval 0.20 to 1.01) compared with the control group, with mean (standard deviation) scores increasing from 5.42 (1.07) to 6.70 (1.23) and 5.77 (1.19) to 6.32 (0.94), respectively. The largest improvements were observed for recruitment, data collection methods, and access to data. Median time to submission of the first revision was similar between the intervention and control groups (27.10 <i>v</i> 29.86 days, respectively). Data on harms were not systematically collected. Among 14 of 35 survey respondents (40%), many (8/13, 62%) reported the template as time consuming and some (5/13, 39%) as burdensome. Most respondents, however, found it easy to use (10/13, 77%), helpful for improving the manuscript (9/13, 69%), and reassuring for adherence to SPIRIT (13/13, 100%).<h4>Conclusions</h4>Providing authors of randomised controlled trials with a SPIRIT based template during manuscript revision modestly improved the completeness of reporting in the trial protocols without extending revision times.<h4>Trial registration</h4>Open Science Framework 10.17605/OSF.IO/C4YQZ.

### [Overuse and False Restraint in Large Language Model Recommendations for Choosing Wisely Scenarios.](https://doi.org/10.7326/annals-26-01667)

- Source: `annals_internal_abstracts` · published `2026-10-20` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42896192`

<h4>Background</h4>The Choosing Wisely initiative made recommendations where screening, testing, or treatment may be overused, with caveats around appropriate use. Clinicians are increasingly using large language models (LLMs).<h4>Objective</h4>To evaluate whether commercial LLMs appropriately call for restraint or action from scenarios based on Choosing Wisely recommendations.<h4>Design</h4>Benchmark evaluation. Fifteen Choosing Wisely recommendations were used to develop 225 scenarios (per recommendation, 10 in which restraint was appropriate and 5 in which action was appropriate). Six commercial LLMs (3 runs each) generated 4050 responses to the 225 scenarios, using a prompt from the clinician perspective.<h4>Setting</h4>Production application programming interfaces of OpenAI, Anthropic, and Google from February to March 2026.<h4>Participants</h4>No human participants were involved.<h4>Measurements</h4>Co-primary outcomes were the overuse rate (endorsing action when restraint was appropriate) and the false restraint rate (withholding an appropriate action).<h4>Results</h4>The rate of LLM-recommended overuse was 5.4% (95% CI, 0.6% to 12.2%); LLMs did not recommend overuse in 8 of 15 recommendations. Two recommendations accounted for most LLM-recommended overuse: thyroid ultrasound for an incidental subcentimeter nodule (overuse recommended in 42.8% of LLM responses) and routine electrocardiography in low-risk adults (overuse recommended in 28.3% of LLM responses). The LLMs recommended overuse in 0.6% to 3.3% of responses for the remaining 5 recommendations. False restraint was rare (0.6% [CI, 0.1% to 1.2%]), was absent for 11 of 15 recommendations, and was highest for prostate-specific antigen screening (3.3%).<h4>Limitations</h4>Single prompts without follow-up; a benchmark rather than real-world use; responses classified by LLM judge with physician adjudication. Findings may not extend to consumer-facing use.<h4>Conclusion</h4>The LLMs rarely withheld recommendations for treatment when it was appropriate. Overuse was concentrated in 2 recommendations.<h4>Primary funding source</h4>None.

### [Projected Effect of 2026 Federal Changes to the Childhood Immunization Schedule on Chronic Hepatitis B Virus Infections in the United States.](https://doi.org/10.7326/annals-26-02135)

- Source: `annals_internal_abstracts` · published `2026-10-20` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42896183`

<h4>Background</h4>The United States recently proposed changes to the recommendations for childhood hepatitis B virus (HBV) vaccination.<h4>Objective</h4>To project the effect of vaccine policy changes, if fully implemented, on chronic HBV infections.<h4>Design</h4>Markov cohort state transition model of perinatal and childhood HBV infection.<h4>Data sources</h4>Published data on maternal HBV prevalence (0.4%) and hepatitis B surface antigen testing (99.5% sensitivity), childhood vaccine completion (universal birth-dose strategy: 91.1%; deferred strategy: 87.1%; shared decision-making strategy: 50.0%), and age-stratified risk for chronic HBV infection.<h4>Target population</h4>One-year birth cohort of U.S.-born infants (<i>n</i> = 3.6 million).<h4>Time horizon</h4>20 years.<h4>Intervention</h4>Universal birth-dose vaccination, deferred (2-month) vaccination, and vaccination through shared decision making.<h4>Outcome measures</h4>Numbers with chronic HBV infection and susceptibility to HBV, with 95% uncertainty intervals (UIs) derived from probabilistic sensitivity analysis.<h4>Results of base-case analysis</h4>Compared with the universal birth-dose strategy (255 childhood infections [95% UI, 130 to 530]), the deferred and shared decision-making strategies would increase chronic HBV infections in the 2026 birth cohort by 19% to 303 (95% UI, 155 to 610) and by 83% to 467 (95% UI, 209 to 1032), respectively. These strategies would also increase the number of HBV-susceptible people at year 20 (474 000 and 1 806 000) compared with the universal birth-dose strategy (232 000).<h4>Results of sensitivity analysis</h4>In a scenario with lower vaccine coverage (84.5%) and higher HBV prevalence (maternal prevalence of 0.72% and higher risk for horizontal infection), chronic HBV infections would increase to 1700 and 2192, respectively, with the deferred and shared decision-making strategies.<h4>Limitation</h4>Uncertainty in horizontal HBV infection risk.<h4>Conclusion</h4>Proposed policy changes for vaccination through shared decision making are projected to result in an 83% increase in childhood HBV infections, and between 50% and 70% of children entering adulthood annually would be susceptible to HBV infection. Policymakers should reimplement recommendations for universal birth-dose vaccination.<h4>Primary funding source</h4>National Institute of Allergy and Infectious Diseases.

### [Acetic acid chromoendoscopy for detection of neoplastic Barrett's oesophagus: stepped wedge cluster randomised controlled trial (ACID-trial).](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj-2026-100872)

- Source: `bmj_abstracts` · published `2026-10-21` · freshness `stale`

<h4>Objective</h4>To assess the added value of acetic acid chromoendoscopy (AAC) for the detection of dysplasia and oesophageal adenocarcinoma during Barrett's oesophagus surveillance endoscopies.<h4>Design</h4>Prospective, open label, stepped wedge cluster, randomised trial.<h4>Setting</h4>18 Dutch community hospitals.<h4>Participants</h4>Patients undergoing surveillance for Barrett's oesophagus were eligible, excluding those with known or previously treated dysplasia.<h4>Interventions</h4>Hospitals randomly shifted from surveillance without AAC (control) to AAC (intervention), with endoscopists trained before implementation. Targeted biopsies were obtained if lesions were present; random four quadrant biopsies were conducted.<h4>Main outcome measures</h4>The primary endpoint was the neoplasia detection rate, defined as the percentage of participants with low grade dysplasia, high grade dysplasia, and oesophageal adenocarcinoma in any biopsy, confirmed by an experienced gastrointestinal pathologist. The secondary endpoint was the neoplasia detection rate in targeted biopsies.<h4>Results</h4>2267 participants were included, of whom 1438 (63%) were allocated to surveillance without AAC and 829 (37%) to surveillance with AAC; AAC was applied in 679 (82%) allocated participants. Overall, neoplasia was diagnosed in 163 (7.2%) participants (111 (4.9%) low grade dysplasia, 30 (1.3%) high grade dysplasia, and 22 (1.0%) oesophageal adenocarcinoma). AAC use did not increase the neoplasia detection rate (7.4% (50/679) with AAC versus 7.1% (113/1588) without AAC; odds ratio 0.97, 95% confidence interval 0.65 to 1.44; adjusted for calendar time and Barrett's oesophagus length). Suspected lesions were detected in 95 (14.0%) participants receiving AAC versus 101 (6.4%) without AAC (P<0.001), but the targeted neoplasia detection rate was not higher (2.1% (14/679) versus 1.8% (29/1588); odds ratio 1.05, 0.49 to 2.24).<h4>Conclusions</h4>Use of AAC did not improve detection of neoplasia in patients with Barrett's oesophagus undergoing endoscopic surveillance in routine clinical practice.<h4>Trial registration</h4>www.onderzoekmetmensen.nl; NL-OMON25059 (prospective registration; 4 Dec 2019).

### [Childhood Cancer Risk After Assisted Reproductive Technology.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.18862)

<h4>Importance</h4>Childhood cancer risk after assisted reproductive technology (ART) remains uncertain, particularly by procedure and cancer type.<h4>Objective</h4>To examine associations between ART and childhood cancer overall and by ART modality and cancer type.<h4>Design, setting, and population</h4>Nationwide Danish cohort of 1 086 136 live-born children from 1996 to 2012, of whom 37 228 (3.4%) were conceived using ART, with follow-up through 2023.<h4>Exposures</h4>Maternal ART, classified as in vitro fertilization (IVF), intracytoplasmic sperm injection, or frozen embryo transfer.<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>Incident childhood cancer. Cox proportional hazards models estimated hazard ratios (HRs); Poisson models estimated incidence rates. Non-ART births were the primary reference; secondary analyses used children of women with a history of infertility and children conceived using IVF as reference groups.<h4>Results</h4>During a median follow-up of 20 years, 3736 cancers occurred. Compared with non-ART births (incidence rate, 18.3 per 100 000 person-years), children conceived using ART had higher cancer incidence (incidence rate, 23.6 per 100 000; HR, 1.26 [95% CI, 1.07-1.48]). Rates were higher after frozen embryo transfer (incidence rate, 41.1 per 100 000; HR, 2.18 [95% CI, 1.45-3.29]) and intracytoplasmic sperm injection (incidence rate, 27.9 per 100 000; HR, 1.48 [95% CI, 1.16-1.89]) but not IVF (incidence rate, 18.5 per 100 000; HR, 1.01 [95% CI, 0.79-1.28]). Frozen embryo transfer was associated with leukemia (incidence rate, 11.1 per 100 000; HR, 2.36 [95% CI, 1.06-5.28]), lymphomas (HR, 3.03 [95% CI, 1.13-8.12]), and soft tissue sarcomas (HR, 7.63 [95% CI, 2.82-20.62]); intracytoplasmic sperm injection was associated with lymphomas (incidence rate, 4.6 per 100 000; HR, 1.94 [95% CI, 1.07-3.54]), soft tissue sarcomas (incidence rate, 3.3 per 100 000; HR, 3.28 [95% CI, 1.54-7.00]), and central nervous system neoplasms (incidence rate, 8.2 per 100 000; HR, 1.63 [95% CI, 1.04-2.57]); no associations were observed for IVF. Findings were similar in secondary analyses using children of women with a history of infertility and children conceived using IVF as reference groups.<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>ART, particularly frozen embryo transfer and intracytoplasmic sperm injection, was associated with increased childhood cancer risk, but absolute risks were low. The consistent results in secondary analyses of children with maternal infertility history or conceived via IVF suggest that procedure-related, rather than infertility-related, factors warrant further investigation.

### [Disease-free survival and overall survival after neoadjuvant immunotherapy in mismatch repair-deficient colon cancer in the Netherlands: results of the single-arm, multicentre, phase 2, NICHE-2 trial.](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0140-6736(26)01602-8)

- Source: `lancet_abstracts` · published `2026-10-21` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42906564`

<h4>Background</h4>Neoadjuvant immune checkpoint blockade induces high pathological response rates in patients with locally advanced mismatch repair-deficient (dMMR) colon cancer, yet long-term survival outcomes have not been established. Previously, we reported safety (a primary endpoint) and pathological response (a secondary endpoint) data from the NICHE-2 trial. Here, we report the primary efficacy endpoint of 3-year disease-free survival and the exploratory endpoint of 5-year overall survival.<h4>Methods</h4>In this single-arm, multicentre, phase 2, NICHE-2 trial, patients with previously untreated, clinical stage 2 or 3, resectable dMMR colon cancer received a single dose of intravenous ipilimumab (1 mg per kg) on day 1, and two doses of intravenous nivolumab (3 mg per kg) on days 1 and 15, followed by surgery in one of the six participating hospitals in the Netherlands. Here, we report the independent primary efficacy endpoint of 3-year disease-free survival (time from surgery until disease recurrence or death from any cause) and the secondary endpoint of circulating tumour DNA (ctDNA) analysis. Overall survival (time from study enrolment until death from any cause) is reported as an exploratory post-hoc analysis. All endpoints were assessed in the per-protocol population (patients who met the eligibility criteria and received at least one cycle of therapy). The study is registered with ClinicalTrials.gov, NCT03026140, and has completed accrual.<h4>Findings</h4>From July 4, 2017, to July 18, 2022, 123 patients were screened, of whom 115 were treated and 111 were included in the per-protocol population. At the data cutoff (June 19, 2026), the postoperative median follow-up was 58·7 months (IQR 49·0-72·9). The 3-year disease-free survival was 100% (97·5% CI 100-100). After a median follow-up of 60·4 months (IQR 50·6-74·1), calculated from time of study enrolment, the 5-year overall survival was 100% (95% CI 100-100). In patients with available plasma samples, ctDNA was detected in 99 (92%) of 108 patients at baseline, and ctDNA clearance was observed in 44 (45%) of 98 patients after the first cycle in week 2 and in 80 (82%) of 97 patients after the second cycle before surgery. In patients with a pathological near-complete response and complete response, presurgery ctDNA clearance was observed in 19 (70%) of 27 patients and 60 (92%) of 65 patients, respectively. Assessment for minimal residual disease after surgery revealed that all patients were ctDNA negative.<h4>Interpretation</h4>In patients with locally advanced dMMR colon cancer, treatment with ipilimumab and nivolumab before surgery resulted in a 3-year disease-free survival and 5-year overall survival of 100%. These findings support neoadjuvant nivolumab plus ipilimumab as a potential standard of care for patients with locally advanced dMMR colon cancer.<h4>Funding</h4>Bristol Myers Squibb.

### [Endometrial ablation plus levonorgestrel-releasing intrauterine system versus endometrial ablation alone in women with heavy menstrual bleeding in The Netherlands (MIRA2): a multicentre, open-label, randomised, controlled, superiority trial.](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0140-6736(26)01536-9)

- Source: `lancet_abstracts` · published `2026-10-21` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42906565`

<h4>Background</h4>Endometrial ablation is an established and effective treatment for women with heavy menstrual bleeding, but many patients require further treatment, with approximately 10% ultimately undergoing a hysterectomy within 2 years. The insertion of a levonorgestrel-releasing intrauterine system (LNG-IUS) immediately after endometrial ablation might inactivate residual untreated endometrium or prevent its regeneration, thereby preventing recurrent symptoms necessitating a hysterectomy. The aim of the study was to assess whether LNG-IUS insertion immediately after endometrial ablation in women with heavy menstrual bleeding reduced the need for subsequent hysterectomy within 2 years compared with endometrial ablation alone.<h4>Methods</h4>This multicentre, open-label, randomised, controlled, superiority trial was conducted in 34 hospitals in the Netherlands. Women younger than 60 years with heavy menstrual bleeding who were eligible for endometrial ablation were included and randomly assigned (1:1) to combined treatment comprising endometrial ablation plus LNG-IUS or to standard care comprising endometrial ablation alone (stratified by dysmenorrhoea and age). Masking was not applied. Endometrial ablation was done using electrical radiofrequency ablation or by transcervical resection of the endometrium. In participants allocated to combined treatment, intrauterine insertion of an LNG-IUS containing a total of 52 mg of levonorgestrel (releasing 20 μg per day in the first year) was done immediately after the ablative procedure. The primary outcome was the incidence of hysterectomy within 2 years, analysed on a modified intention-to-treat basis in all participants with available data. Serious adverse events were systematically assessed at 6 weeks, 12 months, and 24 months after the intervention. This study was registered on the International Clinical Trials Registry Platform (NL-OMON54839) and is complete.<h4>Findings</h4>Between Nov 19, 2019, and April 5, 2023, 715 participants were randomly assigned to combined treatment (n=359) or endometrial ablation alone (n=356). The median age of all participants was 45 years (IQR 40-48). 42 (6%) participants had missing data for the primary outcome at 2 years of follow-up. A hysterectomy was done in 15 (4%) of 342 participants in the combined treatment group versus 34 (10%) of 331 participants in the endometrial ablation group, corresponding to a relative risk (RR) of 0·43 (95% CI 0·24 to 0·77; p=0·0050), with a risk difference of -0·06 (95% CI -0·10 to -0·02; p=0·0040). Serious adverse events were reported in 27 (8%) of 346 participants in the combined treatment group and 26 (8%) of 327 participants in the endometrial ablation alone group (RR 0·98, 95% CI 0·59-1·65; p=0·94), and there were no treatment-related deaths.<h4>Interpretation</h4>The combination of endometrial ablation and LNG-IUS significantly reduced the incidence of hysterectomy compared with endometrial ablation alone. The combined treatment appears to be a safe adjunctive strategy for heavy menstrual bleeding that does not increase the risk of complications.<h4>Funding</h4>The Netherlands Organization for Health Research and Development.

### [Obesity in Children Aged 2 to 12 Years: A Review.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.18806)

- Source: `jama_abstracts` · published `2026-10-21` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42905688`

<h4>Importance</h4>Obesity affects 15% of children aged 2 to 5 years and 22% of children aged 6 to 11 years in the US. Obesity in children is associated with dyslipidemia, hypertension, metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease, and obstructive sleep apnea. Compared with normal-weight peers, children with obesity have lower quality of life and are 5 times more likely to have obesity in adulthood.<h4>Observations</h4>Body mass index (BMI; calculated as weight in kilograms divided by height in meters squared) is used to identify children at risk for excess adiposity, and guidelines recommend that clinicians screen for obesity using BMI starting at age 2 years. Among children, obesity is defined using standardized age- and sex-specific growth charts, with obesity defined as BMI in the 95th or higher percentile and severe obesity as BMI of 120% or greater of the 95th percentile. Obesity treatment in children includes intensive health behavior and lifestyle treatment, consisting of at least 26 contact hours of face-to-face group or family-based activities that incorporate nutrition, physical activity, and behavior change education delivered to the child and caregivers over a 3- to 12-month period. Intensive health behavior and lifestyle treatment reduces BMI by 0.7 compared with children who do not participate in intensive health behavior and lifestyle treatment. The only obesity pharmacotherapy currently approved by the US Food and Drug Administration (FDA) for children younger than 12 years is setmelanotide, a melanocortin-4 receptor agonist, which is indicated for 3 forms of rare monogenic obesity (LEPR, POMC, and PCSK1 genetic variants), Bardet-Biedl syndrome, and acquired hypothalamic obesity. Liraglutide is currently only FDA approved for youth aged 12 years or older; however, a randomized clinical trial among children aged 6 to younger than 12 years demonstrated that, compared with placebo, liraglutide reduced BMI by 7.4%. Metabolic and bariatric surgery is rarely used in children younger than age 12 years and evidence supporting its use in children is limited. Early identification and treatment of obesity during childhood may reduce risk of type 2 diabetes, dyslipidemia, hypertension, and mortality in adulthood.<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>Obesity in children is associated with chronic disease, such as hypertension, and with poor quality of life. Evidence-based treatment for obesity in children includes intensive health behavior and lifestyle treatment, and pharmacotherapy may provide additional benefit, although short- and long-term safety and efficacy data are limited. Early identification and treatment of obesity during childhood may reduce risk of type 2 diabetes, dyslipidemia, hypertension, and mortality in adulthood.

The mean percent change in body weight with retatrutide (key secondary end point) was -12.5 to -22.2% in TRIUMPH-1-OSA and -12.1 to -18.5% in TRIUMPH-2-OSA, versus -4.8% and -5.6% with placebo (treatment-regimen estimand). The most common adverse events were gastrointestinal.<h4>Conclusions</h4>In adults with OSA and obesity or overweight with or without type 2 diabetes, retatrutide resulted in significantly greater decreases in the AHI than placebo. ### [Pregnancy Outcomes After Living Kidney Donation.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.21737)

<h4>Importance</h4>People who become pregnant after living kidney donation may be at increased risk of pregnancy-related adverse outcomes, although the evidence is unclear.<h4>Objective</h4>To determine whether pregnancies after living kidney donation are associated with a higher risk of adverse pregnancy outcomes than matched pregnancies among nondonors.<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>This matched cohort study used provincial administrative health care databases from Ontario, British Columbia, and Alberta, Canada, and included living female kidney donors aged 18 to 42 years who donated a kidney between July 1992 and August 2021 and subsequently had singleton pregnancies of at least 20 weeks' gestation. These pregnancies were matched 1:4 with singleton pregnancies of at least 20 weeks' gestation among female nondonors. Pregnancies in donors and nondonors were matched on delivery date, age at delivery, years between cohort entry and delivery, number of prior pregnancies, and income. Final follow-up occurred until March 2023. Outcome analysis occurred from August 2024 to September 2026.<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>The primary outcome was a composite of hospitalization with a diagnosis of gestational hypertension, preeclampsia, or eclampsia. Secondary outcomes included 12 maternal, fetal and neonatal adverse outcomes, including the individual components of the primary outcome, the composite of low birth weight or preterm birth, and small for gestational age at birth.<h4>Results</h4>The sample included 385 pregnancies in 252 living kidney donors (mean [SD] age, 27.8 [5.0] years) matched to 1540 pregnancies in 1535 nondonors (mean [SD] age, 27.0 [4.8] years). The primary composite outcome of gestational hypertension, preeclampsia, or eclampsia occurred in 46 donor pregnancies (11.9%) and 128 nondonor pregnancies (8.3%; risk ratio, 1.51; 95% CI, 1.10-2.08). Among the 12 secondary outcomes, the 95% CI for the risk ratio excluded 1.00 for the outcome of small for gestational age, which occurred in 53 donor pregnancies (13.8%) and 121 nondonor pregnancies (7.9%) (risk ratio, 1.86; 95% CI, 1.37-2.52).<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>In this study, pregnancy after living kidney donation was associated with a higher risk of the composite outcome of hospitalization with a diagnosis of gestational hypertension, preeclampsia, or eclampsia, when compared with matched pregnancies in nondonors. Most postdonation pregnancies were uncomplicated. These findings support counseling donor candidates who may become pregnant about this risk and informing the prenatal care of donors who become pregnant.

### [Rituximab vs Azathioprine for Eosinophilic Granulomatosis With Polyangiitis Maintenance Therapy: A Randomized Clinical Trial.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.15278)

<h4>Importance</h4>For patients with eosinophilic granulomatosis with polyangiitis (EGPA), guidelines recommend considering maintenance therapy after achieving remission. Data comparing maintenance therapeutic options are limited.<h4>Objective</h4>To compare the effects of rituximab and azathioprine maintenance therapies on remission duration for patients with EGPA who recently achieved remission of vasculitis after standard therapy, including prednisone tapering or withdrawal.<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>From March 7, 2018, to June 2, 2022, this double-blind, double-dummy, investigator-initiated, superiority, randomized clinical trial enrolled 98 participants with EGPA from 36 hospitals in France. Inclusion eligibility required a new EGPA diagnosis or relapsing EGPA within 30 to 360 days of achieving vasculitis remission (Birmingham Vasculitis Activity Score of 0 [range, 0-63, with higher scores indicating more severe disease activity]) after a standard induction regimen. Data were analyzed from April 1, 2025, to September 17, 2025. Additional analyses ended June 22, 2026.<h4>Interventions</h4>Participants were randomized to receive a predefined standardized tapering of oral prednisone and either 500 mg of intravenous rituximab every 6 months for 18 months and daily oral azathioprine placebo for 24 months (n = 49) or rituximab placebo every 6 months for 18 months and daily oral azathioprine for 24 months (n = 49).<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>The primary efficacy end point was the duration of total remission during the 28-month study period while taking 7.5 mg/d or less of prednisone. The 14 secondary end points included proportion of participants remaining in remission, rate of vasculitis relapse, and rate of asthma or rhinosinusitis exacerbations.<h4>Results</h4>All 98 randomized participants (mean age, 59.0 years [SD, 14.4]; 50 women [51%]) received at least 1 dose of the trial regimen and 92 (94%) completed the trial. The mean time in remission with prednisone at 7.5 mg/d or less was 91.1 weeks (95% CI, 80.9-101.2) vs 96.0 weeks (95% CI, 85.6-106.5) in the rituximab and azathioprine groups, respectively (P = .38). The proportion of participants who remained in remission during the 28-month study period with prednisone at 7.5 mg/d or less was 17 of 49 (34.7%) vs 11 of 49 (22.4%; relative risk, 1.51; 95% CI, 0.83-2.73; P = .18) in the rituximab and azathioprine groups, respectively. The proportion of participants with at least 1 vasculitis relapse was 7 (14.3%) vs 9 (18.4%) in the rituximab vs azathioprine groups, respectively, and for those with at least 1 clinically significant asthma exacerbation, rhinosinusitis exacerbation, or both, it was 16 (32.7%) vs 19 (38.8%). One participant in the azathioprine group died of SARS-CoV-2 infection before vaccine development. Rituximab recipients experienced a higher incidence of severe infections (23 [46.9%] vs 12 [24.5%], respectively), primarily due to severe SARS-CoV-2 infections.<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>For participants with newly diagnosed or relapsing EGPA, rituximab was not superior to azathioprine for improving the duration of total remission during 28 months while taking prednisone at 7.5 mg/d or less.<h4>Trial registration</h4>ClinicalTrials.gov Identifier: NCT03164473.

The key secondary end point was the annualized slope of the eGFR over 24 months. Other secondary end points included the change in eGFR from baseline to 24 months, a composite kidney end point of kidney-function decline or kidney failure, and pharmacodynamic and safety end points.<h4>Results</h4>A total of 510 patients underwent randomization - 259 to receive sibeprenlimab and 251 to receive placebo. The annualized eGFR slope over 24 months was 0.3 ml per minute per 1.73 m<sup>2</sup> of body-surface area per year (95% confidence interval [CI], -0.4 to 0.9) with sibeprenlimab and -4.2 ml per minute per 1.73 m<sup>2</sup> per year (95% CI, -4.9 to -3.6) with placebo (between-group difference, 4.5 ml per minute per 1.73 m<sup>2</sup> per year; 95% CI, 3.6 to 5.4; P<0.001). The least-squares mean treatment difference for the change in eGFR from baseline to 24 months was 9.2 ml per minute per 1.73 m<sup>2</sup> (95% CI, 7.1 to 11.4; P<0.001). A composite kidney end-point event occurred in 1 patient (0.4%) in the sibeprenlimab group and in 28 patients (11.2%) in the placebo group (adjusted difference, -10.8 percentage points; hazard ratio, 0.029; 95% CI, 0.004 to 0.214; P<0.001). Adverse events occurred in 235 patients (90.7%) in the sibeprenlimab group and 226 patients (90.0%) in the placebo group; serious adverse events occurred in 21 patients (8.1%) and 32 patients (12.7%), respectively; and serious infections in 5 patients (1.9%) and 10 patients (4.0%). No deaths were reported.<h4>Conclusions</h4>Among patients with IgA nephropathy, treatment with sibeprenlimab stabilized the eGFR slope over 24 months, a finding consistent with a reduced risk of kidney-function decline. (Funded by Otsuka Pharmaceutical Development and Commercialization; VISIONARY ClinicalTrials.gov number, NCT05248646.).

### [Exchange Pre-Eligibility Verification Proposed Rule (CMS 9873-P)](https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/exchange-pre-eligibility-verification-proposed-rule-cms-9873-p)

- Source: `cms_fact_sheets` · published `2026-10-23` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/exchange-pre-eligibility-verification-proposed-rule-cms-9873-p`

Exchange Pre-Eligibility Verification Proposed Rule (CMS 9873-P) Introduction Consistent with the President’s goal to prevent waste, fraud, and abuse, today the U.S. Department of Health and Human Services (HHS), through the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS), issued the Exchange Pre-Eligibility Verification Notice of Proposed Rulemaking. This rule would align Exchange eligibility and verification regulations with new requirements specified under the Working Families Tax Cuts (WFTC) legislation (P.L. 119-21). The WFTC legislation amended the Internal Revenue Code to establish new eligibility requirements for the premium tax credit (PTC). The Exchange Pre-Eligibility Verification proposed rule would establish new Exchange requirements that align advance payments of the premium tax credit (APTC) and cost-sharing reductions (CSRs) eligibility with PTC eligibility changes enacted under sec. 71303 of the WFTC legislation and proposed to be codified in regulations issued by the Department of the Treasury on October 15, 2026. Currently, when consumers apply for coverage through Exchanges, they provide certain information that Exchanges may attempt to verify electronically. If an Exchange cannot verify the information electronically, the consumer enters an inconsistency period during which they need to submit documentation to verify their information. Under existing policy established by the Affordable Care Act (ACA), Exchanges provide APTC and CSRs (collectively, financial assistance) during the inconsistency period to consumers who otherwise appear eligible. If they do not submit documentation to verify their eligibility before the end of the inconsistency period, APTC and CSRs are generally reduced or ended. The Exchange Pre-Eligibility Verification proposed rule would require Exchanges to verify the following criteria before providing financial assistance: household income and family size status as a citizen, U.S. national, or eligible noncitizen (including lawfully present) eligibility for or enrollment in other qualifying coverage place of residence However, under the proposed rule, consumers could enroll in Exchange coverage without financial assistance during the inconsistency period while verifications are pending. It would also establish a verification period beginning August 1 before Open Enrollment (OE) each year to ensure that consumers have ample opportunity to provide and confirm information impacting their eligibility before the following coverage year. This proposed rule also includes provisions on annual eligibility redeterminations and re-enrollment, eligibility redeterminations during the plan year, timeline requirements for states seeking to transition to a State-based Exchange, binder payments, a waiver of the proposed verification requirements for certain Special Enrollment Periods (SEPs), effective dates for eligibility determinations, annual OE notices, and plan selection requirements. The proposed regulations would ensure financial assistance is only provided when income can be verified, potentially reducing the risk that consumers would have to repay excess APTC at tax filing due to inaccurate income estimates. They also would reduce improper or fraudulent enrollments by requiring verification of certain eligibility criteria before providing financial assistance, improving program integrity. The provisions in the Exchange Pre-Eligibility Verification proposed rule would be effective starting with eligibility determinations made for plan year 2028. The rule would require Exchanges to make a pre-OE verification period available starting August 1 each year ahead of OE, during which consumers could choose to provide eligibility verification information earlier for next coverage year. The first pre-OE window would start August 1, 2027, for 2028 coverage. The Exchange Pre-Eligibility Verification proposed rule is available on the Federal Register at: https://www.federalregister.gov/public-inspection/2026-21891/patient-protection-and-affordable-care-act-exchange-pre-eligibility-verification . Stakeholders may submit public comments for a 30-day period following publication of the proposed rule. Strengthening Program Integrity Requiring Verification Before Receiving Financial Assistance CMS proposes new regulations for verification requirements for consumers requesting financial assistance for Exchange coverage. Exchanges would no longer be permitted to provide financial assistance during inconsistency periods when certain eligibility criteria cannot be verified electronically. Additionally, CMS proposes that Exchanges maintain or reduce the amount of financial assistance provided during such inconsistency periods after an enrollee voluntarily reports a change in circumstances that impacts eligibility for financial help. These new requirements would help ensure that only eligible consumers receive financial assistance. Requiring Annual Verification CMS proposes that currently enrolled consumers would only be automatically re-enrolled with financial assistance if they return to the Exchange to confirm certain eligibility criteria annually and have it re-verified by the Exchange. Consumers who don't actively return and have their information re-verified for financial help would be automatically re-enrolled without financial assistance. The new requirement would help ensure that only eligible consumers receive financial assistance through annual re-enrollment. Supporting Exchange Consumers Opportunity for Verification Before Open Enrollment Beginning August 1, 2027, CMS proposes that Exchanges provide consumers the option to submit certain eligibility information for the Exchange to verify before annual OE. CMS proposes requiring all Exchanges make this pre-OE verification period available to new and returning applicants to provide a minimum set of eligibility information and that all Exchanges start their annual pre-OE verification period on August 1 each year. By providing the early opportunity for verification, the proposed rule would help Exchange enrollees to have their eligibility fully verified before the upcoming coverage year, ensuring continuous coverage with financial assistance from year to year. Flexible Enrollment Options for Consumers CMS proposes requiring Exchanges to allow consumers to select a contingent plan during OE or a SEP such that the plan selection would become effective only if the consumer is determined eligible for financial assistance. This means consumers may choose a plan based on preliminary financial assistance estimates that would only start after they confirm eligibility for financial assistance. This process would enable consumers who elect not to enroll when they do not receive financial assistance during their inconsistency period to enroll once they are determined eligible for financial assistance, even if OE or their SEP, as applicable, has ended. Ensuring Timely Access to Financial Assistance CMS proposes new effective date rules for eligibility determinations and redeterminations made under the other proposed requirements in this rule. These proposals include new effective dates for financial assistance eligibility determinations and redeterminations after successful verification of eligibility. For consumers already enrolled in a health plan when their eligibility for financial assistance is verified, we propose providing financial assistance retroactively for the months during their inconsistency period when they were enrolled. For consumers who select a plan pending verification of eligibility for financial assistance, meaning the plan won’t start until after verification, we propose that their enrollment and financial assistance become effective and will start based on when their eligibility is verified. These proposed rules would provide clarity to consumers, Exchanges, and issuers; ensure timely access to coverage and financial assistance; and limit risks of adverse selection. Flexibility in Open Enrollment Notification Starting with plan year 2028, CMS proposes to permit Exchanges to provide the written annual OE notification to each enrollee before the pre-OE verification period, instead of in the month before OE starts. This would permit Exchanges to design the most effective outreach strategy to educate their consumers about the earlier verification period opportunity and the annual OE. Exception for Changes in Family Size CMS proposes waiving new verification requirements for consumers with certain changes in family size, as outlined in Treasury’s regulations, so that their financial assistance is based on their attested application information during inconsistency periods. This applies when a consumer or their household member qualifies for an SEP triggered by birth, adoption, marriage, or loss of a dependent through divorce, separation, or death (if allowed by the Exchange). Improving Exchange Rules Comment Solicitation on Enhancing Verification Procedures CMS seeks comment on how to strengthen Exchange requirements to verify family size and residency, including which data sources CMS should consider using or making available. CMS seeks comment on ways to increase program integrity while limiting burden on consumers and limiting operational costs and burdens for state and federal Exchanges. State Exchange Transitions CMS proposes that a state submits its application for HHS’ review and approval to become a State-based Exchange 15 months before a state begins operations as a State Exchange for the pre-OE verification period, instead of 15 months before OE. This would ensure the required pre-OE verification period is available to consumers when a State-based Exchange launches, removing the risk that a consumer first verifies on the Federally-facilitated Exchange (FFE) during the pre-OE verification period and then must reverify on the State-based Exchange. This requirement would ensure transitioning states align their transition timeline with the start of the pre-OE verification period, which would be the start of their operations as a State-based Exchange, instead of the start of OE. Binder Payment Alignment CMS proposes that the FFE and State-based Exchanges on the Federal Platform (SBE-FPs) must make prospective and retroactive enrollments under this proposed rule subject to the existing binder payment rules for prospective and retroactive enrollments, respectively. In addition, State-based Exchanges have the option to adopt the same changes. This would give issuers additional flexibility to set binder payment due dates for the proposed new enrollment scenarios. Effective Dates CMS proposes limited updates for current rules on effective dates for financial assistance eligibility redeterminations, when an existing enrollee reports a change to the Exchange during the coverage year. Specifically, in cases where the enrollee does not qualify for an SEP, CMS proposes no longer allowing Exchanges to delay effective dates for such eligibility redeterminations beyond the first of the month after the redetermination occurs. This change would align effective dates across existing and new scenarios and ensure timely eligibility updates. CMS News and Media Group Catherine Howden, Director Media Inquiries Form 202-690-6145

### [Interim Analysis of a Phase 3 Trial of Povetacicept in IgA Nephropathy.](https://doi.org/10.1056/nejmoa2608682)

- Source: `nejm_abstracts` · published `2026-10-23` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42912669`

<h4>Background</h4>B-cell activating factor (BAFF) and a proliferation-inducing ligand (APRIL) have distinct roles in IgA-producing B-cell populations and appear to be central to the pathogenesis of IgA nephropathy. Povetacicept, an enhanced, variant transmembrane activator and calcium-modulator and cyclophilin-ligand interactor (TACI)-Fc fragment fusion therapeutic, is engineered for dual inhibition of BAFF and APRIL.<h4>Methods</h4>In this ongoing, phase 3, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial, participants with biopsy-proven IgA nephropathy receiving stable background therapy were randomly assigned in a 2:1 ratio to receive povetacicept at a dose of 80 mg or placebo subcutaneously every 4 weeks. The primary end point of this prespecified interim analysis was the change in the 24-hour urinary protein-to-creatinine ratio at week 36. Secondary end points were the change at week 36 in galactose-deficient IgA1 levels and hematuria resolution at week 36 among participants with baseline hematuria.<h4>Results</h4>A total of 557 participants received povetacicept (372 participants) or placebo (185 participants). Efficacy analyses included the first 199 participants who had undergone randomization (131 in the povetacicept group and 68 in the placebo group). The 24-hour urinary protein-to-creatinine ratio decreased by 52.0% with povetacicept and by 4.3% with placebo by week 36 (adjusted difference, -49.8 percentage points; 95% confidence interval [CI], -58.6 to -39.2; P<0.001). Galactose-deficient IgA1 levels decreased by 77.4% with povetacicept and increased by 9.1% with placebo (adjusted difference, -79.3 percentage points; 95% CI, -82.2 to -75.9; P<0.001). Among the participants with baseline hematuria, resolution occurred in 85% with povetacicept and in 23% with placebo (adjusted difference, 62 percentage points; 95% CI, 45 to 78; P<0.001). The incidence of adverse events (75% with povetacicept and 78% with placebo) and serious adverse events (3% and 4%, respectively) were similar in the trial groups.<h4>Conclusions</h4>Povetacicept led to a significantly greater reduction in the 24-hour urinary protein-to-creatinine ratio than placebo by week 36 in participants with IgA nephropathy. (Funded by Vertex Pharmaceuticals; RAINIER AIS-D08 ClinicalTrials.gov number, NCT06564142.).

### [Mechanical Thrombectomy 24 to 72 Hours after Large-Vessel Ischemic Stroke.](https://doi.org/10.1056/nejmoa2609362)

- Source: `nejm_abstracts` · published `2026-10-23` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42929656`

<h4>Background</h4>Endovascular thrombectomy is an established treatment for acute ischemic stroke. Whether the benefit extends to patients with salvageable brain tissue in the infarct who present more than 24 hours after symptom onset or the time when they were last known to be well is uncertain.<h4>Methods</h4>We conducted an open-label, randomized trial with blinded outcome assessment at comprehensive stroke centers in China. Patients with acute ischemic stroke from large-vessel occlusion of the anterior circulation and imaging-identified salvageable brain tissue at 24 to 72 hours were assigned to receive endovascular therapy plus standard medical care (intervention group) or standard medical care alone (control group). The primary outcome was functional status, defined by the score on the modified Rankin scale (range, 0 to 6, with higher scores indicating greater disability and a score of 6 indicating death) at 90 days, with results assessed as the distribution of scores in each group. Secondary outcomes included disability, neurologic impairment, and safety.<h4>Results</h4>A total of 336 patients were included in the intention-to-treat population, with 168 assigned to each group. The median time from symptom onset or from the point when the patient was last known to be well to randomization was 38.5 hours. Endovascular therapy resulted in a more favorable distribution of modified Rankin scale scores at 90 days than standard medical care alone (common odds ratio for a better functional outcome, 1.74; 95% confidence interval, 1.19 to 2.55). Symptomatic intracranial hemorrhage occurred in 4.8% of the patients in the intervention group and in no patients in the control group, and the 90-day case fatality rate was 6.0% and 1.8%, respectively.<h4>Conclusions</h4>Among patients who presented 24 to 72 hours after an acute ischemic stroke from large-vessel occlusion of the anterior circulation and had imaging-identified salvageable tissue, endovascular therapy resulted in better functional outcomes than standard care but led to a higher risk of intracranial hemorrhage and death. (Funded by the National Health Commission Capacity Building and Continuing Education Center and others; LATE-MT ClinicalTrials.gov number, NCT05326932.).

### [Ravulizumab in IgA Nephropathy - Interim Analysis of a Phase 3 Trial.](https://doi.org/10.1056/nejmoa2607544)

- Source: `nejm_abstracts` · published `2026-10-23` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42912700`

<h4>Background</h4>In patients with IgA nephropathy, complement activation drives glomerular inflammation, which leads to hematuria, proteinuria, and nephron loss. Ravulizumab is a terminal complement inhibitor that prevents formation of C5a and C5b-9.<h4>Methods</h4>In this phase 3 trial, we enrolled adults with IgA nephropathy who had an estimated glomerular filtration rate of at least 30 ml per minute per 1.73 m<sup>2</sup> of body-surface area and proteinuria (24-hour urinary protein-to-creatinine ratio [with protein and creatinine both measured in grams] of ≥0.75 or total urinary protein excretion of ≥1.0 g per day), despite standard therapy. Patients were randomly assigned in a 1:1 ratio to receive an intravenous infusion of either ravulizumab or placebo every 8 weeks. In the prespecified interim analysis, the primary efficacy end point was the change from baseline in the 24-hour urinary protein-to-creatinine ratio at week 34. Safety was also assessed.<h4>Results</h4>In all, 485 patients underwent randomization, with 240 patients assigned to the ravulizumab group and 245 to the placebo group; 352 patients (176 in each group) were included in the interim analysis. In the ravulizumab group, the geometric mean 24-hour urinary protein-to-creatinine ratio at baseline and at week 34 was 1.56 and 0.83, respectively; in the placebo group, it was 1.60 and 1.48, respectively. At week 34, a significant reduction in the geometric mean 24-hour urinary protein-to-creatinine ratio was observed with ravulizumab (-46.6%) as compared with the reduction observed with placebo (-5.6%), corresponding to an adjusted geometric mean 24-hour urinary protein-to-creatinine ratio that was 43.4% (99% confidence interval, 30.1 to 54.2) lower with ravulizumab than with placebo (P<0.001). Adverse events occurred in 69.5% of the patients receiving ravulizumab and in 75.8% of those receiving placebo; serious adverse events occurred in 4.6% and 9.0%, respectively. No meningococcal infections or deaths occurred.<h4>Conclusions</h4>In adults with IgA nephropathy, ravulizumab resulted in a significantly greater reduction in the protein-to-creatinine ratio than placebo at week 34. (Funded by Alexion, AstraZeneca Rare Disease Unit; I CAN ClinicalTrials.gov number, NCT06291376; EU Clinical Trials number, 2023-507851-31-00.).

### [Remibrutinib versus teriflunomide in relapsing multiple sclerosis (REMODEL-1 and REMODEL-2): two randomised, phase 3 trials.](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0140-6736(26)02058-1)

- Source: `lancet_abstracts` · published `2026-10-23` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42915009`

<h4>Background</h4>Remibrutinib is a highly selective, potent, covalent, CNS-penetrant, oral BTK inhibitor. We assessed the efficacy and safety of remibrutinib in adult patients with relapsing multiple sclerosis.<h4>Methods</h4>REMODEL-1 and REMODEL-2 are two identical, randomised, double-blind, double-dummy, active comparator-controlled, parallel-group, event-driven, phase 3 trials conducted at 407 centres in 46 countries. Patients aged 18-55 years with relapsing multiple sclerosis, an Expanded Disability Status Scale score of 0-5·5, and evidence of recent disease activity were randomly assigned 1:1 to oral remibrutinib 100 mg twice daily or teriflunomide 14 mg once daily via interactive response technology. Randomisation was stratified by geographical region, MRI cohort participation, and multiple sclerosis subtype. Patients, site investigators, and trial team were masked to treatment allocation. The primary endpoint was annualised relapse rate (ARR), defined as the number of confirmed multiple sclerosis relapses per patient-year with up to 30 months of follow-up, and assessed in all randomly assigned patients. These trials are registered with ClinicalTrials.gov (NCT05147220 and NCT05156281) and are ongoing, not recruiting.<h4>Findings</h4>Between Dec 13, 2021, and Dec 30, 2025, 2632 patients were screened and 2007 patients were randomly assigned. REMODEL-1 included 1000 patients (remibrutinib 502, teriflunomide 498; mean age 38·2 years, 666 [66·6%] female, 334 [33·4%] male) and REMODEL-2 included 1007 patients (remibrutinib 504, teriflunomide 503; mean age 39·0 years, 675 [67·0%] female, 332 [33·0%] male). ARR was significantly lower with remibrutinib than with teriflunomide: 0·073 versus 0·112 (rate ratio [RR] 0·648 [95% CI 0·453-0·927]; p=0·018) in REMODEL-1 and 0·080 versus 0·116 (RR 0·688 [0·487-0·972]; p=0·034) in REMODEL-2. The most common adverse events were upper respiratory tract infection (132 [13·1%] of 1006 with remibrutinib vs 137 [13·7%] of 1001 with teriflunomide), nasopharyngitis (92 [9·1%] vs 123 [12·3%]), diarrhoea (84 [8·3%] vs 109 [10·9%]), and alopecia (58 [5·8%] vs 135 [13·5%]). Adverse events leading to treatment discontinuation were less frequent with remibrutinib (31 [3·1%]) versus teriflunomide (58 [5·8%]). The safety profile of remibrutinib was consistent with that in other indications, including no liver safety concerns.<h4>Interpretation</h4>Remibrutinib was superior to teriflunomide in patients with relapsing multiple sclerosis, while maintaining a favourable safety profile. These findings support remibrutinib as a potential new oral treatment option for relapsing multiple sclerosis.<h4>Funding</h4>Novartis.

### [Trastuzumab Rezetecan in ERBB2-Low Advanced Breast Cancer: The HORIZON-Breast06 Randomized Clinical Trial.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.21372)

- Source: `jama_abstracts` · published `2026-10-23` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`

<h4>Importance</h4>Trastuzumab rezetecan (SHR-A1811) has demonstrated encouraging antitumor activity in a global phase 1 trial involving heavily pretreated patients with erythroblastic oncogene B (ERBB2)-low metastatic breast cancer.<h4>Objective</h4>To compare trastuzumab rezetecan vs physician's choice of chemotherapy in patients with ERBB2-low metastatic breast cancer.<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>This open-label, randomized, multicenter phase 3 trial was conducted at 73 hospitals across China. Eligible patients were females aged 18 to 75 years with centrally confirmed ERBB2-low (immunohistochemistry [IHC] 1+ or IHC 2+/in-situ hybridization [ISH]-negative) unresectable locally recurrent or metastatic breast cancer who had received 1 or fewer prior lines of chemotherapy for metastatic disease, with prior endocrine therapy required for hormone receptor-positive disease. Enrollment began on June 30, 2023, and ended on August 20, 2025. The last day of follow-up for the final analysis for progression-free survival in the current study was March 20, 2026.<h4>Interventions</h4>Patients were randomly assigned (1:1) to receive trastuzumab rezetecan (6.4 mg/kg intravenously every 3 weeks; n = 277) or physician's choice of chemotherapy (n = 274).<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>The primary end point was progression-free survival assessed by blinded independent central review in chemotherapy-naive patients and the overall population.<h4>Results</h4>Between June 30, 2023, and August 20, 2025, a total of 551 patients were randomized, with a mean (SD) age of 54.9 (9.9) years. At the data cutoff (March 20, 2026), trastuzumab rezetecan significantly prolonged progression-free survival compared with chemotherapy in the chemotherapy-naive patients (median, 16.6 [95% CI, 14.0-19.4] months vs 6.8 [95% CI, 5.7-7.0] months; hazard ratio, 0.25 [95% CI, 0.19-0.33]; P < .001) and the overall population (median, 16.4 [95% CI, 14.0-16.8] months vs 6.8 [95% CI, 5.7-6.9] months; hazard ratio, 0.27 [95% CI, 0.21-0.34]; P < .001). The benefit was consistent in hormone receptor-negative disease (median progression-free survival: 16.5 vs 6.8 months; hazard ratio, 0.34, [95% CI, 0.22-0.54]) and hormone receptor-positive disease (median progression-free survival: 16.4 vs 6.7 months; hazard ratio, 0.24 [95% CI, 0.18-0.33]). Grade 3 or greater treatment-related adverse events occurred in 84.1% of patients (232/276) with trastuzumab rezetecan and 67.9% (184/271) with chemotherapy, most commonly decreased neutrophil count (180 [65.2%] vs 147 [54.2%]).<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>Trastuzumab rezetecan provided significant progression-free survival benefit over physician's choice of chemotherapy in patients with ERBB2-low advanced breast cancer, with benefit observed across all key subgroups. It exhibited the potential as a new treatment option with a distinct safety profile in this setting.<h4>Trial registration</h4>ClinicalTrials.gov Identifier: NCT05814354.

### [Trial of Endovascular Therapy for Mild Stroke with Large-Vessel Occlusion.](https://doi.org/10.1056/nejmoa2609149)

- Source: `nejm_abstracts` · published `2026-10-23` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42929671`

<h4>Background</h4>Whether endovascular therapy benefits patients with mild acute ischemic stroke from large-vessel occlusion involving the anterior circulation is uncertain.<h4>Methods</h4>We conducted a multicenter, open-label, randomized trial in China involving patients with acute ischemic stroke of mild clinical severity (defined by a score of <6 on the National Institutes of Health Stroke Scale) that had resulted from large-vessel occlusion involving the anterior circulation. Patients also had neuroimaging features of a small infarct core and a large perfusion-mismatch volume, representing substantial at-risk tissue, within 24 hours after symptom onset or after they were last known to be well. The patients were randomly assigned to undergo endovascular therapy and receive medical management or to receive medical management alone. The primary outcome was an excellent functional outcome (defined by a modified Rankin scale score of 0 [no symptoms] or 1 [no clinically significant disability]) at 90 days.<h4>Results</h4>A total of 304 patients underwent randomization, of whom 300 were included in the modified intention-to-treat population: 144 in the endovascular-therapy group and 156 in the medical-management group. At 90 days, a modified Rankin scale score of 0 or 1 was reported in 69.4% of the patients in the endovascular-therapy group and in 50.0% of those in the medical-management group (odds ratio, 2.32; 95% confidence interval, 1.41 to 3.81; P<0.001). Symptomatic intracranial hemorrhage within 48 hours occurred in 3 patients in each group. Serious procedure-related complications occurred in 19 patients (13.2%) in the endovascular-therapy group. Among all the participants, one death occurred (in the endovascular-therapy group).<h4>Conclusions</h4>In this trial from China involving patients with mild acute ischemic stroke caused by large-vessel occlusion and substantial at-risk tissue on perfusion imaging, endovascular therapy performed within 24 hours after symptom onset plus medical management resulted in better 90-day functional outcomes than medical management alone, without an increase in the risk of symptomatic intracranial hemorrhage but with a high incidence of serious procedural complications. (Funded by Fujian Medical University Union Hospital and others; MILD-MT ClinicalTrials.gov number, NCT06179017.).

### [14-day versus 28-day trifluridine-tipiracil plus bevacizumab for refractory metastatic colorectal cancer (PRABITAS): a pragmatic, multicentre, open-label, randomised, phase 3, non-inferiority trial in Japan.](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0140-6736(26)01782-4)
