### [Generating Population-Linked Case Vignettes From Nationwide Triage Data: Framework Development and Validation Study](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e104801)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-11-03` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e104801`

Background: Digital decision-support tools such as triage systems and symptom checkers support millions of health-related decisions each year. Their quality and safety are commonly evaluated using textual patient cases, known as case vignettes. However, expert-written vignette sets cover only a limited spectrum of real-world patient presentations and lack population weights, which would allow extrapolating evaluation results to the underlying patient population. Objective: This study aimed to develop a data-driven framework for automatically generating a human-manageable set of case vignettes from nationwide triage data that capture broad presentation diversity and link each vignette to a quantitative weight reflecting the number of underlying patient assessments. Methods: From 3.2 million triage assessments conducted over 1 year using structured triage software in the German medical on-call service (telephone triage and online self-triage) and at joint contact points of the outpatient emergency care service and hospital emergency departments, we randomly sampled 50,000 cases. Triage questionnaires were converted into semantic embeddings using a German Sentence Transformer model and grouped by agglomerative clustering. For clusters containing sufficient assessments, we generated one representative assessment using a 2-phase simulated annealing optimization that minimized the distance from the cluster centroid while maximizing the number of answered triage questions. Each representative assessment was assigned the size of its source cluster as its sample-based weight. A similarity-based sensitivity analysis assessed whether these weights were preserved in the full 1-year population. Finally, question-answer pairs of the representative assessments were converted into textual case vignettes using controlled prompting of a large language model. Results: The cluster analysis yielded 514 included clusters covering 96.8% of the sampled 50,000 assessments. The generated representatives showed strong agreement with the majority treatment urgency recommendation of their source cluster (Spearman ρ=0.78; <.001) and contained, on average, 4.3 more answered triage questions than the original assessments within their clusters. When weighted by cluster size, the representatives approximated the sample distributions of treatment urgency, demographics, and symptoms, although some systematic deviations remained, most notably an overrepresentation of female cases (13.5%), patients aged 14‐49 (8.0%) years, and the urgency category “As soon as possible” (6.6%). Of 121 recorded symptoms, 101 (83.5%) were covered by the representatives; the rest each occurred in <0.5% of the sample. Cluster-based vignette weights were strongly correlated with similarity-based population weights (Spearman ρ=0.77; <.001), and 90.1% of assessments in the full 1-year population matched at least one vignette. Conclusions: We present a data-driven framework for deriving a manageable set of population-weighted case vignettes from nationwide triage data. The resulting vignettes captured broad presentation diversity, approximated key sample characteristics, and provided an explicit quantitative link to the number of underlying assessments. After medical expert review and refinement, the vignettes may support more population-aware evaluation and quality assurance of digital decision-support tools.

### [Implementation Evaluation of an AI Scribing Tool in the Ambulatory Setting: Single-Site Qualitative Implementation Evaluation Study](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e96245)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-11-03` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e96245`

Background: AI scribing tools to assist health care providers by generating clinical notes are rapidly spreading across health care settings. Although these tools may reduce documentation burden, successful implementation depends on the contextual factors influencing their integration into clinical practice. Implementation evaluations are needed to inform practice integration, guide future adoption, and enhance effective use of this novel technology. Objective: This study aims to explore contextual factors influencing the implementation of an AI scribe for physicians in the ambulatory setting. Methods: This study was a single-site qualitative implementation evaluation at a metropolitan university health system where the AI scribe was available across primary and specialty care clinics. The Practical, Robust Implementation and Sustainability Model (PRISM) and the Sociotechnical Systems framework guided the evaluation. Using maximum variation sampling, we recruited attending physicians with varied levels of AI scribe use. Semistructured interviews were conducted with attending physicians working in the ambulatory setting. Data collection and analysis occurred iteratively until thematic saturation was achieved. Interviews were analyzed using thematic analysis with a constant comparative method, incorporating both deductive and inductive coding. Resulting subthemes were mapped onto corresponding domains of the PRISM and Sociotechnical Systems frameworks. Results: A total of 25 physicians were interviewed, including 7 who had never used the AI scribe, 9 who used it minimally, and 9 who used it frequently. Participants represented a broad range of specialties. A total of 5 themes and 15 subthemes emerged. First, workflow integration influenced adoption. Limitations in customization were a barrier to adoption, while flexibility among users was critical to fit. Second, ease of tool usability facilitated initial adoption, whereby users tried the tool without paying close attention to training materials. However, this lack of training engagement hindered widespread sustained use due to limited knowledge of advanced functionalities. Third, attending physicians balanced trust and confidence in AI-generated documentation with mistrust and professional accountability. Many described a “trust but verify” approach. Also, some concerns about others’ inadequate review of AI-generated content weakened physician-physician trust. Fourth, attending physicians viewed the ubiquitous presence of AI scribing as inevitable, driven by organizational priorities related to productivity, cost, and burnout. Interviewees disagreed about the appropriate use of AI scribes by trainees. Finally, identical mechanisms were viewed as both benefits and drawbacks. Increased efficiency from AI documentation was perceived by some as reducing burden and cognitive load, but perceived by others as potentially intensifying workload expectations. Similarly, some thought AI scribes facilitated more patient-centered communication, while others thought it had the opposite impact. Conclusions: Implementation of AI scribes in ambulatory care is shaped by complex sociotechnical and contextual factors. Adoption is influenced by workflow alignment, trust, and preexisting beliefs about the tool’s benefits and drawbacks, illustrating how clinical context affects AI scribe implementation.

### [Informatics for the Planet: Integrating Species, Ecosystems, and Geophysical Data to Manage the Health of Earth Systems](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e113778)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-11-03` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e113778`

Biomedical and health informatics (BMHI) has traditionally focused on human-centric clinical data to support individual patient care and health systems. However, contemporary health challenges driven by climate change, biodiversity loss, and ecological degradation demand an expanded paradigm that addresses interdependencies among human, animal, and environmental health. This editorial introduces a new JMIR Medical Informatics section on “Planetary Health Informatics” (PHI), an essential evolution extending informatics praxis beyond human healthcare toward Earth’s interconnected systems. JMIR Medical Informatics is launching a call for papers for a new section dedicated to advancing PHI theory, methods, and applications for a sustainable future shaped by human impact on Earth. We invite authors to submit work that explores how PHI integrates species, ecosystem, and geophysical data to address critical global challenges, including metagenomic disease surveillance, climate-related hazard forecasting, environmental pollution monitoring, and health infrastructure decarbonization. By synthesizing broader ecological frameworks, we present a roadmap for transforming fragmented data streams into planetary-scale intelligence.

### [Long-term outcomes after percutaneous coronary intervention with drug-eluting stents versus coronary artery bypass grafting for left main coronary artery disease: a prospective analysis of the multicentre IRIS-MAIN registry.](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0140-6736(26)02104-5)

- Source: `lancet_abstracts` · published `2026-11-03` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `new` · identity: `pmid:42976899`

<h4>Background</h4>Percutaneous coronary intervention (PCI) with drug-eluting stents has been widely used to treat patients with left main coronary artery disease; however, long-term comparative outcomes versus coronary artery bypass grafting (CABG) in real-world populations remain poorly characterised. We aimed to compare long-term all-cause mortality after PCI or CABG in Asian patients with unprotected left main coronary artery disease.<h4>Methods</h4>We analysed prospectively collected data from the multicentre IRIS-MAIN registry, conducted at 46 hospitals in South Korea, India, Japan, Indonesia, and Malaysia. The registry enrolled adults aged 18 years or older with unprotected left main coronary artery disease (defined as a stenosis diameter of ≥50% as visually estimated on angiography), who had been treated with PCI, CABG, or medical therapy alone. The primary outcome was all-cause mortality. We used a target trial emulation framework to compare the long-term risk of all-cause mortality between PCI with drug-eluting stents and isolated CABG. We used propensity-score overlap weighting to adjust for measured baseline covariates. The IRIS-MAIN Registry study is registered with ClinicalTrials.gov (NCT01341327).<h4>Findings</h4>Between Jan 1, 2003, and Dec 31, 2024, 6900 patients were enrolled in the IRIS-MAIN registry, of whom 5379 (78·0%) underwent coronary revascularisation through either PCI with drug-eluting stents (3836 [71·3%]) or CABG (1543 [28·7%]) and were included in the analysis. The median duration of follow-up for all patients was 10·0 years (IQR 5·0-16·7). In the overlap-weighted population, no statistically detectable difference in 10-year weighted Kaplan-Meier rates of all-cause mortality was observed between PCI and CABG (24·0% vs 22·9%; overlap-weighted hazard ratio 1·00 [95% CI 0·87-1·14]; p=0·99). The confidence interval does not exclude a mortality benefit or harm with either strategy.<h4>Interpretation</h4>In this non-randomised registry analysis of patients with left main coronary artery disease, there was no statistically detectable difference in all-cause mortality between PCI and CABG. For patients considered suitable for either strategy, multidisciplinary Heart Team discussion might help patients and clinicians weigh potential trade-offs in the choice between PCI and CABG. These findings are observational and cannot establish causal treatment effects or equivalence in survival.<h4>Funding</h4>The CardioVascular Research Foundation (South Korea).

Measles is reemerging, with cases and outbreaks increasing across the United States and other countries where measles had previously been eliminated. Most U.S. physicians have no experience with treating measles. However, the increasing likelihood of encountering not only children but also adults with measles requires a clinical review of the epidemiology, prevention, diagnosis, and management of this previously uncommon infection for general internal medicine physicians and hospitalists who care for adults in settings with measles reemergence. Prompt clinical suspicion, infection control measures, and notification of public health departments support timely diagnosis and disease control.

### [Non-invasive oral magnetoelectric vagus nerve neuromodulation for Parkinson’s disease therapy](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41551-026-01829-x)

- Source: `nature_biomedical_engineering` · published `2026-11-03` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41551-026-01829-x`

Vagus nerve stimulation has shown promising neuroprotective and anti-inflammatory effects in progressive neurodegenerative disorders such as Parkinson’s disease. However, its invasive nature introduces procedural and device-related risks. Here we introduce a magnetoelectric particulate system for oral delivery and non-invasive vagus nerve stimulation through the gut. The system comprises a magneto-responsive nickel ferrite core and a piezoelectric barium titanate shell, conjugated with capsaicin to specifically target TRPV1 receptors, ensuring effective adherence to the stomach lining after oral administration. Upon magnetic field stimulation, the particles generate electrical pulses that activate vagal afferent fibres, engaging the nucleus tractus solitarius in the brainstem via the gut−brain axis. This activation enhances norepinephrine production in the locus coeruleus and dopamine synthesis in the substantia nigra. Vagal efferent activation triggers the cholinergic anti-inflammatory pathway, mitigating both neuroinflammation and systemic inflammation. This non-invasive approach demonstrates potential for alleviating motor symptoms and non-motor anxiety in Parkinson’s disease, offering valuable insights into its mechanisms and clinical applications. Orally delivered magnetoelectric particles activate vagal afferent fibres in the gut−brain axis and influence neurodegeneration and neuroinflammation in mouse models of Parkinson’s disease.

### [Timing of Long COVID Improvement in Prospectively Followed RECOVER-Adult Participants.](https://doi.org/10.7326/annals-25-05275)

- Source: `annals_internal_abstracts` · published `2026-11-03` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42970801`

<h4>Background</h4>Long COVID (LC) is widespread; however, limited information has been reported on the likelihood that symptoms remit over time.<h4>Objective</h4>To evaluate the likelihood of improvement among persons with likely LC.<h4>Design</h4>Prospective, multisite cohort study (RECOVER-Adult [Researching COVID to Enhance Recovery]).<h4>Setting</h4>83 U.S.-based sites across 33 states; Washington, DC; and Puerto Rico.<h4>Participants</h4>1019 persons in RECOVER-Adult with likely LC (LC Research Index [LCRI] ≥11) 12 months after first SARS-CoV-2 infection.<h4>Measurements</h4>The primary outcome was time until 2 consecutive study visits with LCRI less than 11. Secondary outcomes were time until LCRI measure reduced 50% or more and quality of life was good or better, and LCRI decreased to 0. Unadjusted estimates of cumulative incidence and adjusted marginal cumulative incidence by age, sex, SARS-CoV-2 wave, and LC subtype were reported across all follow-up time points.<h4>Results</h4>An estimated 36.2% (95% CI, 33.0% to 39.5%) met the primary outcome by 24 months after infection, and 50.1% (CI, 46.1% to 54.0%) met the primary outcome by 36 months. An estimated 56.4% (CI, 52.2% to 60.4%) and 26.9% (CI, 23.3% to 30.5%), respectively, met the 2 secondary outcomes by 36 months. Among persons who still reported likely LC at 18 months and 24 months after first infection, relative timing of subsequent symptom improvement was similar.<h4>Limitation</h4>Findings in this observational cohort are interpretable in a population with prevalent likely LC and do not address causal relationships between individual characteristics and symptom improvement.<h4>Conclusion</h4>Among persons with likely LC 12 months after initial SARS-CoV-2 infection, one half experience meaningful reductions in symptoms by 36 months, whereas a quarter experience complete remission of symptoms. These findings underscore both the potential for natural recovery and the urgent need for placebo-controlled therapeutic intervention trials.<h4>Primary funding source</h4>National Institutes of Health.

### [AI-Enabled Digital Health Tool Implementation in Clinical Practice Settings: Umbrella Review](https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e101509/)

- Source: `jmir_ai` · published `2026-11-04` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e101509/`

Background: AI has the potential to improve clinical decision-making, efficiency, and patient outcomes, yet adoption in routine clinical practice has been slower than anticipated. While prior studies have identified factors that influence AI implementation, a comprehensive synthesis grounded in implementation science frameworks is lacking. Objective: This study aimed to qualitatively and comprehensively describe barriers and facilitators to AI implementation in clinical care settings using an implementation science determinants framework, the Consolidated Framework for Implementation Research (CFIR), and to identify AI-specific determinants not fully captured by CFIR. Methods: We conducted an umbrella review of reviews examining determinants of AI implementation in clinical practice. In accordance with the PRIOR (Preferred Reporting Items for Overviews of Reviews) reporting guidance, we systematically searched PubMed, Scopus, and CINAHL for English-language reviews published between January 2013 and July 2025. Reviews were included if they addressed barriers to and facilitators of implementing AI-enabled digital health tools in clinical care settings. Determinants were deductively mapped to the updated CFIR and inductively analyzed to identify emergent AI-specific constructs. Review quality was assessed using the Joanna Briggs Institute critical appraisal checklist, and overlap among primary studies was evaluated using the GROOVE (Graphical Representation of Overlap for Overviews) tool. Results: Twenty-two reviews published between 2021 and 2025 were included, encompassing studies conducted from 1990 to 2025. Reviews varied in design, including systematic, scoping, rapid, narrative, and literature reviews. Overall review quality was mixed, with 9 (41%) reviews rated at 100% quality and 4 (18%) at 50% or lower. Primary study overlap was slight, with a corrected covered area of 0.4%. Determinants of AI implementation were identified across all CFIR domains. Prominent barriers at the innovation level included limited evidence of clinical impact, poor interpretability, complexity, lack of adaptability, and insufficiently representative data. Facilitators included perceived relative advantage, co-design with clinicians, and evidence of local clinical benefit. At the implementer level, low AI literacy, limited confidence, time constraints, and concerns about professional displacement were common barriers, while training and technical support facilitated adoption. Inner setting barriers included inadequate infrastructure, limited resources, poor workflow compatibility, and weak governance structures. Outer setting barriers included unclear policies, laws, and liability frameworks, whereas partnerships and cross-sector collaboration were frequent facilitators. Inductively identified AI-specific determinants included transparency, trust, accountability, model bias, data governance, model maintenance, robustness, generalizability, existential threat, and concern about loss of the human element in care. Conclusions: AI implementation in clinical settings is shaped by complex, multilevel, and AI-specific determinants that extend beyond traditional digital health considerations. Implementation science frameworks can be adapted to incorporate these constructs. Tailored, theory-informed strategies addressing technical, organizational, psychological, and regulatory factors are essential to support safe, equitable, and sustainable integration of AI into clinical care. Trial Registration: PROSPERO CRD42024501642; https://www.crd.york.ac.uk/PROSPERO/view/CRD42024501642 <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/3324ef57cb4c56e8d377f603dffa0fd1" />

### [Calendar Year (CY) 2027 Home Health Prospective Payment System Final Rule - CMS-1844-F](https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/calendar-year-cy-2027-home-health-prospective-payment-system-final-rule-cms-1844-f)

- Source: `cms_fact_sheets` · published `2026-11-04` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/calendar-year-cy-2027-home-health-prospective-payment-system-final-rule-cms-1844-f`

Calendar Year (CY) 2027 Home Health Prospective Payment System Final Rule Fact Sheet (CMS-1844-P) On November 4, 2026, the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS) issued a final rule that announces policy changes under the Home Health (HH) Prospective Payment System (PPS), consistent with the legal requirements to update Medicare payment policies for home health agencies (HHAs) annually. This fact sheet discusses the final rule’s major provisions. Medicare Provider Enrollment In its ongoing efforts to crush fraud, waste, and abuse, CMS is finalizing several provider enrollment provisions. Most of the provisions will apply to all provider types enrolled in the Medicare program. T hese changes will help reduce improper Medicare payments and protect beneficiaries. The provisions include, but are not limited to, the following: Retroactive Revocations — Under current regulations, certain Medicare enrollment revocations become effective prospectively — specifically, 30 days after the date that CMS or the CMS contractor mails notice of the revocation to the affected provider or supplier (hereafter “provider”). However, other revocations take effect retroactively to the date the provider’s noncompliance began. CMS is finalizing a policy to make all revocation grounds retroactive. This action will help CMS recover monies paid to non-compliant providers and help ensure taxpayer money is paid only to legitimate, compliant providers. Adding or Expanding Bases for Revocation or Denial — CMS is adding several new grounds for revocation or denial of enrollment and expanding some of its existing grounds. Among these provisions are the following: Change in Majority Ownership — Hospices; HHAs; and suppliers of durable medical equipment, prosthetics, orthotics, and supplies (DMEPOS) must reenroll in Medicare as a new provider and undergo a survey/accreditation if they experience certain changes in majority ownership. CMS will have the authority to deny or revoke enrollment if the provider does not comply with this requirement. Program or License Suspension/Termination — CMS currently may deny or revoke enrollment if a provider: (1) has a suspended/revoked license in another state; or (2) is suspended/revoked from Medicaid or another federal healthcare program. We are expanding this to include similar suspensions/revocations involving the provider’s owners or managing employees/organizations. The final revocation/denial authorities address situations noted by CMS as involving provider noncompliance and actual and potential fraud, waste, and abuse. They also would allow the agency to target improper activity by those who own or operate Medicare providers. CY 2027 Final Payment and Policy Updates for Home Health Agencies The provisions in this final rule will help improve patient care and protect the Medicare program’s sustainability for future generations. This rule finalizes routine, statutorily required updates to the home health payment rates for CY 2027. The CY 2027 updated rates include the final CY 2027 home health payment update of 2.1% ($365 million increase) and an estimated 0.3% increase that reflects the updated fixed dollar loss (FDL) amount ($50 million increase). CMS estimates that Medicare payments to HHAs in CY 2027 would increase in the aggregate by 2.4%, or $415 million, compared to CY 2026, based on the finalized policies. In this final rule, CMS is also continuing its implementation of a 30-day unit of payment and of the Patient-Driven Groupings Model (PDGM) case-mix adjustment methodology within the HH PPS per the Bipartisan Budget Act of 2018. Specifically, it includes an analysis of the difference between assumed versus actual behavior change on estimated aggregate expenditures for home health payments linked to these changes. This rule also discusses the permanent adjustments to HH PPS payment rates applied in previous years and finalizes a temporary adjustment to the CY 2027 home health base payment rate of -3.0%. CMS did not propose an additional permanent adjustment to the CY 2027 30-day base payment rate; however we continue to annually analyze the data through CY 2026 claims, as required by section 1895(b)(3)(D)(ii) of the Social Security Act (the Act), to determine any additional permanent adjustments to account for the impact of implementing the PDGM. In addition, CMS finalizes the recalibrated PDGM case-mix weights; updates the FDL for outlier payments; and updates the low utilization payment adjustment (LUPA) thresholds, functional impairment levels, and comorbidity adjustment subgroups for CY 2027. This final rule also responds to comments received on the provision of palliative care services under the Medicare home health benefit and the request for information (RFI) regarding the construction of a home health specific wage index included in the proposed rule. PDGM and Behavior Assumptions On January 1, 2020, CMS implemented the home health PDGM and a 30-day unit of payment (previously 60 days), as required by section 1895(b) of the Social Security Act, as amended by the Bipartisan Budget Act of 2018. The PDGM better aligns payments with patient care needs, especially for clinically complex individuals. The law required CMS to make assumptions about behavior changes that could occur because of the 30-day unit of payment and the PDGM. CMS finalized three behavior assumptions in the CY 2019 HH PPS final rule: clinical group coding, comorbidity coding, and LUPA threshold. The law also requires CMS to annually determine the impact of differences between assumed behavior changes and actual behavior changes on estimated aggregate expenditures, beginning with 2020 and ending with 2026, and to make temporary and permanent increases or decreases, as needed, to the 30-day payment amount to offset such increases or decreases. In the CY 2023 HH PPS final rule (87 FR 66790), CMS finalized a methodology for analyzing the differences between assumed and actual behavior changes on estimated aggregate expenditures and calculated levels of actual and estimated aggregate expenditures. Based on analyses of CYs 2020 and 2021 claims data, CMS determined a permanent adjustment was needed and finalized implementing half (-3.925%) of the permanent adjustment estimated at the time (-7.85%). For CYs 2024 (88 FR 77676) and 2025 (88 FR 88373), using CY 2022 and 2023 claims, respectively, CMS similarly implemented half of the permanent adjustments estimated at those times (-2.890% out of -5.779% total for 2024 and -1.975% out of -3.95% total for 2025). In the CY 2026 HH PPS final rule (90 FR 55342), CMS finalized a policy applying the remaining permanent adjustment for claims years 2020-2022, as our analysis indicated that behavior changes after 2022 could be related to other policy factors beyond the PDGM implementation. As such, we exercised the authority expressly delegated under the statute to apply permanent adjustments “at a time and in a manner appropriate” to the remaining permanent adjustment of -1.023% to account for behavior changes associated with the PDGM implementation in CYs 2020 through 2022. We also exercised our authority to begin recouping retrospective overpayments (i.e., the temporary adjustment) for CYs 2020 through 2024 and finalized a temporary adjustment with a 3.0% reduction in CY 2026. In the CY 2027 HH PPS final rule, similar to the policies finalized in the CY 2026 HH PPS final rule (90 FR 55364 through 55365), we state that behavior changes reflected in preliminary CY 2025 claims for this CY 2027 final rule may not be directly attributable to the PDGM. Other confounding factors began in CY 2023 (i.e., continued recalibration of case-mix weights, a change to the OASIS-E, and previous reductions to the home health payment rate). While we did not propose an additional permanent adjustment to the CY 2027 30-day base payment rate, this final rule continues to satisfy the statutory requirements at section 1895(b)(3)(D)(ii) of the Act as it includes our annual analysis of the data, as required by law, to determine if any additional permanent adjustments would be needed based on the impact of assumed versus actual behavior change on estimated aggregate expenditures from implementing the PDGM and the 30-day unit of payment. In accordance with the statutory requirement to ensure that the permanent adjustments are implemented in a budget-neutral manner, we finalized our proposal to implement a temporary adjustment of -3.0% to the CY 2027 national, standardized payment rate. This finalized proposal continues recoupment of the retrospective overpayments made for CYs 2020 through 2025 through “one or more” temporary adjustments. We believe that continuing to incrementally adjust the base payment rate to account for the calculated temporary dollar amount to date may help reduce the need for a large reduction in future years. Update to the Fixed-Dollar Loss Amount The HH PPS allows outlier payments to be made to providers for periods of care with unusually large costs due to patient home healthcare needs. Outlier payments are made for periods of care when the estimated costs exceed a threshold amount. To determine the threshold, the 30-day period of care payment rate is multiplied by a fixed-dollar loss (FDL) ratio and then wage-adjusted to derive the FDL amount. The FDL ratio is determined so outlier payments do not exceed 2.5% of total payments (as required by section 1895(b)(5)(A) of the Act) and are annually recalibrated as part of the routine payment update. The final FDL for CY 2027 is 0.29%. Recalibration of PDGM Case-Mix Weights Each of the 432 payment groups under the PDGM has an associated case-mix weight and LUPA threshold. CMS’ policy is to annually recalibrate the case-mix weights and LUPA thresholds with the most complete utilization data available at the time of rulemaking. In this final rule, CMS is finalizing the recalibrated case-mix weights — including the functional levels and comorbidity adjustment subgroups — and LUPA thresholds with CY 2025 data to more accurately pay for the types of patients HHAs are serving. Provision of Palliative Care Services Under the Home Health Benefit CMS is interested in encouraging beneficiary access to community-based palliative care under the Medicare home health benefit and believes the Medicare home health benefit can be an important step in the care continuum when a patient needs palliative care, either during episodes of serious or progressive illness. In this rule, we respond to comments received on how to best promote access to community-based palliative care services through the Medicare home health benefit. This final rule acknowledges the role of home health in palliative care services and confirms that CMS will provide examples of such services through future sub-regulatory guidance. Request for Information (RFI) on a Home Health-Specific Wage Index This final rule also summarizes and responds to comments received on an RFI on the potential development of a home health-specific wage index from an alternate data source (e.g., Bureau of Labor Statistics) consistent with CMS’s statutory authority and regulatory requirements. Durable Medical Equipment Benefit Expansion for Infusion Pumps and Drugs CMS is amending the Medicare Part B definition of durable medical equipment (DME) at 42 CFR 414.202 to implement Section 6222(a) of the Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2026 (CAA, 2026), which expands coverage under the DME benefit to certain external infusion pumps and associated home infusion drugs that would be considered appropriate for home use if certain criteria are met, such as the requirement for the drug to be administered by or under the supervision of a healthcare professional. The effective date of this provision is April 1, 2027. Specifically, section 6222(a) of the CAA, 2026, amended the definition of DME at section 1861(n) of the Act to include coverage of external infusion pumps and associated ho

### [Calendar Year (CY) 2027 Medicare Physician Fee Schedule Final Rule](https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/calendar-year-cy-2027-medicare-physician-fee-schedule-final-rule)

- Source: `cms_fact_sheets` · published `2026-11-04` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/calendar-year-cy-2027-medicare-physician-fee-schedule-final-rule`

Calendar Year (CY) 2027 Medicare Physician Fee Schedule Final Rule On 2026, the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS) issued a final rule that finalizes policy changes for Medicare payments under the Physician Fee Schedule (PFS), and other Medicare Part B issues, effective on or after Jan. 1, 2027. The calendar year (CY) 2027 PFS final rule is one of several that reflect a broader Administration-wide strategy to create a healthcare system that results in better quality, efficiency, empowerment, and innovation for all Medicare beneficiaries. Background on the Physician Fee Schedule Since 1992, Medicare payment has been made under the PFS for the services of physicians and other billing professionals in a variety of settings. For most services furnished in a physician’s office, Medicare pays physicians and other professionals at a single rate based on the full range of resources involved in furnishing the service. In contrast, PFS rates paid to physicians and other billing practitioners in facility settings, such as a hospital outpatient department (HOPD) or an ambulatory surgical center (ASC), reflect only the portion of the resources typically incurred by the practitioner while furnishing the service. For many diagnostic tests and a limited number of other services under the PFS, separate payment may be made for the professional and technical components of services. The technical component is frequently billed by suppliers for which no institutional payment is made, like independent diagnostic testing facilities and radiation treatment centers, while the professional component is billed by the physician or practitioner. Payments are based on the relative resources typically used to furnish the service. Relative value units (RVUs) are applied to each service for work, practice expense, and malpractice expense. These RVUs become payment rates through the application of a conversion factor. Geographic adjusters (geographic practice cost indices) are also applied to the total RVUs to account for variation in costs by geographic area. Payment rates are calculated to include an overall payment update specified by statute. CY 2027 PFS Rate Setting and Conversion Factor As required by statute, beginning in CY 2026, there are two separate conversion factors: one for qualifying alternative payment model (APM) participants (QPs) and one for physicians and practitioners who are not QPs. By statute, QPs are those that meet certain thresholds for participation in an Advanced APM, which means generally that the payment model has features to ensure accountability for quality and cost of care. The update to the qualifying APM conversion factor for CY 2027 is +0.75% while the update to the non-qualifying APM conversion factor for CY 2027 is +0.25%. The changes to the PFS conversion factors for CY 2027 include these updates as required by statute and an estimated -0.12% adjustment necessary to account for proposed changes in work RVUs for some services. However, Public Law 119-21, which CMS refers to as the Working Families Tax Cut (WFTC) legislation, provided a one-year PFS conversion factor increase of 2.50% for CY 2026, which will no longer be in effect for CY 2027. This effectively means that current law requires -2.50% reduction in Medicare payment under the PFS compared to CY 2026. The CY 2027 qualifying APM conversion factor of $32.95 represents a projected decrease of $0.62 (-1.83%) from the current conversion factor of $33.57. Similarly, the CY 2027 nonqualifying APM conversion factor of $32.63 represents a projected decrease of $0.77 (-2.31%) from the current conversion factor of $33.40. Accounting for Overlap Between Stand-Alone E/M Visits and Global Periods For CY 2027, we proposed to reduce payment when a separately identifiable office/outpatient evaluation and management (E/M) visit is furnished by the same physician (or a physician in the same practice) on the same day as a 0-, 10-, or 90-day global procedure. The most expensive service (either surgical or E/M visit) would be paid at 100% and all other surgical procedure(s) or E/M visit(s) furnished on the same day would be paid at 50%. A similar policy was first proposed in the CY 2019 PFS proposed rule, made in the context of a broader proposal that would have modified the payment structure of E/M visits. While we did not finalize the proposal at that time, we noted in the CY 2019 PFS final rule that we continued to believe that there are efficiencies when the same physician (or a physician in the same group practice) provides an E/M service for the same patient in conjunction with a procedure with a global period and that we are likely duplicating payment under the current payment methodology. After considering the comments we received from the public, we made two significant modifications to our proposed policy. We are finalizing payment reduction when a separately identifiable office/outpatient (O/O) evaluation and management (E/M) visit is furnished on the same day as a 0-day global procedure, rather than also including the 10- or 90-day global procedures as proposed. Additionally, when O/O E/M visits are billed separately with procedures on the same day, the most expensive service (either surgical or E/M visit) will be paid at 100% and the other surgical procedure(s) or O/O E/M visit(s) furnished on the same day will be paid at 80%, rather than 50% as proposed. E/M Visit Complexity Add-On (HCPCS code G2211) In the CY 2021 PFS final rule, we finalized separate payment for the office/outpatient evaluation and management (O/O E/M) visit complexity add-on code, HCPCS code G2211. Policy implementation was temporarily delayed by statute but implemented for CY 2025. For CY 2027, we are finalizing two changes, as proposed. We will transition HCPCS code G2211 to a modifier that can be appended to the associated E/M base code. (This was placeholder modifier MOD1 in the proposed rule. We are finalizing this as modifier B1.) This modifier will increase the payment of the associated E/M code by 16%, instead of a flat rate, maintaining an equal percentage increase across all levels of E/M codes. We believe this will result in more streamlined billing that will be easier for beneficiaries to understand. We also propose to recognize additional resource costs incurred by practitioners in Accountable Care Organizations (ACOs) when providing longitudinal care, such as maintaining total cost of care accountability and reporting quality measures that can align with providing longitudinal care and care coordination. This modifier (placeholder modifier MOD2, now being finalized as modifier B2) will be available only for practitioners participating in a Shared Savings Program ACO or Participant Providers in a Long-term Enhanced ACO Design (LEAD) Model ACO and would increase payment of the associated E/M visit by 32%. Under the Shared Savings Program, use of the B2 modifier is voluntary and available to be billed for all beneficiaries to whom an ACO participant or provider furnishes services, not limited only to Shared Savings Program-assigned beneficiaries. The B2 modifier would also be voluntary for the LEAD Model and will be available to be billed for all beneficiaries to whom the Participant Provider furnishes services, not limited only to LEAD-aligned beneficiaries. Claims submitted with B2 will be included in beneficiary assignment calculations, historical benchmark expenditures, and performance year expenditures for both the Shared Savings Program and the LEAD Model. Remote Monitoring Recently, we have established payment for two code families that describe certain remote monitoring services: remote physiologic monitoring (RPM) and remote therapeutic monitoring (RTM). For CY 2027, we proposed: (1) RTM services be furnished only to established patients; (2) practitioners reporting RPM or RTM services must furnish a separately reportable initiating visit in association with the onset of RPM or RTM services; (3) updates to how these services are valued under the PFS; and (4) only allowing payment for RPM or RTM services when performed by clinical staff employed by the practice and not when those services are delivered by contractors. We are finalizing the first three of these policies as proposed for CY 2027. After consideration of the public comments, we are delaying for two years, to Jan. 1, 2029, the requirement to only allow payment for RPM or RTM services when performed by clinical staff employed by the practice to provide additional time for practices to adapt to this aspect of the policy. This policy will be effective for RPM and RTM services beginning with CY 2029. We also sought comment in the CY 2027 PFS proposed rule on bundling the RPM and RTM CPT codes and creating four new HCPCS G-codes to describe remote monitoring services. We received public comments on this proposal and may consider these comments for future rulemaking. Practice Expense CMS has historically relied on the American Medical Association (AMA) surveys to estimate physician work, direct practice expense (PE) inputs, and overall PE for calculating payment rates. These recommendations have been limited by several factors, especially from a reliance on resource-intensive but ultimately unreliable surveys with low response rates asking for expert estimates that present significant discrepancies with alternative empirical data sources, in the cases where empirical data exists. These dynamics are particularly problematic for specialty-level practice expense data. In these cases, data collection is particularly burdensome and subject to volatility based on low response rates and significant incongruities in collection methodologies between physician groups and other entities paid under the PFS. We are engaged in a multi-year effort to transition the PE methodology away from reliance on the AMA’s data and toward an approach that relies on more objective, routinely updated and auditable cost data. We believe this change will lead to PFS payments being more sensitive to market pressures, open broader possibilities for right-sizing payments across outpatient care settings and make payments more transparent for practitioners and beneficiaries. As part of reforming the way PE RVUs are assigned to specific services, we are finalizing our proposal to reduce reliance on outdated specialty-specific practice expense per hour (PE/HR) data. Specifically, we are finalizing the phase-out of the indirect practice cost index (IPCI), which ensures the overall number of PE RVUs by specialty is consistent with the old PE/HR data from 2007 or earlier. Under the methodology, the final PE RVUs would still be derived from the input data (including both work RVUs and direct PE inputs, as well as specialty-specific indirect allocators). However, the IPCI will be phased out over two years and replaced with a PE stabilizer that mitigates short term volatility without anchoring overall values to a specific point in time. We are finalizing our proposal to allocate indirect PE on both the work RVU and clinical labor PE RVU, rather than just the maximum of the two, for all codes except 10- and 90-day global period codes. We are also finalizing our proposal to adjust the indirect PE allocation for visits furnished to beneficiaries in a Part A stay in a skilled nursing facility consistent with our site of service payment differential policy finalized in the CY 2026 PFS final rule. We also received feedback in response to our comment solicitation on whether the site of service payment differential between Facility and Non-facility is still appropriate, or whether we should consider an alternative approach to improve assumptions about indirect practice costs, particularly for physicians employed by a hospital, health system, or other entity. We will consider these comments for future rulemaking. Comment Solicitation on Redesigning Primary Care to Make America Healthy Again For CY 2027, we sou

### [Calendar Year (CY) 2027 Medicare Physician Fee Schedule Final Rule (CMS-1848-F) — Medicare Shared Savings Program](https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/calendar-year-cy-2027-medicare-physician-fee-schedule-final-rule-cms-1848-f-medicare-shared-savings)

- Source: `cms_fact_sheets` · published `2026-11-04` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/calendar-year-cy-2027-medicare-physician-fee-schedule-final-rule-cms-1848-f-medicare-shared-savings`

Calendar Year (CY) 2027 Medicare Physician Fee Schedule Final Rule (CMS-1848-F) — Medicare Shared Savings Program On November 4, 2026, the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS) issued the calendar year (CY) 2027 Medicare Physician Fee Schedule (PFS) final rule (CMS-1848-F), which includes changes to the Medicare Shared Savings Program (Shared Savings Program). These policies aim to accelerate accountable care service delivery, further CMS’ goal of aligning spending and value in Original Medicare, and support achieving other related strategic objectives. This fact sheet summarizes the major changes to the Shared Savings Program included in the CY 2027 PFS final rule. We are finalizing changes to the Shared Savings Program’s benchmarking and financial methodology to strengthen financial incentives for ACOs to participate in the program while mitigating selection issues and benchmark rebasing concerns. The changes are projected to grow the program by adding up to 1 million assigned beneficiaries in an average year during the 10-year projection period, increase net federal savings by $5.5 billion over 10 years (range of uncertainty +/- $3.3 billion), and generate an overall increase in shared savings net of losses to ACOs by $1.1 billion over 10 years (+/- $3.6 billion). Modifications to the Shared Savings Program Financial Methodology Strengthen Financial Incentives to Participate and Encourage Additional Savings in Two-Sided Risk We are finalizing the following changes to the Shared Savings Program’s financial methodology which, in combination, would balance incentives between Level E of the BASIC track and the ENHANCED track, mitigate selection issues and benchmark rebasing concerns, and encourage participation by ACOs with higher risk and higher cost populations. Given the timing of CY 2027 PFS rulemaking and the Shared Savings Program application cycle for the Jan. 1, 2027 start date (occurring in CY 2026), we are providing additional flexibility to ACO applicants in making their final selection of track/level of participation. Accordingly, following the issuance of the CY 2027 PFS final rule, ACOs applying for an agreement period start date of Jan.1, 2027, will be able to change their final selection during Phase 2 RFI (Nov. 6-12, 2026, at 12:00 p.m. (noon) ET). (1) Increase the shared savings rate (“sharing rate”) for Level E of the BASIC track from 50% to 60%. The Shared Savings Program is structured into risk tracks that financially reward ACOs that transform their care delivery practices by taking on higher levels of financial risk and reward on behalf of taxpayers. Currently, evidence suggests that ACOs in BASIC track Level E generate higher savings to the Trust Funds than ACOs in the ENHANCED track, and there are fewer ACOs participating in Level E of the BASIC track than the ENHANCED track. Increasing the sharing rate for BASIC track Level E could more properly balance this participation option relative to the ENHANCED track (with a 75% sharing rate). By reducing the savings percentage gap between BASIC track Level E and the ENHANCED track, we may increase the participation and long-term success of ACOs, particularly those with less experience with the Shared Savings Program, with unique patient and/or provider populations, and low revenue ACOs (which tend to be small, rural, and physician-only ACOs). (2) Reduce the maximum weight used in calculating the positive regional adjustment for lower-spending ACOs participating under the ENHANCED track from 50% to 35%. Our analysis indicates that ENHANCED track ACOs on average, achieve higher gross savings than BASIC track Level E ACOs and have substantially larger positive regional adjustments. Moreover, the average size of the positive regional adjustment has gone up while the share of ACOs receiving such an adjustment has remained stable over the same period. This pattern suggests that, while the prevalence of regional adjustments has remained broadly unchanged, the financial impact of those adjustments has increased over time, amplifying the influence of regional cost variation adjustments on ACO benchmark calculations. In the current structure, ENHANCED track ACOs with lower spending than their region are receiving both a higher shared savings rate and larger positive regional adjustment on average. Together, these advantages may incentivize selection into the ENHANCED track independent of an ACO’s capacity to achieve genuine cost savings to Medicare. The reduction in the positive regional adjustment weight for these ACOs, paired with an increase in the shared savings rate for BASIC track Level E, is designed to rebalance financial incentives across tracks and encourage ACOs to select their tracks based on their actual capacity to reduce costs rather than differences in financial advantages embedded in track design, thereby potentially improving Trust Funds net savings. (3) Increase the prior savings adjustment’s scaling factor from 50% to 75%. Increased prior savings adjustment amounts could provide additional relief from the rebasing “ratchet effect,” in which the savings an ACO achieves in one agreement period can reduce its rebased benchmark for the subsequent period by lowering the historical spending that forms the basis for that benchmark. This change could incentivize ACOs to generate more savings in their initial agreement period and encourage long-term participation in the Shared Savings Program. (4) Risk adjust the 5% cap on upward adjustments to the historical benchmark. ACOs with higher risk populations have expressed concern that the current 5% cap that applies to the three upward adjustments to the historical benchmark (regional adjustment, prior savings adjustment, and population adjustment) is too restrictive. Under the approach we are finalizing, we will account for the severity and case mix of the ACO’s assigned population in calculating the 5% cap, which is based on national per capita FFS expenditures for the assignable beneficiary population. This approach will result in potentially higher upward adjustments to the benchmark for ACOs whose assigned beneficiary populations have higher risk, and potentially higher costs, and thereby encourage such ACOs to enter and remain in the Shared Savings Program. Growth Adjustment to the Historical Benchmark to Incentivize New Participation We are finalizing a growth adjustment to the historical benchmark to reward ACOs for recruiting ACO professionals who are inexperienced with value-based care arrangements and serving beneficiaries new to value-based care. This growth adjustment will be applied on top of the highest existing benchmark adjustments for which an ACO is eligible, either a positive regional adjustment, prior savings adjustment, or population adjustment, and will not exceed the finalized risk adjusted 5% cap. Additionally, the growth incentive will be included in a subsequent agreement period’s prior savings adjustment (if applicable), if the ACO continued to maintain or increase the growth it achieved in the previous agreement period. Reform ACPT Component of the Benchmark Update Factor The Accountable Care Prospective Trend (ACPT) is part of a three-way blended benchmark update factor that projects an administrative growth target to partially insulate ACOs from a downward “ratchet effect” in benchmarks from both individual and collective success in lowering programmatic costs. However, the ACPT depends on cost growth projections that could be subject to potential projection errors. For agreement periods beginning on Jan. 1, 2027, and in subsequent years, we are finalizing an ACPT guardrail policy that limits the effect of under-projection or over-projection as compared to observed growth in expenditures for the national assignable FFS population such that the ACPT is no more than 1 percentage point below (or no more than 1.5 percentage points above) national growth. For ACOs within existing agreement periods with 2024, 2025, and 2026 start dates, we are retroactively applying the lower guardrail, which will limit the ACPT to no more than 1 percentage point below the national growth rate in determining financial reconciliation for PY 2025 and any subsequent performance year of the ACO’s agreement period. We are delaying PY 2025 financial reconciliation until November 2026, after the final rule is issued, so that we can implement this policy for PY 2025 reconciliation. We also are finalizing the policy to calculate annualized growth rates used in determining the ACPT for each performance year for all ACOs in agreement periods beginning on or after Jan. 1, 2027, rather than calculating the annualized growth rates on an agreement period basis (under the existing policy). This approach will improve consistency in the calculations across agreement periods. Changes to the Shared Savings Program Beneficiary Assignment Methodology We are finalizing several changes to the Shared Savings Program’s beneficiary assignment methodology, applicable for the performance year starting on Jan. 1, 2028, and subsequent performance years, with the goal of increasing the number of Medicare beneficiaries involved in accountable care relationships. First, we are excluding from assignment calculations allowed charges for primary care services billed through a non-ACO Taxpayer Identification Number (TIN) by an ACO professional used in assignment. We would apply this approach in determining whether a beneficiary received the plurality of primary care services under the stepwise claims-based assignment methodology. This policy will be a tailored approach reducing the potential for ACO professionals’ billing patterns, inside and outside the ACO, for the care of the same beneficiaries, to result in differences in assignment outcomes that advantage the ACO’s financial performance. Second, we are finalizing modifications to assignment eligibility criteria and prospective assignment exclusion criteria based on Medicare enrollment status. Currently, to be eligible for assignment to an ACO for a performance year or benchmark year, a beneficiary must meet criteria including having at least one month of Part A and Part B enrollment without any month of Part A only or Part B only enrollment, or Medicare group health plan enrollment (including Medicare Advantage (MA)), during the assignment window. For an ACO participating under prospective assignment, a beneficiary may be excluded later from the ACO’s prospective assignment list if they no longer meet these eligibility criteria. We are finalizing modifications to the assignment criteria based on Medicare enrollment status to align with our current operational approach to identifying the assignable beneficiary population. Under our new approach, we will consider as eligible for assignment beneficiaries who have at least one month of Part A and Part B enrollment and no Medicare group health plan enrollment (including MA) during that same month during the 12-month assignment window for the relevant performance year or benchmark year. Third, we are finalizing updates to the definition of primary care services used for beneficiary assignment under the Shared Savings Program to align with payment policy proposals under the Medicare PFS. We are finalizing to include, among other services for determining beneficiary assignment for the performance year starting on Jan. 1, 2027, and subsequent performance years, new codes for Screening, Brief Intervention, and Referral to Treatment, Vaccine Adverse Effects Management, and Advance Care Planning. Shared Savings Program ACOs and ACO participants may also be impacted by the replacement of HCPCS code G2211 (E/M Visit Complexity Add-On) with a modifier and should review the CY 2027 PFS Final Rule Fact Sheet for additional information. Enhance Beneficiary Engagement — Allow ACOs to Reduce or Eliminate Part B Cost Sharing Informed by experience in the ACO REACH model, we are finalizing a

### [Calendar Year 2027 Hospital Outpatient Prospective Payment System (OPPS) and Ambulatory Surgical Center (ASC) Final Rule (CMS-1850-F)](https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/calendar-year-2027-hospital-outpatient-prospective-payment-system-opps-ambulatory-surgical-center-0)

- Source: `cms_fact_sheets` · published `2026-11-04` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/calendar-year-2027-hospital-outpatient-prospective-payment-system-opps-ambulatory-surgical-center-0`

Calendar Year 2027 Hospital Outpatient Prospective Payment System (OPPS) and Ambulatory Surgical Center (ASC) Final Rule (CMS-1850-F) On November 4, 2026, the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS) issued a final rule that updates Medicare payment policies and rates for hospital outpatient and Ambulatory Surgical Center (ASC) services under the Hospital Outpatient Prospective Payment System (OPPS) and ASC Payment System for calendar year (CY) 2027. CMS is publishing this final rule consistent with statutory requirements to review and update Medicare payment rates for hospital outpatient departments and ASCs annually. This fact sheet discusses the final rule’s major provisions. These payment updates affect approximately 3,500 hospitals and approximately 6,400 ASCs. In addition to finalizing the payment rates, this year’s rule includes updates to the methodology used to calculate the Overall Hospital Quality Star Rating to emphasize the Safety of Care measure group in hospitals’ star ratings. CMS is also finalizing changes to the Hospital Outpatient Quality Reporting (OQR) and ASC Quality Reporting (ASCQR) programs to further meaningful measurement and reporting for outpatient quality of care. Updates to OPPS and ASC Payment Rates In accordance with statutory requirements, CMS is finalizing an update to the OPPS payment rates for hospitals that meet applicable quality reporting requirements by 2.3%. This update is based on the projected hospital market basket percentage increase of 3.2%, reduced by a 0.9 percentage point productivity adjustment. For CY 2027, using the hospital market basket update, CMS is finalizing an update factor to the ASC rates of 2.3%. The update applies to ASCs meeting relevant quality reporting requirements. This update is based on the IPPS market basket percentage increase of 3.2%, reduced by 0.9 percentage point for the productivity adjustment. Expanding the Method to Control Unnecessary Increases in the Volume of Outpatient Services In the CY 2019 OPPS/ASC final rule, CMS adopted a method to control unnecessary increases in the volume of clinic visit services furnished in excepted off-campus provider-based departments (PBDs). This method prevents Medicare and beneficiaries from paying significantly more in the excepted off-campus PBD setting than in the physician office setting for some services. In the CY 2026 OPPS/ASC final rule, CMS finalized a policy expansion to include drug administration services furnished in excepted off-campus PBDs. For CY 2027, CMS is finalizing a policy to apply this volume control method to imaging without contrast services, which include certain bone density scans, x-rays, ultrasounds, CT scans and MRIs, furnished in excepted off-campus PBDs. Specifically, CMS is using the agency’s authority under section 1833(t)(2)(F) of the Social Security Act to apply the Physician Fee Schedule equivalent payment rate for any HCPCS codes assigned to the imaging without contrast ambulatory payment classifications (APCs) when provided at an off-campus PBD excepted from section 603 of the Bipartisan Budget Act of 2015. As with the existing volume control method for off-campus clinic visits and drug administration services, CMS is exempting rural Sole Community Hospitals from this policy. For CY 2027, we estimate this provision would reduce Medicare Part B expenditures by approximately $260 million in the first year, including approximately $190 million in Part B savings and approximately $70 million in reduced beneficiary premiums. In addition, beneficiary cost-sharing obligations are estimated to decrease by approximately $70 million in the first year. This policy helps ensure that beneficiaries are not subject to higher premiums and cost sharing based solely on the site at which care is furnished. Reduced Payments for 340B-Acquired Drugs Based on the Medicare OPPS Drugs Acquisition Cost Survey Section 1833(t)(14)(D)(ii) of the Social Security Act requires the Secretary to periodically conduct hospital drug acquisition cost surveys for specified covered outpatient drugs and use this information to help set the payment rates for such drugs. Additionally, on April 18, 2025, President Trump signed Executive Order (E.O.) 14273, “Lowering Drug Prices by Once Again Putting Americans First.” Section 5 of the E.O., “Appropriately Accounting for Acquisition Costs of Drugs in Medicare” directs the Secretary of HHS to publish in the Federal Register a plan to conduct a survey under section 1833(t)(14)(D)(ii) of the Social Security Act to determine the hospital acquisition cost for covered outpatient drugs at hospital outpatient departments. Accordingly, from Jan. 1, 2026, through April 7, 2026, we conducted a survey of the acquisition costs for each separately payable drug acquired by all hospitals paid under the OPPS. The survey revealed significant disparities between hospital acquisition costs for drugs acquired through the 340B program and those drugs acquired outside of the 340B program. In some instances, the survey revealed that the beneficiary cost-sharing amount, typically 20% of the total payment amount, was greater than the total price that the hospital paid for the drug. Taking the survey’s results into account to more accurately align Medicare payments with hospital drug acquisition costs, we are finalizing for CY 2027 to pay for 340B-acquired drugs at the drug’s Average Sales Price (ASP) minus 28%. This payment policy is estimated to reduce Original Medicare drug payment by $4.35 billion and beneficiary drug payments by $1.1 billion in the first year. Statute requires that this policy be implemented in a budget neutral manner, so this policy will increase OPPS payments for non-drug services by an equivalent amount. The individual beneficiary who receives a qualifying drug in the hospital outpatient department is expected to save an average of $900 in drug costs in CY 2027. CY 2027 Prospective Adjustment to Payments for Non-Drug Items and Services to Offset the Increased Payments for Non-Drug Items and Services Made in CY 2018 Through CY 2022 as a Result of the Prior 340B Payment Policy The November 2023 340B Remedy final rule (88 FR 77150) finalized changes to the calculation of the OPPS conversion factor applicable to non-drug items and services beginning in CY 2026. Specifically, effective Jan. 1, 2026, the rule codified a 0.5% reduction in the OPPS conversion factor applicable to non-drug items and services, excluding hospitals that enrolled in Medicare after Jan. 1, 2018. This reduction would remain in effect until the estimated aggregate payment reduction reached the $7.8 billion of increased non-drug items and services payments made from CY 2018 through CY 2022. This prospective offset aimed to balance the goal of restoring hospitals to their financial position had the original 340B policy never existed with avoiding burdening them with an immediate single year recovery. After subsequent reconsideration of balancing these two goals, CMS has determined a shorter timeframe to be more appropriate, consistent with our discussion in the CY 2026 OPPS/ASC final rule with comment period. During CY 2026 rulemaking, we proposed, but did not finalize, a 2% reduction in the OPPS conversion factor for CY 2026. Although we did not finalize an increase to the 0.5% reduction for CY 2026, we indicated in the CY 2026 final rule that we anticipated delaying an increase for only a single year and that commenters should expect an increase greater than 0.5% in CY 2027. Consequently, after consideration of the public comments received on the proposed rule, CMS is finalizing a revision to the annual offset percentage for non-drug items and services from 0.5% to 2% effective CY 2027, excluding hospitals that enrolled in Medicare after Jan. 1, 2018. This 2% reduction will remain in effect until the estimated payment reduction reaches $7.8 billion, which CMS estimates will occur in CY 2031. Eliminating the Inpatient Only (IPO) List To give beneficiaries more choices on where to obtain care with the potential for lower out-of-pocket expenses, CMS is continuing to phase out the IPO list over a three-year period. For this second year of the phase out, CMS is finalizing its proposal to remove 637 services from the following clinical families: auditory, digestive, endocrine, female genital, hemic and lymphatic systems, integumentary, male genital, maternity care and delivery, mediastinum and diaphragm, respiratory, and urinary. CMS believes that the evolving nature of medical practice allows more procedures to be performed on an outpatient basis with a shorter recovery time. This will allow for these services to be paid by Medicare in the hospital outpatient setting when determined to be clinically appropriate, giving physicians greater flexibility in determining the most appropriate site of service. Adjustment to Cost-of-Living in Alaska and Hawaii Hospitals located in Alaska and Hawaii face unique geographical circumstances, including dependency on outside goods and components, remote locations and transportation challenges, and other factors inherent to providing healthcare in these noncontiguous states. While higher labor-related costs for these two states are considered under the OPPS through the IPPS hospital wage index, the nonlabor portion of OPPS payment does not currently address higher nonlabor costs in these states as in the IPPS through a Cost-of-Living-Adjustment (COLA) policy. To resolve this inconsistency between the IPPS and OPPS, we are applying a COLA to the nonlabor share of outpatient hospital service payments provided in Alaska and Hawaii for CY 2027 and future years based on the IPPS COLA. The finalized policy is budget neutral across the OPPS and is projected to increase payments to hospitals in these states by $66 million in CY 2027. Intensive Outpatient and Partial Hospitalization Programs Intensive Outpatient Program (IOP) and Partial Hospitalization Program (PHP) Rate Setting The CY 2027 OPPS/ASC final rule updates Medicare payment rates for IOP and PHP services furnished in hospital outpatient departments and Community Mental Health Centers (CMHCs). The IOP is a distinct and organized outpatient program of psychiatric services provided for individuals who have an acute mental illness or substance use disorder, consisting of a specified group of behavioral health services paid on a per diem basis for a minimum of nine hours of IOP services per week under the OPPS, or other applicable payment system, when furnished in hospital outpatient departments, CMHCs, Federally Qualified Health Centers (FQHCs), and Rural Health Clinics (RHCs). IOP services may also be furnished in Opioid Treatment Programs (OTPs) for opioid use disorder (OUD). The PHP is an intensive, structured outpatient program provided as an alternative to psychiatric hospitalization, consisting of a specified group of mental health services paid on a per diem basis for a minimum of 20 hours of PHP services per week under the OPPS, based on PHP per diem costs. Update to IOP and PHP Payment Rates in Hospital Outpatient Departments and CMHCs CMS is finalizing its proposal to maintain the existing rate structure, with two IOP APCs for each provider type and two PHP APCs for each provider type. For CY 2027, CMS is calculating hospital-based PHP payment rates for three services per day and four or more services per day based on cost per day using OPPS data that includes IOP or PHP and non-IOP or PHP days. CMS believes continuing to use the OPPS data set will allow CMS to capture data from hospital claims that are not identified as IOP or PHP but that include the service codes and intensity required for an IOP or PHP day. CMS calculates CMHC costs based on 40% of the hospital-based PHP and IOP costs. Payment for Software as a Medical Service (SaMS) Over the past decade, there have been rapid developments in the use of software-based technologies to diagnose disease

### [CogniPaz, an Ecologically Oriented Gamified mHealth Tool for Detecting Mild Cognitive Impairment: Prospective Cohort Diagnostic Accuracy Study](https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e99688)

- Source: `jmir_mhealth` · published `2026-11-04` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e99688`

Background: Discriminating mild cognitive impairment (MCI) from subjective cognitive decline (SCD), and healthy controls (HC) remains challenging. Digital tools may overcome paper-and-pencil screening’s limitations. Objective: This study evaluated the diagnostic accuracy and usability of CogniPaz (Daniel Blanco), an ecologically oriented, mobile health (mHealth), gamified cognitive assessment tool exploring memory and executive functions, in discriminating MCI (particularly amnestic phenotypes [aMCI]) from SCD, and HC. Methods: This prospective observational study recruited a convenience sample of consecutive patients with cognitive complaints and HC at La Paz University Hospital Cognitive Disorders Unit from April 2025 to January 2026. Demographic and clinical characteristics, and Montreal Cognitive Assessment (MoCA) scores were collected. An evaluator blinded to the MoCA scores oversaw the automated, self-administered CogniPaz assessment. Participants were classified into HC (no cognitive complaints, MoCA score≥26), SCD (cognitive complaints, MoCA score≥26), and MCI (cognitive complaints without functional impairment, MoCA score<26, subdivided into amnestic [aMCI] and nonamnestic phenotypes). Receiver operating characteristic (ROC) analyses using age-adjusted models, the DeLong test, and bootstrap validation evaluated diagnostic accuracy across groups. The correlation between MoCA and CogniPaz scores was assessed using Spearman rank correlation. Participants with available biomarker data underwent exploratory analysis of CogniPaz’s ability to detect biologically confirmed any-cause neurodegeneration (bND). Usability was tested using an ad hoc Likert questionnaire (1‐9 points). Results: The participants (n=84, 57.1% female; median age 72.0, IQR 62.5-77.0 years) included 27 HC, 17 with SCD, 22 with aMCI, and 18 with nonamnestic MCI. The aMCI group was older than the SCD group (age: median 76.5, IQR 70.8-78.8 vs median 59.0, IQR 50.0-69.0 years; Kruskal-Wallis/Dunn, =.003). CogniPaz scores correlated with MoCA scores (ρ0.81, 95% CI 0.75‐0.89; .001). CogniPaz showed good concurrent accuracy, independently of age: MCI vs HC (area under the curve [AUC]=0.880, 95% CI 0.798‐0.961), MCI vs SCD (AUC=0.915, 95% CI 0.841‐0.990), aMCI vs HC (AUC=0.910, 95% CI 0.830‐0.990), and aMCI vs SCD (AUC=0.943, 95% CI 0.878‐1.000). At the provisional <28.5 cutoff point, sensitivity ranged from 85.0% (34/40, 95% CI 70.2‐94.3) to 90.9% (20/22, 95% CI 70.8‐98.9) and specificity from 74.1% (20/27, 95% CI 53.7‐88.9) to 82.4% (14/17, 95% CI 56.6‐96.2) across comparisons. CogniPaz suggested capacity to detect bND (AUC=0.810, 95% CI 0.661‐0.958), with potential incremental value over MoCA and age (odds ratio [OR]=0.78, 95% CI 0.60‐0.94; =.02). Median administration time was 9.39 (IQR 7.53‐11.38) minutes with 100% (84/84) completion. User satisfaction was high (median score 8.4/9, IQR 7.9‐8.6; Cronbach α=0.84). Conclusions: CogniPaz is an innovative, user-friendly mHealth tool that addresses classical cognitive screening tools’ limitations through a brief, multidomain, minimally supervised gamified format, with high patient acceptance. It correlates with MoCA scores, shows good concurrent capacity to differentiate MCI, particularly aMCI, from SCD and HC, and appears promising for identifying bND. Pending external longitudinal validation, these findings support CogniPaz as a candidate for early cognitive screening in primary care.

### [Development and Content Validation of a Self-Assessment Checklist for Electronic Medical Record Use Certification in Korea: Convergent Mixed Methods Study](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e95500)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-11-04` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e95500`

Background: Electronic medical record (EMR) certification programs have traditionally focused on product-level requirements, such as interoperability, security, and core functionality. However, growing evidence suggests that many patient safety risks associated with EMRs arise after implementation and are closely related to system configuration, workflow integration, and real-world use. Despite the presence of a national EMR product certification system in Korea, a systematic evaluation of how certified systems are implemented and used in clinical practice is still lacking. Objective: This study aimed to develop and validate a self-assessment checklist for EMR use certification, focused on patient safety-critical functions, and to evaluate its content validity and expert-assessed practical applicability across health care institutions of different sizes. Methods: A convergent mixed methods design was used. A self-assessment checklist comprising 10 patient safety–related EMR functions was developed by adapting the US Office of the National Coordinator for Health Information Technology self-assessment templates to the Korean EMR certification context. Content validity was quantitatively assessed using the Content Validity Index across 4 dimensions (relevance, usefulness, clarity, and importance) by a panel of 24 experts with experience in EMR implementation and certification. Qualitative feedback was collected through open-ended survey questions to examine feasibility, anticipated challenges, and differentiation between product and use certification across tertiary referral hospitals, general hospitals, and clinics. Results: The overall Scale-Level Content Validity Index was 0.921, indicating strong content validity for the proposed checklist. Although most items demonstrated high agreement, lower ratings were observed for certain functions, particularly in clinic-level settings, reflecting structural and resource-related constraints rather than limited recognition of patient safety priorities. Qualitative findings highlighted challenges related to staffing, system governance, and the feasibility of assembling multidisciplinary assessment teams in smaller institutions. Conclusions: The self-assessment checklist demonstrated high content validity and was perceived as relevant for evaluating patient safety–critical aspects of EMR use. However, expert-assessed feasibility varied across health care institution types, particularly in clinic-level settings. Further empirical testing is needed to assess the checklist’s reliability, usability, and applicability in real-world certification processes.
