### [Impact of Ambient Scribes on Primary Care Communication Pathways: Qualitative Observation and Interview Study](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e104647)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-11-04` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e104647`

<strong>Background:</strong> The administrative burden of manual documentation has been a primary driver of physician burnout and a barrier to patient-centered care. Ambient scribes have emerged as a promising solution that uses passive audio capture and natural language processing to automate clinical note-taking. While their impact on clinical efficiency is frequently studied, there is a lack of research on how these tools have impacted interpersonal communication during clinical encounters. <strong>Objective:</strong> This study investigates the communication pathways and interactional shifts that occur when ambient scribes are integrated into primary care, with a focus on how physicians adapt their behavior to accommodate an ambient digital listener. <strong>Methods:</strong> We conducted a qualitative study with 17 licensed family practice physicians in the Greater Toronto Area who use ambient scribes in their daily practice. Data collection included observations from in-person clinical shadowing (n=5) and think-aloud walk-throughs (n=12), each followed by semistructured interviews; reported findings are predominantly drawn from interview data. We analyzed the combined dataset using iterative thematic analysis and open coding to identify affordances and constraints along 3 communication pathways: physician-to-patient, physician-to-patient-and-scribe, and physician-to-note. <strong>Results:</strong> Along the physician-to-patient pathway, participants reported that ambient scribes increased their capacity to demonstrate active listening, with some reporting greater attentiveness to nonverbal cues, yet introducing the technology to patients to obtain consent consumed limited consultation time, particularly when addressing sensitive health concerns. Along the physician-to-patient-and-scribe pathway, participants appreciated how ambient scribes acted as a forcing function for physicians to verbalize their clinical reasoning to enhance patient comprehension, as well as their scribes’ ability to follow and organize nonlinear conversations. However, they reported that scribes frequently omitted implicit information (eg, deictic gestures) or created unintended documentation artifacts (eg, casual remarks about family members) that required post hoc corrections. Along the physician-to-note pathway, participants reported that scribes optimized their note-taking workflows and enabled note manipulation via spoken words for some physicians but recalling patient information to fix transcription errors and support patient follow-ups proved challenging. <strong>Conclusions:</strong> Integrating ambient scribes introduces a triadic interactional dynamic into traditionally dyadic patient consultations. Clinicians must continuously navigate the boundaries between patient-facing empathy and scribe-facing precision, deciding when to articulate clinical reasoning aloud and when to exercise verbal discretion regarding sensitive disclosures. While ambient scribes effectively reduce in-encounter computer screen time, they do not eliminate documentation overhead—they redistribute it from real-time typing to postencounter cognitive recall and verbal note curation. To prevent delayed cognitive fatigue from postencounter error verification, we suggest that future ambient scribes should move beyond passive transcription to incorporate interactive recall scaffolding and context-aware error prevention.

### [Interventions for adhesive capsulitis: systematic review and network meta-analysis of randomised controlled trials.](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj-2026-101057)

- Source: `bmj_abstracts` · published `2026-11-04` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `new` · identity: `pmid:42982479`

<h4>Objective</h4>To compare the benefits and harms of therapeutic interventions for adhesive capsulitis.<h4>Design</h4>Systematic review and network meta-analysis of randomised controlled trials.<h4>Data sources</h4>Medline, Cochrane Library, Embase, Web of Science, and Scopus were searched from inception to 1 May 2025, with an update in November 2025.<h4>Eligibility criteria for selecting studies</h4>Randomised controlled trials enrolling adults with adhesive capsulitis, comparing active treatment with another intervention, placebo, or usual care.<h4>Main outcome measures</h4>Pain, function, range of motion, and adverse events.<h4>Results</h4>A total of 189 randomised controlled trials involving 12 988 participants and 59 interventions were included. Most trials contributing to principal outcomes were at high risk of bias (71% for pain, 72% for function, and 71% for range of motion). At short term follow-up, moderate certainty evidence suggested that extracorporeal shockwave therapy probably reduced pain compared with usual care (mean difference -1.97 on a 0-10 scale, 95% confidence interval (CI) -2.97 to -0.97). Low certainty evidence suggested that extracorporeal shockwave therapy may improve function (mean difference -11.42 on a 0-100 scale, 95% CI -16.68 to -6.15), although direct and indirect estimates were limited by important incoherence. For short term range of motion, moderate certainty evidence suggested that manipulation under anaesthesia combined with intra-articular steroid injections probably improved flexion (mean difference 33.29°, 95% CI 11.19° to 55.39°), as did manipulation under anaesthesia alone (26.16°, 8.19° to 44.13°). Low certainty evidence suggested that extracorporeal shockwave therapy may improve flexion (22.42°, 12.99° to 31.84°) and abduction (18.62°, 8.09° to 29.14°), but both comparisons were limited by important incoherence. At mid-term follow-up, evidence for possible improvements in function with extracorporeal shockwave therapy and flexion with passive mobilisation was of low certainty, while evidence for pain reduction with extracorporeal shockwave therapy was of very low certainty. Most other comparative estimates were supported by low or very low certainty evidence. Comparative safety remained highly uncertain because adverse events were incompletely and inconsistently reported; the absence of reported adverse events should not be interpreted as evidence of absence of harm.<h4>Conclusion</h4>Moderate certainty evidence suggests short term pain reduction with extracorporeal shockwave therapy and improved short term flexion with manipulation under anaesthesia, with or without intra-articular steroid injections. Most other treatment effects remain uncertain because of low certainty and incoherence, and comparative safety and mid-term effects are poorly established. The available evidence is mainly short term and outcome specific. Longer term comparative effectiveness, overall benefits and harms, and applicability beyond predominantly primary adhesive capsulitis remain uncertain.<h4>Systematic review registration</h4>PROSPERO CRD420251070468.

### [Perioperative respiratory adverse events in children undergoing adenotonsillectomy with laryngeal mask airway versus endotracheal tube ventilation: multicentre, randomised controlled trial.](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj-2026-101019)

- Source: `bmj_abstracts` · published `2026-11-04` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `new` · identity: `pmid:42982504`

<h4>Objective</h4>To assess the effect of ventilation with a laryngeal mask airway (LMA) compared with endotracheal tube (ETT) on perioperative respiratory adverse events (PRAEs) in children undergoing adenotonsillectomy.<h4>Design</h4>Multicentre, randomised, parallel group trial.<h4>Setting</h4>Four tertiary hospitals in China, 2023-24.<h4>Participants</h4>400 children aged ≥2 years and <7 years who were scheduled for adenotonsillectomy under general anaesthesia.<h4>Interventions</h4>Participants were randomised 1:1 to receive ventilation with a laryngeal mask airway (n=200) or an endotracheal tube (n=200) during adenotonsillectomy.<h4>Main outcome measure</h4>The primary outcome was the proportion of participants who developed any perioperative respiratory adverse event (PRAE): laryngospasm, bronchospasm, desaturation, airway obstruction, severe cough, or stridor in the intention-to-treat population.<h4>Results</h4>400 children (mean age 4.7 (standard deviation 1.0) years; 155 (38.7%) girls) were enrolled between 24 May 2023 and 4 April 2024, with last follow-up on 4 May 2024. LMA was converted to ETT in three participants. Overall, 22.5% (45/200) participants in the LMA group experienced PRAEs versus 57.5% (115/200) in the ETT group (absolute risk difference -35.0 percentage points, 95% confidence interval -43.5 to -25.7; P<0.001). The LMA group had fewer PRAEs in both the intraoperative (9.0% (18/200) <i>v</i> 17.5% (35/200); -8.5, -15.2 to -1.8; P=0.01) and the postoperative period (16.5% (33/200) <i>v</i> 49.5% (99/200); -33.0, -41.2 to -24.0; P<0.001). The LMA group also had a lower proportion of participants with laryngospasm (0.0% <i>v</i> 3.0%; P=0.03), desaturation (15.0% <i>v</i> 28.0%; P=0.002), airway obstruction (3.5% <i>v</i> 12.5%; P<0.001), and severe cough (10.5% <i>v</i> 42.0%; P<0.001). Neither group experienced serious adverse events.<h4>Conclusion</h4>Ventilation with an LMA by experienced anaesthesiologists resulted in fewer perioperative respiratory adverse events than ventilation with an ETT in children undergoing adenotonsillectomy.<h4>Trial registration</h4>Chictr.org.cn ChiCTR2300071399.

### [Preparing for responsive management versus renal dialysis in older people living with kidney failure and frailty or multimorbidity in the UK (Prepare for Kidney Care): a two-arm, superiority, parallel-group, open-label, pragmatic, phase 3, individually randomised controlled trial.](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0140-6736(26)01818-0)

- Source: `lancet_abstracts` · published `2026-11-04` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `new` · identity: `pmid:42982076`

<h4>Background</h4>The comparative effectiveness of conservative kidney management and dialysis in older people (aged ≥80 years regardless of frailty and comorbidity or aged 65-79 years if frailty or multimorbidity are present) living with kidney failure is unknown. We aimed to assess the relative effectiveness of preparing for responsive management-a protocolised version of conservative kidney management-and preparing for renal dialysis.<h4>Methods</h4>Prepare for Kidney Care was a two-arm, superiority, parallel-group, open-label, pragmatic, phase 3, individually randomised controlled trial conducted in 31 kidney centres in England, Wales, Scotland, and Northern Ireland. Eligible patients had an estimated glomerular filtration rate below 15 mL/min per 1·73 m<sup>2</sup>, confirmed by at least one additional reading in the 12 months preceding consent that was not due to acute kidney injury, and were either aged 80 years or older, or aged 65-79 years with a WHO performance status of at least 3 or a Davies comorbidity score of at least 2. Eligible participants were randomly assigned (1:1) to prepare for responsive management-a patient-centred approach focused on slowing disease progression, active symptom management, and preserving quality of life without the use of dialysis or kidney transplantation-or to prepare for renal dialysis. Randomisation was stratified by centre and minimised by age; a centralised system ensured allocation concealment until randomisation had taken place. The primary outcome was the mean difference in quality-adjusted life-years (QALYs), combining length and health-related quality of life from the patient-reported EQ-5D-5L scale, including all follow-up from randomisation to Aug 31, 2025. The primary outcome was analysed by intention to treat in the full analysis set, which included all randomly assigned participants, with missing EQ-5D-5L values for living participants imputed. Harms (hospital admissions and dialysis access complications) and adverse events (deaths and hospital admissions, by cause) were assessed in the full analysis set. The trial was registered with ISRCTN, 17133653 (completed).<h4>Findings</h4>From July 1, 2017, to Aug 31, 2024, 6120 patients were screened across 31 kidney centres in the UK, 445 (7%) of whom were eligible and agreed to be randomly assigned. 228 (51%) of 445 participants were randomly assigned to prepare for responsive management and 217 (49%) of 445 participants were randomly assigned to prepare for renal dialysis. Median age was 82 years (IQR 80 to 86) and 137 (31%) of 445 participants were female. After a mean follow-up duration of 4·5 years (SD 2·1), mean QALYs were 1·354 (95% CI 1·201 to 1·508) in the prepare for responsive management group and 1·472 (1·288 to 1·655) in the prepare for renal dialysis group (adjusted mean difference -0·147 [95% CI -0·350 to 0·056] p=0·16). Specific harms outcomes were not prespecified, but hospital admission rates were similar in the two groups and dialysis access complications were significantly lower in the prepare for responsive management group (adjusted incident rate ratio 0·41 [95% CI 0·19 to 0·88]), based on 74 events in 36 participants across both groups. There were 667 serious adverse events in the prepare for responsive management group (157 [69%] of 228 participants died, with 510 hospital admissions) and 578 in the prepare for renal dialysis group (136 [63%] of 217 participants died, with 442 hospital admissions). None of these serious adverse events met the criteria for expedited reporting.<h4>Interpretation</h4>Among older people living with kidney failure and frailty or multimorbidity, we found no evidence that either preparing for responsive management or preparing for renal dialysis was superior in terms of QALYs over a mean follow-up duration of 4·5 years. This trial provides novel comparative information for clinical decision making.<h4>Funding</h4>National Institute for Health Research Health Technology Assessment programme.

### [Retinoblastoma.](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0140-6736(26)01382-6)

- Source: `lancet_abstracts` · published `2026-11-04` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `new` · identity: `pmid:42982077`

Retinoblastoma, the most common primary intraocular malignancy of childhood, represents a substantial global health equity concern, exemplifying profound disparities in global burden and outcome despite being a largely curable cancer. Retinoblastoma is thought to arise from cone photoreceptor precursor cells of the developing retina, mainly driven by biallelic inactivation of the RB1 tumour suppressor gene. Fewer than 2% of cases have no detectable RB1 pathogenic variant; approximately half of these cases instead show high-level amplification of the MYCN oncogene. Diagnosis and monitoring currently rely on clinical examination and multimodal imaging. Emerging molecular diagnostic approaches such as aqueous humour liquid biopsy hold promise for minimally invasive tumour characterisation and real-time disease monitoring. Management has shifted towards eye-preserving strategies, with intra-arterial, intravitreal, and intracameral chemotherapy showing efficacy and safety. The global burden and mortality of retinoblastoma remains highest in regions with a low sociodemographic index, highlighting the need for continued interdisciplinary, equity-focused efforts to ensure timely diagnosis and accessible, high-quality care worldwide. This Seminar reviews the advances in retinoblastoma biology, diagnosis, treatment, survivorship, and global efforts to reduce inequities.

### [AI-driven multi-horizon risk stratification for heart failure hospitalization in UA with HFpEF: a Chinese multi-center cohort study](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03427-2)

- Source: `npj_digital_medicine` · published `2026-11-05` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03427-2`

Heart failure with preserved ejection fraction (HFpEF) complicated by unstable angina (UA) represents a major challenge in chronic disease management with high risk of recurrent heart failure hospitalization (HFH), and despite the availability of multicenter real-world data, AI-driven tools for multi-horizon risk assessment remain limited. This multicenter retrospective cohort enrolled 4367 patients with UA and HFpEF, and an ensemble feature selection pipeline integrating LASSO, SVM-RFE and Boruta identified robust predictors, followed by training of twelve machine learning algorithms to estimate HFH risk at 12-, 24- and 36-month horizons with external validation. The optimal time-specific models (ET, GBDT and LightGBM) achieved strong discrimination in the training cohort with AUCs of 0.818, 0.834 and 0.863, and maintained performance in external validation with AUCs of 0.839, 0.836 and 0.851, respectively, while SHAP analysis highlighted diabetes mellitus, TyG-BMI and NT-proBNP as the most influential predictors. We developed and externally validated a multi-time-point machine learning framework for HFH risk stratification in UA and HFpEF, which showed acceptable calibration and potential clinical utility on decision-curve analysis. Further prospective and real-world validation across diverse populations and healthcare systems is needed to confirm clinical effectiveness and support translation into routine practice.

### [American Society of Anesthesiologists Physical Status Classification: Hybrid Machine Learning–Large Language Model Ensemble Retrospective Validation Study](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e90724)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-11-05` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e90724`

Background: The American Society of Anesthesiologists Physical Status (ASA-PS) classification system fundamentally shapes perioperative care delivery but suffers from poor interrater reliability (0.4‐0.6). Machine learning (ML) models and large language models (LLMs) offer complementary remedies: ML processes structured data consistently but cannot interpret the nuance of clinical narratives, while LLMs parse narrative text and generate explanations but adhere inconsistently to diagnostic criteria. Objective: This study aimed to develop and evaluate a parallel ML-LLM ensemble that combines the complementary strengths of both approaches for automated ASA-PS classification. Methods: We retrospectively analyzed 3000 adult surgical encounters from the University of Arkansas for Medical Sciences (UAMS) between August 2024 and May 2025. Cases were randomly allocated to training (n=2000), validation (N=500), and held-out test sets (N=500). The validation set was used exclusively for architecture comparison and hyperparameter tuning, and the held-out test set was reserved for final model performance validation. We developed multiple architectures, including traditional ML models (Extreme Gradient Boosting [XGBoost], Light Gradient Boosting Machine [LightGBM], ExtraTrees), an LLM-only baseline (Generative Pretrained Transformer-4o [GPT-4o]), and hybrid approaches. The parallel ensemble processed structured data through XGBoost and unstructured clinical notes through GPT-4o independently, with outputs combined via weighted averaging. Model performance was evaluated using macro--score, exact match accuracy, and ±1-class accuracy. Results: A theoretical performance ceiling of macro--score=0.59 was determined through evaluation by an expert panel of 2 board-certified anesthesiologists (AF and GF) who independently rated 50 patient charts for ASA-PS scores. On the true held-out test set (N=500), the parallel ensemble (=0.30) achieved a macro--score of 0.530 (95% CI 0.350‐0.600), exact match accuracy of 0.630 (95% CI 0.580‐0.670), and ±1-class accuracy of 0.980 (95% CI 0.968‐0.992), exceeding the frozen LLM-only baseline evaluated on the same set (macro--score 0.493, exact match accuracy 0.621,±1-class accuracy 0.981). On the validation set used for architecture selection, the ensemble outperformed structured-only ML (XGBoost macro--score=0.333, &lt;.001), and the LLM-only model reached a macro--score of 0.642 (95% CI 0.525‐0.691), nominally exceeding the expert panel ceiling. Per-class analysis attributed this to a perfect score on the 2 validation ASA-PS 5 cases. Excluding that class, the adjusted macro--score was approximately 0.55, within the range of the parallel ensemble. The LLM component also generated explanations detailing comorbidities, severity descriptors, and functional status indicators. Conclusions: The parallel ML-LLM ensemble achieved performance approaching the theoretical ceiling established by the expert panel benchmark while providing interpretable clinical explanations. The ±1-class accuracy of 0.980 (95% CI 0.968‐0.992) may enhance operational safety by minimizing extreme misclassifications. This approach demonstrates how complementary AI architectures can enhance perioperative risk assessment, which is particularly valuable given current health care workforce shortages. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/29f81801922fcf9c4f2f347a164a32ee" />

### [Application of Digital Health Interventions for Cognitive Management in Children With Cancer: Scoping Review](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e104881)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-11-05` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e104881`

Background: Advances in the diagnosis and treatment of childhood cancer have substantially improved survival, resulting in a growing population of survivors of childhood cancer who may experience long-term treatment-related sequelae. Cognitive late effects are an important long-term consequence of childhood cancer and can affect cognitive functioning, psychosocial well-being, and quality of life. Digital health interventions offer accessible and flexible approaches to managing cognitive difficulties among children with cancer. However, the existing evidence has not yet been comprehensively synthesized. Objective: This study aimed to map and synthesize the existing evidence on digital health interventions for cognitive management among children with cancer; characterize intervention modalities, delivery approaches, and functional components; and identify evidence gaps and priorities for future research. Methods: This scoping review was conducted in accordance with the Arksey and O’Malley framework and the PRISMA-ScR (Preferred Reporting Items for Systematic Reviews and Meta-Analyses Extension for Scoping Reviews) checklist. Six academic databases, including PubMed, Embase (Ovid), CINAHL, PsycINFO, Web of Science, and the Cochrane Library, were systematically searched for relevant studies published between January 2010 and August 2026. Eligibility criteria were developed using the population-concept-context framework. Two reviewers independently performed study screening and data extraction. The methodological quality of included studies was assessed using the Mixed Methods Appraisal Tool. Screening was performed independently at the title/abstract and full-text stages, with disagreements resolved through discussion and, when necessary, consultation with a third reviewer. Descriptive statistics and thematic content analysis were used to synthesize the findings. Results: A total of 5371 records were identified through the literature search. Following screening, 19 publications representing 14 independent study samples and involving 566 children with cancer were included. Four digital health modalities were identified: computer-based interventions, internet-based interventions, mobile apps, and neurofeedback training. Computer-based interventions were the predominant modality. Across these modalities, working memory and attention were the most frequently targeted cognitive domains, while feasibility, acceptability, and adherence were the most commonly assessed implementation-related outcomes. However, relatively few interventions incorporated coach support, telephone-based communication, or educational components, and none evaluated economic outcomes. Conclusions: Although digital health interventions show promise for supporting cognitive function in children with cancer, substantial gaps remain in the current evidence. This scoping review mapped the field according to intervention modalities, delivery approaches, and functional components, rather than focusing solely on the efficacy of individual technologies. Future research should prioritize the development of multidimensional, personalized, and functionally integrated digital health interventions that combine cognitive training with supportive care, education, monitoring, and communication. Multicenter randomized controlled trials should subsequently evaluate these interventions using clinically meaningful outcomes, with comprehensive assessments of safety, adherence, implementation, and health economic outcomes to establish their effectiveness, feasibility, and sustainability. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/4d8731b0eac9b6096979f084d182386b" />

### [Collaborative generalist-specialist AI for safe worklist deprioritization of non-contrast brain computed tomography](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03447-y)

- Source: `npj_digital_medicine` · published `2026-11-05` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03447-y`

Safe deprioritization of non-contrast brain computed tomography (NCCT) demands high sensitivity and negative predictive value (NPV) across common and uncommon findings requiring clinical attention. Here, we present a collaborative safety net integrating BrainCT-CLIP, a generalist foundation model via contrastive language-image pre-training (CLIP), with commercial detectors. Every examination remained in the reporting workflow, deprioritized only when no model returned a positive result. In a retrospective multicenter external validation of consecutive emergency and trauma cohorts (n = 2001), standalone commercial detectors left 31 of 324 remarkable examinations deprioritized, including 16 target-disease false negatives and 15 outside their indications. In contrast, the collaborative system recovered every remarkable examination at a threshold prespecified in the development cohort, achieving 100% sensitivity and NPV while deprioritizing 18% of examinations (368/2001; 95% CI, 17–20). These findings support generalist-specialist AI for safe worklist deprioritization under radiologist oversight, with high sensitivity for intracranial findings requiring clinical attention.

### [Digital Health Technologies for Mobility Recovery Among Intensive Care Unit Survivors: Scoping Review](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e105445)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-11-05` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e105445`

Background: Mobility recovery after critical illness is a complex and multidimensional process. Digital health technologies have emerged as approaches for delivering rehabilitation, monitoring recovery, and facilitating self-management among intensive care unit (ICU) survivors. However, the extent to which current digital health approaches address the multidimensional nature of mobility recovery remains unclear. Objective: This scoping review aimed to map the current evidence on digital health approaches supporting mobility recovery among ICU survivors and to examine the mobility domains, outcomes, feasibility, and acceptability associated with these approaches. Methods: A scoping review was conducted following the Arksey and O’Malley framework and was reported according to the Preferred Reporting Items for Systematic Reviews and Meta-Analyses Extension for Scoping Reviews (PRISMA-ScR). PubMed, Embase, CINAHL, Web of Science, and the Cochrane Library were searched for studies published from January 2016 to September 21, 2026. Studies involving adult ICU survivors that used digital health approaches to assess, monitor, support, or promote mobility recovery were included. Mobility outcomes were mapped to the International Classification of Functioning, Disability and Health domains of body functions and structures, activities, and participation. Clinical outcomes were synthesized separately from implementation and user experience outcomes. Results: Twenty-seven reports representing 24 unique parent studies were included. Digital health approaches comprised telemedicine, telerehabilitation, mobile health, wearable monitoring, virtual reality, robot-assisted rehabilitation, and digital recovery platforms. Telemedicine and telerehabilitation were the most frequently reported approaches, and exercise-based rehabilitation was the predominant intervention component. Mobility outcomes were heterogeneous and concentrated on body functions and structures and activity-level performance, particularly muscle strength, exercise capacity, and functional performance, whereas participation outcomes were less frequently represented. Controlled and comparative studies showed mixed between-group findings, with clinical benefits not consistently demonstrated across primary or mobility-related outcomes. In contrast, feasibility, adherence, usability, and acceptability were generally favorable when assessed, although technical difficulties and incomplete device-generated data were reported in some studies. Conclusions: Digital health technologies offer diverse approaches to supporting mobility recovery after critical illness, but current evidence remains concentrated on physical function and activity-level outcomes, with limited attention to participation and long-term recovery. More rigorous and longitudinal studies using standardized, multidimensional mobility outcomes are needed to clarify clinical effectiveness and support sustainable implementation. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/f9db256efd5ac66e4e49f4bffd6ae812" />

### [Digital readiness for cardiovascular care in high-burden US communities with cardiology workforce constraints](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04800-0)

- Source: `nature_medicine` · published `2026-11-05` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04800-0`

Digital health may extend cardiovascular care in communities with limited specialist access, but its effectiveness depends on whether communities have the infrastructure, devices, affordability, and capacity needed to use digital services. We linked national data on cardiometabolic disease burden, cardiology workforce, and digital readiness across 82,999 US census tracts. Among high-burden tracts in counties with no cardiologist or declining per-capita cardiologist supply, 4,765 (18.0%) tracts representing 19.9 million residents had higher digital readiness and were thus classified as deployment-priority tracts, whereas 21,752 (82.0%) tracts representing 74.1 million residents had lower readiness and were investment-priority tracts. Investment-priority tracts were spread across the urban-rural continuum and faced different barriers (adoption versus infrastructure). Contrasting classifications frequently occurred among neighboring census tracts within the same metropolitan area, highlighting geographic variation not captured by county-level targeting. Findings were broadly consistent across nine additional analyses with varying measures of readiness, workforce access, and classification thresholds. These findings provide a national framework for distinguishing communities where digital cardiovascular care may be deployed from those where infrastructure and adoption support may first be needed to enable equitable implementation. As presented at 2026 AHA Scientific Sessions: Analysis of U.S. Census and other national health data identifies communities with high cardiovascular care needs and assess health system readiness for digital care deployment.

### [Eating disorders.](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0140-6736(26)01326-7)

- Source: `lancet_abstracts` · published `2026-11-05` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `new` · identity: `pmid:42987891`

The eating and feeding disorders are serious psychiatric disorders at the mind-body interface, often with an exceptionally high burden for affected individuals, their families and supporters, and wider society. In anorexia nervosa, high acute medical risk and patients' ambivalence about treatment can make a challenging combination that can impede engagement. First-line treatments for eating disorders are mainly psychological, with medications playing an adjunctive role. Novel biological and technology-based interventions are only slowly emerging. Compared with other psychiatric disorders, research into these disorders is seriously underfunded. Many uncertainties regarding their aetiology, maintenance, and treatment remain. Controversies mainly concern the treatment and care of those with the most severe and longstanding forms of illness. Advances in knowledge are most likely to arise from longitudinal studies combining multimodal assessments, using big data to develop better mechanistic understanding, and greater use of flexible trial designs to allow development of more personalised and precise interventions.

### [Expert Perspectives on Transparency and Trust in AI-Based Clinical Decision Support Systems for Chest X-Ray Diagnosis: Qualitative Study](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e99472)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-11-05` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e99472`

Background: The integration of AI-based Clinical Decision Support Systems (AI-CDSS) into clinical practice has expanded rapidly, particularly in medical imaging. Beyond algorithmic performance, growing evidence suggests that clinicians’ engagement with AI-CDSS is shaped by how transparently systems communicate their outputs, and how trust is formed through human-AI interaction. However, a limited understanding of how these concepts are perceived by end users and developers continues to hinder real-world implementation. Objective: This study aims to explore expert perspectives on transparency and trust in AI-CDSS for chest X-ray diagnosis, with a focus on the two-dimensional lens: user interface and users’ mental models. Methods: A qualitative study was conducted using semistructured interviews with a total of 21 experts, including 11 radiologists and 10 AI experts experienced in AI-CDSS. Data were analyzed thematically to explore factors perceived to influence transparent communication and trust. Results: Three themes emerged: (1) perspective of transparency and trust, (2) user interface, and (3) users’ mental models. Findings reveal that trust is a dynamic calibration process developed through repeated use, feedback loops, and alignment with clinicians’ mental models, rather than algorithmic accuracy alone. Significant discrepancies were identified between how developers design system transparency and how clinicians interpret AI outputs. Conclusions: This study demonstrates that transparency and trust are achieved through interpretable outputs, clear communication of system limitations, and alignment with clinical workflows. Designing trustworthy AI-CDSS requires integrating transparent interface design with consideration of users’ knowledge, experience, and attitudes to support effective human-AI interaction.

### [Exposure to legitimate and illegal online medicine vendors via search engines in 30 European countries](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03432-5)

- Source: `npj_digital_medicine` · published `2026-11-05` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03432-5`

Online medicine sales have not only improved access but also expose consumers to illegal online medicine vendors (IOMVs) selling substandard/falsified products. This study provides the first pan-European assessment of exposure to legitimate online pharmacies (LOPs) and IOMVs in search engine results (SERs). We scraped the top-50 Google results in 30 European countries for six tracer active pharmaceutical ingredients across three categories: controlled substances, prescription-only medicines (POMs), and over-the-counter products (OTCs). Links were categorised as LOPs or IOMVs based on official registries. From 9000 SERs, 3839 linked to medicine-selling websites (63.7% LOPs, 36.3% IOMVs). LOPs outnumbered and outranked IOMVs, though IOMV:LOP ratios reached 2.37 (Norway) and 2.18 (Cyprus). Search engine redirection affected 56.6% of IOMV links. IOMV prevalence increased with regulatory control: 1.4% (paracetamol), 5.5% (ibuprofen), 10.2% (omeprazole), 55.1% (amoxicillin), 76.2% (alprazolam) and 77.5% (clonazepam). IOMV links were less likely to be highest-ranked and less common in the top-10 SERs in countries permitting online OTC and POM sales, than in those prohibiting them. Despite LOP dominance, Europeans face substantial exposure to IOMV links, higher for controlled substances and in countries prohibiting online sales. Harmonised regulatory action, supported by search engine providers, is needed for safer online medication access.

### [External validation of an image-only ultrasound model for delivery-date prediction in four Chilean public hospitals](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03453-0)

- Source: `npj_digital_medicine` · published `2026-11-05` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03453-0`

Pregnancy dating fails the women who need it most: those who reach care without a first-trimester scan. We externally validated a commercial image-only ultrasound deep-learning model for delivery-date prediction, applied unmodified to 64,235 frames from 3788 examinations of 2550 pregnancies (34% migrant) at four Chilean public hospitals. Mean absolute error was 6.9 days versus 11.6 for the conventional due date, yet most of this gain reflected a shorter local gestation and fell below one day against recentred comparators. Fixed-term comparators assign every pregnancy the same gestational age at birth and therefore cannot rank who will deliver early, at any level of point accuracy: accuracy metrics are blind to the ranking on which management turns whenever the outcome distribution is concentrated, as in term-dominated cohorts. In a prespecified landmark analysis the model’s separate image-only risk score discriminated preterm birth with an area under the curve of 0.86, against 0.52 for gestational age alone; outside the landmark restriction it exceeded a clinical baseline of coded diagnoses plus same-examination biometry by 0.09, although it was miscalibrated. Error and calibration were also assessed by maternal nationality. These classification analyses are exploratory, and no woman was managed with the model; they identify what a prospective study should test.

### [Measuring Perceived Stress in Healthy Adults in Daily Life: Scoping Review of Smartphone-Based Self-Assessment](https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e94204)

- Source: `jmir_mhealth` · published `2026-11-05` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e94204`

<strong>Background:</strong> Smartphone-based self-assessment methods, such as ecological momentary assessment (EMA), are increasingly used to measure perceived stress in research and emerging clinical applications. However, the proliferation of studies and methods has made it difficult for researchers and practitioners to maintain an overview and make informed methodological choices. An updated synthesis of smartphone-based self-report methods is therefore needed. <strong>Objective:</strong> This scoping review aims to systematically map smartphone-based methods for assessing self-reported perceived stress in healthy adults during daily life, to describe measurement characteristics and methodological choices, and to narratively examine the extent to which validity and reliability evidence are reported. <strong>Methods:</strong> Following PRISMA‑ScR (Preferred Reporting Items for Systematic Reviews and Meta-Analyses extension for Scoping Reviews) guidelines, we searched a total of 7 databases: Web of Science, Embase, MEDLINE, PubMed, IEEE Xplore, ACM, and PsycInfo. Eligible studies assessed general perceived stress in healthy adults using smartphone-based self-report measures in daily life. Screening was conducted independently by at least 2 reviewers at the abstract and full‑text levels. Methodological characteristics of stress measurement were extracted and summarized descriptively. <strong>Results:</strong> From 8777 deduplicated records, 108 studies met all eligibility criteria. Publication frequency increased notably over the review period. Most studies used observational designs, with EMA-style prompting schedules. Stress was typically assessed using very brief measures: most instruments (84/114, 74%) consisted of a single item, most commonly answered using a Likert-type scale or a visual analog scale. Measurement frequency ranged from once per week to multiple prompts per day, with intensive approaches (≥3 prompts per day) being most common. Despite the prevalence of these instruments, only a minority of studies reported validity or reliability measures. When provided, evidence typically consisted of correlations with established stress questionnaires or affective measures. A small subset conducted elaborate psychometric analyses or validation of instruments. <strong>Conclusions:</strong> Smartphone-based stress assessment is widespread but methodologically heterogeneous. This review provides an updated methodological overview to support researchers in selecting or developing robust smartphone-based measures of perceived stress. Only a small minority of studies reported formal psychometric evaluations, highlighting the need for more systematic validation of smartphone-based stress assessment. <strong>Trial Registration:</strong> OSF Registries 7UM6B; https://osf.io/7um6b

### [Medicare and Medicaid Programs; Cutting Administrative Requirements for Excellence in Patient Care (CARE) (CMS-3484-P)](https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/medicare-medicaid-programs-cutting-administrative-requirements-excellence-patient-care-care-cms-3484)

- Source: `cms_fact_sheets` · published `2026-11-05` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/medicare-medicaid-programs-cutting-administrative-requirements-excellence-patient-care-care-cms-3484`

Medicare and Medicaid Programs; Cutting Administrative Requirements for Excellence in Patient Care (CARE) (CMS-3484-P) Overview On Nov. 5, 2026, the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS) issued the Cutting Administrative Requirements for Excellence in Patient Care (CARE) proposed rule. This rule proposes regulatory revisions to the Conditions of Participation (CoPs), Conditions for Coverage (CfCs), and Requirements for Participation for Medicare- and Medicaid-participating providers and suppliers (collectively referred to as CoPs). These revisions would reduce costs, increase transparency, enhance flexibility for providers to deliver innovative patient care, and streamline existing regulations. It also proposes to rescind certain Medicaid institutional payment transparency reporting requirements to reduce burden for states and institutional providers. Background CMS conducted a comprehensive review of the CoPs for all 21 provider and supplier types, as well as some payment policies, to identify opportunities to reduce administrative burden while maintaining patient safety and quality care. The agency identified obsolete, outdated, and excessively burdensome regulations that CMS proposes to eliminate or reform to enhance the effectiveness of facility operations and reduce unnecessary burden or other costs, with a particular focus on freeing up resources that healthcare providers could otherwise use to improve patient health and safety. Collectively, CMS estimates the proposals would save $320 million, returning more than 3.26 million hours of staff time to providers. This proposed rule would alleviate unnecessary regulatory burden through deregulation in the following ways: Hospitals Remove the staff training requirements for transfer protocols and emergency services readiness (both currently effective), and obstetric (OB) care (otherwise effective Jan. 1, 2027). Remove the OB care-specific Quality Assessment and Performance Improvement (QAPI) requirements (otherwise effective Jan. 1, 2027). Propose to establish new requirements regarding patients’ rights related to birth tissue collection by hospitals that provide OB care services, including standards for informed consent and policies and procedures to support families requesting their birth tissues for cultural, religious, or personal reasons. Critical Access Hospitals (CAHs) Remove the staff training requirements related to emergency services readiness (currently effective) and OB care (otherwise effective Jan. 1, 2027). Remove the OB care-specific Quality Assessment and Performance Improvement (QAPI) requirements (otherwise effective Jan. 1, 2027). Propose to establish new requirements regarding patients’ rights related to birth tissue collection by CAHs that provide OB care services, including standards for informed consent and policies and procedures to support families requesting their birth tissues for cultural, religious, or personal reasons. Revise regulations to clarify current CMS policy that CAH distance eligibility determinations are based on proximity to hospitals that provide acute care inpatient services or another CAH. Implement new statutory requirements to allow CAHs that meet certain qualifying criteria, as defined by the statute, to be exempt from the distance eligibility requirements. Long-Term Care (LTC) Facilities Remove the COVID-19 vaccine requirements related to educating staff about the benefits and risks, offering the vaccine, and documenting these actions. Streamline the remaining COVID-19 vaccine requirements for residents. Remove the continuing respiratory illness data reporting requirement while retaining the requirement for specific reporting during a future Secretary-declared public health emergency (PHE). Modify the requirement for LTC facilities to provide a state ombudsman with notice of resident transfers related to emergent care. Revise the requirements for the active involvement of representatives of direct care staff in conducting the LTC facility assessment. Revise the physical environment requirements to exempt existing facilities from the room occupancy and bathroom facility standards. Intermediate Care Facilities for Individuals with Intellectual Disabilities (ICFs/IID) Remove the requirements related to educating staff about the COVID-19 vaccine and its benefits and risks, offering it to them, and documenting these actions. Streamline the remaining COVID-19 vaccine requirements for clients. Rural Emergency Hospitals (REHs) Remove expired COVID-19 and seasonal influenza reporting requirements related to ongoing data reporting and staff vaccination. Remove REH data reporting requirements during a PHE. Physical Environment Life Safety Code (LSC) and Health Care Facilities Code (HCFC) Updates and Emergency and Standby Powers Systems (ESPS) Codes Adopt the 2024 editions of National Fire Protection Association (NFPA) LSC and HCFC and the 2022 edition of the ESPS Codes. Outside Window or Door Requirement Remove the requirement that patient care areas have access to natural light and ventilation through exterior windows and doors for hospitals, CAHs, and other providers with similar regulations (Religious Nonmedical Health Care Institutions, LTC facilities, ICFs/IID and hospice inpatient care facilities). Waiver Authority Codify in the regulations CMS’ authority to permit facilities to comply with specific provisions of a newer edition of the LSC or HCFC. Medicaid Payment Transparency for Nursing Facilities and ICFs/IID Rescind the following Medicaid institutional payment transparency reporting requirements to reduce burden for states and institutional providers: The percentage of Medicaid payments for nursing facility services spent on compensation for direct care workers, The percentage of Medicaid payments for nursing facility services spent on compensation for support staff, and The percentage of Medicaid payments for ICFs/IID services spent on compensation for support staff. Comment Solicitations CMS is including comment solicitations in this proposed rule to obtain input from interested parties related to the following topics: Allowing LTC facility residents to opt-out of having a discharge notice sent to the state ombudsman office; Strategies to minimize burden for hospitals and CAHs to comply with ongoing respiratory illness and PHE infectious illness data reporting requirements; Approaches to allow flexibility for providers regarding the requirement for physician supervision of certified registered nurse anesthetists (CRNAs); Expanding the scope of practice of physician assistants and nurse practitioners providing care in LTC facilities, especially as it pertains to performing visits currently required by a physician; Revising criteria for transplant program initial certification to reduce barriers to entry for new transplant programs while maintaining patient safety and ensuring the transplant program’s competence; and Future policies to address Medicaid institutional workforce shortages and strengthen the institutional workforce. View the final rule on the Federal Register at: https://www.federalregister.gov/public-inspection/2026-22812/medicare-and-medicaid-programs-cutting-administrative-requirements-for-excellence-in-patient-care . CMS News and Media Group Catherine Howden, Director Media Inquiries Form 202-690-6145

### [Medicare Drug Price Negotiation Program and Medicare Prescription Drug Benefit Program Final Rule (CMS-4215-F)](https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/medicare-drug-price-negotiation-program-medicare-prescription-drug-benefit-program-final-rule-cms)

- Source: `cms_fact_sheets` · published `2026-11-05` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/medicare-drug-price-negotiation-program-medicare-prescription-drug-benefit-program-final-rule-cms`

Medicare Drug Price Negotiation Program and Medicare Prescription Drug Benefit Program Final Rule (CMS-4215-F) On Nov. 5, 2026, the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS) issued a final rule that codifies policies established in guidance documents for the Medicare Drug Price Negotiation Program (“Negotiation Program”) for initial price applicability year 2029 and beyond (CMS-4215-F). For initial price applicability years 2026 through 2028 of the Negotiation Program, statute directed CMS to implement the Negotiation Program through program instruction and other forms of program guidance. With this authority expiring, CMS is codifying the Negotiation Program for 2029 and beyond. In addition to codifying these policies, we are establishing new policies to continue implementation of statutory provisions under the Negotiation Program as well as finalizing revisions or clarifications to certain policies previously established to incorporate lessons learned from ongoing experience. 1 The Negotiation Program final rule is one of several activities that reflect a broader CMS-wide strategy to lower prescription drug costs for people with Medicare. CMS is approaching codification of the Negotiation Program consistent with the goals of achieving greater value for beneficiaries and taxpayers and continuing to foster innovation. The final rule is effective Dec. 5, 2026. Background on the Negotiation Program Sections 11001 and 11002 of the Inflation Reduction Act of 2022 (IRA) (P.L. 117-169) establish the Negotiation Program to negotiate maximum fair prices (MFPs) for certain high expenditure, single source drugs and biological products. Specifically, in accordance with section 1191(c)(3) of the Social Security Act (the Act), MFP means, with respect to a year during a price applicability period and with respect to a selected drug (as defined in section 1192(c) of the Act) with respect to such period, the MFP negotiated in accordance with section 1194 of the Act, and updated to account for inflation in accordance with section 1195(b) of the Act, as applicable, for such drug and year. Under the IRA, with respect to each initial price applicability year, CMS will: (1) publish a list of selected drugs for negotiation and renegotiation, if any; (2) enter into agreements with manufacturers of selected drugs that choose to participate; (3) negotiate (or renegotiate) MFPs for such selected drugs; (4) publish any agreed-upon MFPs for selected drugs; (5) carry out administrative and compliance duties; and (6) impose civil monetary penalties (CMPs), when applicable. CMS will select up to 20 drugs covered under Part D and/or payable under Part B for initial price applicability year 2029 and for each subsequent initial price applicability year. In addition to the drugs selected for negotiation, CMS may also select drugs for renegotiation if selected drugs with an agreed-upon MFP meet certain eligibility and selection criteria. Codification of the Negotiation Program In codifying the requirements for the Negotiation Program, CMS builds upon policies established for the first three cycles of negotiations and establishes new requirements for the fourth and future cycles of negotiations with a particular focus on continuing to increase transparency in the Negotiation Program. The final regulations include, but are not limited to: Identification of Selected Drugs. Section 1192 of the Act establishes the requirements governing the publication of the selected drug list for an initial price applicability year, the identification of selected drugs, ranking of negotiation-eligible drugs, and the identification of qualifying single source drugs. CMS codifies these policies consistent with statute and guidance for initial price applicability year 2028, with a new policy to clarify how CMS will identify qualifying single source drugs to address potential program integrity concerns posed by specific new formulations. Additionally, CMS clarifies the treatment of withdrawn approvals for indications when identifying whether a drug that formerly qualified for the orphan drug exclusion meets eligibility criteria to be a qualifying single source drug, in accordance with amendments made by the “Working Families Tax Cut” legislation (Pub. L. 119-21). Requirements for Manufacturers of Selected Drugs. Section 1193(a) of the Act establishes the Negotiation Program Agreement and the requirements for a willing primary manufacturer to enter into and sign the Negotiation Program Agreement no later than Feb. 28 following the selected drug publication date. The Negotiation Program Agreement will remain in effect, including through renegotiation, as applicable, until the selected drug is no longer considered a selected drug under section 1192(c) of the Act (due to a determination that a generic drug is approved or a biosimilar is licensed and that the generic drug or biosimilar is subject to Bona Fide Marketing) unless it is terminated sooner by the primary manufacturer under specified conditions. CMS finalizes additional details related to the primary manufacturer transfer of responsibility for all requirements of the Negotiation Program Agreement to another entity. In addition, CMS codifies the timing and process for executing and terminating a Negotiation Program Agreement with CMS and the requirements a willing primary manufacturer is subject to upon executing the Negotiation Program Agreement with CMS, including mandatory submission of data to CMS, consistent with statute and guidance for initial price applicability year 2028. Negotiation Factors. After entering into an Agreement with CMS and in accordance with section 1193(a)(4) of the Act, the primary manufacturer of each selected drug must submit the following information with respect to the selected drug: information on the non-Federal Average Manufacturer Price (non-FAMP) and any information that CMS requires to carry out the Negotiation Program, including but not limited to, information related to the manufacturer-specific factors listed in section 1194(e)(1) of the Act. Additionally, primary manufacturers and any interested party may voluntarily submit evidence about the selected drugs and potential therapeutic alternatives as described in section 1194(e)(2) of the Act. In accordance with section 1194(e) of the Act, CMS uses manufacturer-specific data and available evidence about the selected drugs and their therapeutic alternatives as the basis for determining offers and considering counteroffers in the negotiation process. CMS codifies these policies, including submission requirements for primary manufacturers, requirements for primary manufacturers to update submitted data, and how CMS will consider evidence from comparative effectiveness studies. These policies are consistent with statute and guidance for initial price applicability year 2028. Negotiation Process. CMS develops and uses a consistent methodology and process for negotiation with the aim of achieving agreement on “the lowest [MFP] for each selected drug” in accordance with section 1194(b)(1) of the Act. The negotiation process includes engagement with primary manufacturers and interested parties, including patients; the development and delivery of the written initial offer, including a concise justification, to the primary manufacturer of a selected drug; the ability for the primary manufacturer to accept an offer or provide a statutory written counteroffer; optional negotiation meetings between CMS and the primary manufacturer; additional price exchange opportunities; the conclusion of negotiation, including a final offer sent by CMS to the primary manufacturer, where applicable; and the publication of agreed-upon MFPs and explanations of such MFPs. CMS codifies these policies for the negotiation process, including engagement with primary manufacturers and interested parties, the offer and counteroffer process, and the publication and explanation of the MFP. These policies are consistent with statute and guidance for initial price applicability year 2028. Ineligibility for Selection, or Deselection of a Selected Drug Before or During Negotiation, or Following the Negotiation Period . In accordance with sections 1192(c) and 1192(e) of the Act, a potential qualifying single source drug is not eligible for selection, a selected drug will no longer be subject to the negotiation process, and a selected drug will cease to be a selected drug, subject to certain timing and situations, if CMS determines: (1) the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) has approved a generic drug under section 505(j) of the Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act that identifies as its reference-listed drug a product that is included in the potential qualifying single source drug or selected drug (as applicable), or FDA has licensed a biosimilar under section 351(k) of the Public Health Service Act that identifies as its reference product a product that is included in the potential qualifying single source drug or selected drug (as applicable); and (2) the generic drug or biosimilar, as applicable, is marketed pursuant to such approval or licensure. CMS codifies provisions regarding its identification of when such generic drug or biosimilar is marketed within the meaning of these statutory requirements (“Bona Fide Marketing”). Further, CMS codifies the criteria, process, and timing related to the determination of ineligibility of a potential qualifying single source drug that would otherwise be eligible for selection and to the determination of the deselection of a selected drug, with additional information provided on the points in time CMS reviews the information to determine if a generic drug is approved or a biosimilar is licensed and that the generic drug or biosimilar is subject to Bona Fide Marketing. CMS also codifies the criteria, process, and timing related to monitoring that the approved generic drug(s) or licensed biosimilar(s) continue to be subject to Bona Fide Marketing for any potential qualifying single source drug(s) that were previously considered ineligible for selection or for any selected drug(s) deselected because CMS determined that a generic drug is approved or a biosimilar is licensed and that generic drug or biosimilar is subject to Bona Fide Marketing. These policies are consistent with statute and build on CMS guidance regarding the timing of review of the information to evaluate whether a generic drug or biosimilar is subject to Bona Fide Marketing. Renegotiation of an MFP. Section 1194(f) of the Act establishes the requirements governing the identification of renegotiation-eligible drugs, the selection of drugs for renegotiation, and the renegotiation process. CMS codifies the criteria CMS will use to determine renegotiation eligibility and selection, the types of information CMS will consider when determining renegotiation eligibility and selection, opportunities and requirements for data submission, and the process for renegotiation, which will remain consistent, to the extent practicable, with the process and methodology used for negotiation. These policies are consistent with statute and guidance for initial price applicability year 2028. For renegotiation with respect to initial price applicability year 2029 and beyond, CMS finalizes its policy to consider the agreed-upon MFP for a drug selected for renegotiation as part of the consideration of section 1194(e)(1) factors. Strengthening Program Integrity to Address Fixed Combination Drug Loophole Under policy established for initial price applicability years 2026 through 2028, if a drug is a fixed combination drug with two or more active moieties, active ingredients, or, for a vaccine for infectious disease(s), antigen components, a potential qualifying single source drug is identified using the same distinct combination of active moieties, active ingredients, or antigen components. Under this policy, CMS is aware of a program integrity risk

### [Miscarriage Risk Prediction for Women With Polycystic Ovary Syndrome Who Achieve Clinical Pregnancy After Fresh Embryo Transfer: Multicenter Development, Geographic External Validation, and Prospective Temporal Validation Study](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e101829)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-11-05` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e101829`

&lt;strong&gt;Background:&lt;/strong&gt; Women with polycystic ovary syndrome (PCOS) who achieve clinical pregnancy following fresh in vitro fertilization (IVF)/intracytoplasmic sperm injection (ICSI) carry an elevated miscarriage risk. However, individualized risk stratification after clinical pregnancy confirmation but before outcome determination remains challenging in clinical practice. &lt;strong&gt;Objective:&lt;/strong&gt; This study aimed to develop, externally and prospectively validate over time, and interpret a machine learning–based risk stratification model for women with PCOS who had achieved a confirmed clinical pregnancy after fresh IVF/ICSI. &lt;strong&gt;Methods:&lt;/strong&gt; This multicenter study included 8911 clinical pregnancy IVF/ICSI cycles across development (n=7241; miscarriages: n=973), external validation (n=1170; miscarriages: n=146), and prospective temporal validation (n=500; miscarriages: n=62) cohorts. Prespecified predictors contained no observed missing values; training-fold median imputation was kept only as a leakage-safe fallback. A total of 13 candidate machine learning and traditional statistical algorithms were compared internally using repeated stratified 5-fold cross-validation (5 repeats) within the development cohort; preprocessing and hyperparameter tuning were performed exclusively in training folds. A prelocked Extreme Gradient Boosting (XGBoost) model was carried forward unchanged for external and prospective temporal validation. Cross-fitted logistic recalibration of averaged XGBoost out-of-fold probabilities was evaluated as a secondary development-sample diagnostic analysis. Model performance encompassed discrimination, precision-recall, calibration, threshold-based metrics, decision curve analysis, risk stratification, and Shapley additive explanations–based interpretability. &lt;strong&gt;Results:&lt;/strong&gt; Miscarriage rates were 13.4% (973/7241), 12.5% (146/1170), and 12.4% (62/500) for the development, external validation, and prospective temporal validation cohorts, respectively. In repeated cross-validation, XGBoost (area under the receiver operating characteristic curve [AUC] 0.885, 95% CI 0.874-0.896) and LightGBM (AUC 0.882, 95% CI 0.871-0.893) showed comparable discrimination; the paired XGBoost-LightGBM DeLong test was not significant (Holm-adjusted &lt;i&gt;P&lt;/i&gt;=.07). Raw XGBoost out-of-fold probabilities overestimated absolute risk (mean predicted risk 30.3% vs observed risk 13.4%); cross-fitted recalibration within the development cohort improved calibration but was not propagated to validation cohorts. The locked XGBoost model achieved AUCs of 0.787 (95% CI 0.748-0.827; Brier score 0.099) in external validation and 0.763 (95% CI 0.709-0.816; Brier score 0.104) in prospective temporal validation, showing cross-cohort performance attenuation. Risk-quartile stratification using fixed development-cohort cutoffs showed consistent event enrichment across validation cohorts, but positive predictive values remained modest. Shapley additive explanations analysis identified anti-Müllerian hormone, endometrial thickness on the day of human chorionic gonadotropin administration, and basal luteinizing hormone as leading predictors. &lt;strong&gt;Conclusions:&lt;/strong&gt; The locked XGBoost model using routine preoutcome variables may support miscarriage risk stratification and counseling for women with PCOS with clinical pregnancy after fresh IVF/ICSI. Given cross-cohort discrimination loss and calibration drift, it should serve only as a risk-ranking tool, not for diagnosis or treatment selection. Local calibration monitoring is mandatory before any real-world clinical deployment. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/ea172ac64b675d72ef7fa93c35fe7fc3" />

### [Nonreceipt of Newborn Vitamin K and Risk of Bleeding.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.21764)

- Source: `jama_abstracts` · published `2026-11-05` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `new` · identity: `pmid:42986716`

<h4>Importance</h4>Refusal of newborn prophylactic vitamin K has been increasing in the US, placing more infants at risk for vitamin K deficiency bleeding.<h4>Objective</h4>To describe US trends in nonreceipt of newborn vitamin K and infant bleeding, and to compare risks of bleeding and mortality with concurrent bleeding among infants who did and did not receive newborn intramuscular (IM) vitamin K.<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>This retrospective cohort study included infants born at 35 to 43 weeks' gestation between January 1, 2017, and December 31, 2025, at hospitals and health systems from 50 states contributing to the Epic Cosmos dataset.<h4>Exposure</h4>Nonreceipt of newborn IM vitamin K.<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>The primary outcome was bleeding within the first 6 months of life based on International Statistical Classification of Diseases and Related Health Problems, Tenth Revision codes. Secondary outcomes were bleeding during the birth hospitalization and after discharge, intracranial bleeding, and mortality with concurrent bleeding during the birth hospitalization. Robust Poisson regression was used to estimate relative risks while adjusting for covariates.<h4>Results</h4>Among 5 240 780 infants, the rate of vitamin K nonreceipt increased from 3.1% in 2017 to 6.8% in 2025. Between 2017 and 2025, the incidence of bleeding by 6 months increased from 225.3 to 394.0 per 10 000 births in infants who did not receive newborn IM vitamin K, compared with 136.6 to 221.8 per 10 000 in infants who received vitamin K. Infants who did not receive vitamin K had significantly higher risk of bleeding by 6 months than infants who did receive vitamin K (288.0 vs 185.9 per 10 000; adjusted risk ratio [ARR], 1.47; 95% CI, 1.43-1.51). They also had higher risk of intracranial bleeding (33.0 vs 13.2 per 10 000; ARR, 2.43; 95% CI, 2.26-2.62) and mortality with concurrent bleeding during the birth hospitalization (2.9 vs 1.1 per 10 000; ARR, 2.69; 95% CI, 2.09-3.47).<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>Nonreceipt of vitamin K and infant bleeding incidence have increased in the US, and newborns who did not receive vitamin K were at significantly higher risk of bleeding during their first 6 months.

### [Opposing microbiome-mediated axes link modifiable lifestyles to health and chronic diseases](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04744-5)

- Source: `nature_medicine` · published `2026-11-05` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04744-5`

Analyses disentangling the role of the gut microbiome in linking lifestyle factors to health outcomes remain limited. Here, using fecal shotgun metagenomics from 6,803 adults in the Japanese 4D cohort, we identified two opposing microbiome-mediated axes linking lifestyle to health and 15 diseases. A health-associated axis enriched in microbial metabolism of dietary components was linked to plant-based, soy and dairy intake. In contrast, a disease-associated axis, enriched in oral-derived bacteria and pathobionts, was linked to refined carbohydrates and sedentary behavior. Starter culture microorganisms used to ferment foods did not show statistically significant mediation of the observed health effects. Alcohol was associated with both health- and disease-oriented mediation patterns involving microbial host-glycan utilization. Microbiome-defined health- and disease-oriented mediator axes were supported by fecal short-chain fatty acids and plasma cytokines from &gt;1,700 individuals, revealing butyrate-associated anti-inflammatory and pro-inflammatory host profiles, respectively. Microbiome-based prediction (MADE) scores captured lifestyle–microbiome–disease mediation patterns and distinguished individual diseases across 13 independent external cohorts from the United States, China, Sweden, Russia and Australia. We identify the gut microbiome as a key mediator linking lifestyle to health and disease, providing a framework for microbiome-based assessment to guide lifestyle interventions for health promotion and disease prevention. Fecal shotgun metagenomics from 6,803 adults in the Japanese 4D cohort and microbiome-based prediction scores validated across 13 independent and geographically diverse cohorts revealed two opposing microbiome-mediated axes linking lifestyle to health and 15 diseases.

### [Thoracic epidural versus paravertebral blockade for reducing chronic post-thoracotomy pain (TOPIC-2): open label, parallel group, superiority, multicentre, randomised controlled trial.](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj-2026-101114)

- Source: `bmj_abstracts` · published `2026-11-05` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `new` · identity: `pmid:42988384`

<h4>Objectives</h4>To evaluate the effectiveness of paravertebral blockade versus thoracic epidural during the perioperative period in reducing chronic post-thoracotomy pain.<h4>Design</h4>Open label, parallel group, superiority, multicentre, randomised controlled trial.<h4>Setting</h4>15 thoracic centres in the UK.<h4>Participants</h4>Adult patients undergoing thoracotomy randomised (1:1) to either paravertebral blockade or thoracic epidural using a web based service between January 2019 and September 2023.<h4>Interventions</h4>Participants in the paravertebral group received three single-shot paravertebral injections of local anaesthetic before incision <b>f</b>ollowed by placement of a paravertebral catheter. Participants in the epidural group had a thoracic epidural catheter placed and loaded with local anaesthetic before incision.<h4>Main outcome measure</h4>The primary outcome was incidence of chronic post-thoracotomy pain at six months after randomisation, defined as a 100 mm visual analogue score greater than or equal to 40 mm (indicating moderate pain) when considering worst chest pain over the past week. Secondary outcomes included acute pain (visual analogue score of 100 mm) and complications.<h4>Results</h4>770 patients were enrolled, of which 428 (56%) were male (mean (standard deviation, SD) age 66.6 (11.0) years). After 33 exclusions of patients after randomisation who did not proceed to thoracotomy, 737 remained in the modified intention-to-treat population (364 in the paravertebral group, 373 in the epidural group). Six months after randomisation, 59 (22%) of 272 participants in the paravertebral group and 47 (16%) of 292 participants in the epidural group developed chronic post-thoracotomy pain (adjusted risk ratio 1.32 (95% confidence interval (CI) 0.93 to 1.86), adjusted risk difference 0.05 (95% CI -0.01 to 0.11), P=0.12). During the acute phase, worst and average pain was greater on day 1 with paravertebral blockade (adjusted mean difference 7.7 mm (95% CI 2.8 to 12.5 mm) and 7.0 mm (95% CI 2.7 to 11.2 mm), respectively), but no different on days 2-3. Hypotension was less common in the paravertebral group than the epidural group (12% <i>v</i> 18%, respectively, adjusted risk ratio 0.66 (95% CI 0.46 to 0.94), adjusted risk difference -0.06 (95% CI -0.11 to -0.01)), as was pruritis (4% <i>v</i> 12%, adjusted risk ratio 0.37 (95% CI 0.21 to 0.64), adjusted risk difference -0.07 (95% CI -0.11 to -0.03)), although more participants in this group experienced urinary retention (12% <i>v</i> 6%, adjusted risk ratio 2.21 (95% CI 1.35 to 3.61), adjusted risk difference 0.05 (95% CI 0.01 to 0.09)), complications were similar otherwise.<h4>Conclusions</h4>Paravertebral blockade did not reduce chronic post-thoracotomy pain at six month follow-up compared to thoracic epidural. Thoracic epidural provided marginally better acute pain relief one day after the operation, but showed no difference thereafter. Postoperative complications were comparable between groups. The findings support the ongoing use of both techniques.<h4>Trial registration</h4>ClinicalTrials.gov NCT03677856.
