### [A gait foundation model for prediction of multisystem health phenotypes from 3D skeletal motion](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04724-9)

- Source: `nature_medicine` · published `2026-11-06` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04724-9`

Gait is increasingly recognized as a vital sign, yet current approaches treat it as a symptom of specific pathologies rather than a systemic biomarker. Here we developed a gait foundation model for three-dimensional skeletal motion, which was recorded via a single-depth camera during 5 motor tasks, from 3,328 deeply phenotyped adults. Our purpose was to capture the full pattern of a person’s movement and use it to predict health phenotypes such as grip strength and sleep quality across 18 body systems. Gait is conventionally described by engineered features (for example, calculated measurements such as cadence and stride length), which may fail to capture the fine detail of how a person moves. Our model instead translates movement into learned embeddings: sets of numbers that describe how all body joints move together over time. Learned embeddings outperformed engineered features for prediction of age (Pearsonʼs r = 0.69), body mass index (r = 0.91) and visceral adipose tissue area (r = 0.83), and embeddings significantly predicted 1,887 of 3,210 tested phenotypes. Relative to a baseline model using age, body mass index, visceral adipose tissue and height alone, gait embeddings improved prediction in all 18 systems in males and 17 of 18 in females, as well as prediction of clinical diagnoses and medication use. Anatomical ablation analyses revealed that leg dynamics dominated metabolic and frailty predictions, and torso dynamics encoded sleep and lifestyle phenotypes. These findings establish gait as an independent, multisystem biosignal, motivating integration of gait analysis into routine clinical assessment as a scalable, passive vital sign. A gait foundation model, developed using three-dimensional skeletal motion data from 3,328 adults, predicts a wide array of clinical characteristics and diagnoses, including cardiometabolic, mental health and frailty phenotypes.

### [Association Between Intradialytic Foot Temperature Gradients and Severe Peripheral Artery Disease in Patients Undergoing Hemodialysis: Prospective Cohort Study](https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e96782)

- Source: `jmir_mhealth` · published `2026-11-06` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e96782`

Background: Peripheral artery disease (PAD) is prevalent in hemodialysis (HD) patients but remains difficult to diagnose due to unreliable ankle-brachial index (ABI) measurements caused by vascular calcification. We hypothesized that monitoring foot temperatures and their changes during HD, provoked by hemodynamic shifts, could serve as a novel diagnostic tool for detecting severe PAD. Objective: This study aims to evaluate the diagnostic utility of intradialytic foot temperature monitoring using wearable mobile health (mHealth) sensors for detecting severe PAD in patients undergoing HD. Methods: In this prospective observational study, we recruited 109 patients undergoing chronic maintenance HD. Skin temperatures were monitored continuously at the instep and sole during HD sessions using wearable sensors (Temp Pal, iWEECARE Co, Ltd), which are wireless, nanochip-based mHealth thermometers with ±0.05 °C accuracy. The diagnostic performance of temperature parameters, specifically the temperature difference between the wrist and foot at the end of HD, was evaluated against vascular duplex ultrasound (DUS). Severe PAD was defined as abnormal (monophasic or absent) flow in the distal posterior tibial artery (PTA) or anterior tibial artery (ATA). Results: Patients with abnormal tibial flow on DUS exhibited significantly different temperature gradients between the wrist and foot at the end of dialysis compared to those with normal flow. Receiver operating characteristic (ROC) analysis demonstrated that the wrist-foot temperature difference at the sole effectively discriminated abnormal ATA flow (area under the curve [AUC] 64.5%, 95% CI 57.8%‐70.9%) and abnormal PTA flow (AUC 62.0%, 95% CI 55.2%‐68.5%). Notably, an instep wrist-foot temperature difference cutoff of –2.2 °C or less yielded a specificity of 97.6% (95% CI 94.0%-99.3%) for detecting abnormal PTA flow. Conclusions: Intradialytic foot temperature monitoring, particularly the temperature gradient between the wrist and foot, significantly correlates with severe tibial artery flow abnormalities. This method offers an accessible, noninvasive screening adjunct to the ABI for detecting severe PAD in the HD population. These findings indicate an association rather than a standalone diagnostic test; given the modest discriminative ability, external validation is warranted.

### [Balancing Privacy Protection and Reproducible Entity Extraction in Real-World Data in the Development of a Framework for Deidentified Japanese Electronic Health Record Narratives Using BERT: Retrospective Observational Study](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e97411)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-11-06` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e97411`

Background: Pharmacoepidemiologic studies using real-world data often lack clinical context embedded in free-text electronic health records (EHRs). However, sharing EHR narratives is restricted by privacy requirements, and conventional deidentification approaches may reduce analytic utility and limit auditability. Objective: This study aims to develop and evaluate a framework that (1) extracts structured cancer-related entities from Japanese EHR free text using BERT (Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers)–based natural language processing and (2) generates deidentified analytic text that preserves contextual information for post hoc auditing under privacy constraints. Methods: This retrospective observational study used the DATuM IDEA database, which integrates EHR narratives with structured records (claims, prescriptions, and procedure/surgery records) from 2 hospitals in Japan from January 1, 2019, through June 30, 2025. Deterministic linkage was performed within ICI (Integrated Clinical Care Informatics Inc) prior to deidentification. Downstream recoverability analyses were conducted within a governance-constrained analyzable text cohort derived after deidentification and preprocessing. We quantified deidentification using masking rates and evaluated downstream recoverability of TNM (tumor-node-metastasis) or stage-related mentions, internal logical consistency (M1 vs Stage IV), and proxy-based concordance using structured data treatment proxies (Anatomical Therapeutic Chemical [ATC] code L for systemic anticancer therapy and procedure-name keywords for cancer-directed interventions). A stratified manual audit of 400 deidentified narratives primarily inspected for apparent residual direct identifiers and secondarily for retention of TNM and stage outputs. Results: Among 10,214 patients with neoplasms ( [] C00-D48), linked progress notes were available for 4383 patients (82,863 records). The analyzable text cohort (Layer C) comprised 3689 patients and 38,841 documents, of which 1339 patients (1339/3689, 36.3%) and 11,914 documents (11,914/38,841, 30.7%) had at least one recoverable TNM or stage mention (Layer D). Marginal document-level recoverability in Layer C was 24.2% for T, N, and M elements and 23.6% for stage. Conditional on Layer D, it was 78.8% for T, 78.7% for N, 78.8% for M, and 76.8% for stage. Median masking rates ranged from 9.9% to 15% across major cancer categories. M1 and Stage IV mention indicators showed an overall agreement of 94.7%, positive percent agreement of 54.8%, and negative percent agreement of approximately 99%. Proxy-based concordance analysis showed positive predictive values of 0.700 for Stage IV using the systemic therapy proxy and 0.031 for early-stage classification using the procedure proxy. Manual audit demonstrated retention of intended TNM/stage-related outputs after normalization and identified no apparent residual direct identifiers in the audited deidentified narratives. Conclusions: This framework generates deidentified Japanese EHR narratives that preserve contextual structure for auditing while supporting structured entity extraction, addressing the trade-off between privacy protection and reproducibility in real-world data research.

### [Correction: A Machine Learning–Based Model to Predict Overactive Bladder Risk Among US Women: Evidence From the National Health and Nutrition Examination Survey 2011-2018](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e113546)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-11-06` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e113546`

In "A Machine Learning-Based Model to Predict Overactive Bladder Risk Among US Women: Evidence From the National Health and Nutrition Examination Survey 2011-2018" [1], the authors noted the following corrections. First, the Figure 2B legend has been corrected. Original: “Seven predictor variables corresponding to the log(λ) value with the minimum MSE were retained.” Revised: “Fourteen predictor variables corresponding to the log(λ) value with the minimum MSE (λ = 0.00550) were retained.” At the penalty selected under the minimum criterion (λ = 0.00550), the least absolute shrinkage and selection operator (LASSO) model retained 14 non-zero coefficients, and all 14 variables were entered into model construction. The error was confined to the figure legend text, and no numerical result is affected. Second, the Figure 4A legend has been clarified. The legend now states that Figure 4A displays the 7 continuous predictors among the 14 variables retained by LASSO regression. SHAP beeswarm and dependence plots require a continuous colour gradient and therefore cannot display categorical predictors, which are presented separately; no figure content is affected by this clarification. Third, the sentence reporting the significant predictors in the Results section of the Abstract has been corrected. Original: “Five variables were identified as significant predictors.” Revised: “Five of these 14 variables were identified as the most significant clinical predictors.” Fourth, the sentence characterising the predictive accuracy of the model in the Conclusions has been corrected. Original: “The RF model demonstrated good predictive accuracy and practical clinical applicability, serving as a noninvasive, cost-effective tool for early screening and risk stratification in community health and primary outpatient settings.” Revised: “The RF model demonstrated moderate predictive accuracy and practical clinical applicability, serving as a noninvasive, cost-effective tool for early screening and risk stratification in community health and primary outpatient settings.” These corrections affect the wording of the figure legends, the Abstract, and the Conclusions only. All analyses were performed on the 14 predictors retained by LASSO regression, and every reported metric derives from them; no analytical result is affected. The correction will appear in the online version of the paper on the JMIR Publications website together with the publication of this correction notice. Because this was made after submission to PubMed, PubMed Central, and other full-text repositories, the corrected article has also been resubmitted to those repositories.

### [Effective trialing of digital interventions in daily life – the mental health perspective](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03414-7)

- Source: `npj_digital_medicine` · published `2026-11-06` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03414-7`

Digital mental health interventions show promise but have yet to meet expectations. We discuss gaps between promising results of pilot studies and null findings in randomized controlled trials, focusing on two major approaches: just-in-time relapse prevention and just-in-time adaptive interventions. We propose that relapse prevention will profit from long-term studies capturing emerging episodes, and we suggest a novel framework for adaptive interventions including causality testing to proceed towards effective trialing.

### [Effectiveness of a Multicomponent Digital Therapeutic Program on Inhaled Medication Adherence in Chronic Obstructive Pulmonary Disease: Pilot Randomized Controlled Trial](https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e98870)

- Source: `jmir_mhealth` · published `2026-11-06` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e98870`

Background: Poor medication adherence is a critical impediment to effective chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) management and remains challenging to track in routine care. Digital therapeutic interventions may improve chronic disease management through remote monitoring and patient education. However, most existing digital interventions focus on individual components of disease management, and evidence from longer-term pragmatic randomized controlled trials evaluating integrated digital therapeutics in routine clinical practice remains limited. Objective: This study aimed to evaluate the effectiveness of a multicomponent digital therapeutic program combining electronic inhaler monitoring (EIM) with behavioral support in improving medication adherence and symptom-related outcomes among patients with COPD prescribed pressurized metered-dose inhalers, and to assess patient engagement and implementation in routine clinical practice. Methods: In this prospective, open-label, unblinded, randomized controlled trial, patients with COPD prescribed pressurized metered-dose inhalers were enrolled and randomized in a 2:1 ratio to either the intervention group or the control group. All participants received an EIM device to objectively record inhaler use throughout the 24-week study period. Patients in the intervention group also received personalized medication reminders from the EIM, adherence feedback, educational content, and clinician-supported follow-up through an integrated digital management platform based on EIM-derived data, whereas the control group received usual care. The primary outcome was medication adherence over 24 weeks. Secondary outcomes included exacerbations, COPD symptoms, quality of life, and lung function. Results: A total of 158 participants were randomized, including 106 in the intervention group and 52 in the control group. The median monitoring coverage was 161.5 (IQR 86.3‐168.0) days during the follow-up period. The self-reported adherence measured using the Test of Adherence to Inhalers showed no significant between-group differences at any follow-up time point. However, the electronically monitored adherence was significantly higher in the intervention group than in the control group (59.5% vs 33.1%, =.009). Adherence declined over time in both groups, decreasing from 85.3% during weeks 0 to 4 to 43.0% during weeks 12 to 24 in the intervention group and from 76.8% to 11.9% in the control group over the same intervals, resulting in a progressively larger between-group difference over follow-up. No significant differences were observed in exacerbations, health-related quality of life, symptom burden, lung function, or mental health outcomes. In an exploratory analysis, a significantly greater proportion of participants in the intervention group achieved the minimal clinically important difference in the COPD Assessment Test score than in the control group (odds ratio 2.21, 95% CI 1.11‐4.41; =.02). Conclusions: The multicomponent digital therapeutic program significantly improved objective medication adherence in patients with COPD over 24 weeks. While most clinical outcomes did not differ significantly between groups, the higher proportion of participants achieving a clinically meaningful improvement in the COPD Assessment Test score warrants further investigation. Trial Registration: ClinicalTrials.gov NCT05667363; https://clinicaltrials.gov/study/NCT05667363

### [Evaluation of PainWaive: a consumer-grade EEG headset for remote EEG monitoring and neurofeedback research in chronic pain](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03424-5)

- Source: `npj_digital_medicine` · published `2026-11-06` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03424-5`

Affordable electroencephalography (EEG) headsets could widen access to EEG-based treatment and assessment, but require rigorous validation. We evaluated PainWaive, a custom-developed 2-channel central scalp EEG headset designed for remote EEG monitoring and neurofeedback research in chronic pain. Eighty participants completed two resting-state EEGs using PainWaive and a research-grade system (LiveAmp). Alpha, beta and theta power, and peak alpha frequency (PAF), were derived from homologous central channels. PainWaive demonstrated good-to-excellent test-retest reliability for alpha power and PAF (ICCs = 0.72–0.93), with beta and theta ranging from moderate-good (ICCs = 0.42–0.76). Across 160 home-based neurofeedback sessions in two chronic pain samples, reliability was good-to-excellent across metrics (ICCs = 0.72–0.88). Spectral-shape correlations between PainWaive and LiveAmp were excellent (r &gt; 0.91). Cross-device agreement was moderate-to-good for alpha power and PAF (ICCs = 0.53–0.74), but weaker for beta and theta (ICCs = 0.16–0.51). Sensitivity analyses showed alpha power and PAF were robust across choices, whereas beta and theta agreement varied by pipeline. Overall, PainWaive demonstrated reliable measurement of central scalp EEG metrics, supporting repeated within-device monitoring. Cross-device findings showed correspondence with research-grade EEG for some measures but should not be interpreted as establishing direct numerical interchangeability between systems. These findings provide a technical foundation for future remote EEG and neurofeedback studies in chronic pain.

### [Large Language Models for Generating Patient Education Materials: Scoping Review](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e98084)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-11-06` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e98084`

&lt;strong&gt;Background:&lt;/strong&gt; Patient education materials (PEMs) are essential for improving health literacy and clinical outcomes. Although large language models (LLMs) hold promise for optimizing content, their performance in generating new PEMs remains unclear. &lt;strong&gt;Objective:&lt;/strong&gt; This study aimed to summarize the current evidence on LLMs in generating new PEMs, evaluate readability, quality, and usability in various health care settings, and guide future research and practice. &lt;strong&gt;Methods:&lt;/strong&gt; This review followed PRISMA-ScR (Preferred Reporting Items for Systematic Reviews and Meta-Analyses extension for Scoping Reviews) guidelines. A comprehensive literature search was conducted in PubMed, Embase, Cochrane Library, Web of Science, and Scopus from January 1, 2000, to January 1, 2026. Eligible studies were English-language original research studies that evaluated new PEMs generated by LLMs in health care using information assessment tools. Two reviewers independently screened records and extracted data. We conducted a quantitative analysis of the characteristics of the included literature. Furthermore, we classified the PEMs based on core educational tasks and clinical scenarios, and compared their readability, quality, and usability. &lt;strong&gt;Results:&lt;/strong&gt; Thirty studies were included, including 11 LLMs and more than 30 diseases; most compared multiple LLMs and used a single prompt. PEMs were classified into 5 categories: perioperative patient education (n=8), chronic disease management education (n=6), specialty disease diagnostic and therapeutic education (n=9), pharmacotherapy safety and adherence education (n=3), and public health and prevention education (n=4). In terms of readability, only 3 records achieved a sixth-grade reading level. Specialty disease diagnostic and therapeutic education performed relatively well, while chronic disease management education had poor readability. The DeepSeek series demonstrated the most consistent performance. In terms of quality, most records were rated as good or higher. Specialty disease diagnostic and therapeutic education showed a polarized distribution, with both high and low quality ratings. Pharmacotherapy safety and adherence education and public health and prevention education had no poor-quality ratings. The ChatGPT series performed relatively well. In terms of usability, most records performed well in understandability but poor in actionability. Perioperative patient education showed a relatively balanced performance. &lt;strong&gt;Conclusions:&lt;/strong&gt; LLMs offer a transformative opportunity for developing effective and high-quality PEMs, but they currently fail to meet health literacy standards, particularly in readability and actionability. Future efforts must shift from performance evaluation to implementation strategies. Only through rigorous validation and equitable deployment can LLMs truly improve patient education. &lt;strong&gt;Trial Registration:&lt;/strong&gt; <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/4ad66a203f2acb072c16d7528ba106b0" />

### [Large Vision-Language Models for Pediatric Motor Tic Detection: Case-Control Diagnostic Study](https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e98782/)

- Source: `jmir_ai` · published `2026-11-06` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e98782/`

&lt;strong&gt;Background:&lt;/strong&gt; Clinical diagnosis of tic disorders (TDs) is often delayed by fluctuating symptoms and the subjective nature of assessments. While automated methods have been proposed to bridge this gap, traditional machine learning models frequently lack robustness in unconstrained environments and fail to capture the complex, multisite tics characteristic of severe TDs. &lt;strong&gt;Objective:&lt;/strong&gt; This study aimed to develop and evaluate a fine-tuned large vision-language model framework for the automated, interpretable detection of pediatric motor tics, addressing the clinical need for objective and efficient symptom assessment in unconstrained environments. &lt;strong&gt;Methods:&lt;/strong&gt; In this multicenter study, 168 pediatric participants (n=83, 49.4% with TDs and n=85, 50.6% healthy controls) underwent 12-hour continuous video-electroencephalography monitoring. We fine-tuned the Qwen3-VL-8B model using 3- to 5-minute video segments via low-rank adaptation. The framework used chain-of-thought prompting to generate both binary classifications and textual justifications. A strict patient-wise split ensured zero identity overlap between training and test sets. Diagnostic precision was evaluated using 95% CIs calculated with the Clopper-Pearson exact method. &lt;strong&gt;Results:&lt;/strong&gt; In total, 168 pediatric participants (age: mean 8.3, SD 2.3 years) were enrolled, providing 559 expert-validated video segments for analysis. On the independent test set (n=55), the framework achieved 89.1% (49/55; 95% CI 77.8%-95.9%) accuracy and an area under the curve of 0.950 (95% CI 0.892-1.000), significantly outperforming the landmark-based deep neural network (accuracy 74.1%; &lt;i&gt;P&lt;/i&gt;=.004); recall reached 100% (25/25 positive episodes; 95% CI 86.3%-100.0%). Model-generated justifications showed substantial agreement with expert annotations (κ=0.784, 95% CI 0.625-0.943; &lt;i&gt;P&lt;/i&gt;&amp;lt;.001). &lt;strong&gt;Conclusions:&lt;/strong&gt; These findings suggest that a vision-language model framework can capture clinically relevant tic features beyond conventional geometric descriptors. By combining high sensitivity (recall) with text-based diagnostic justifications aligned with clinical severity dimensions (eg, frequency and intensity), the approach may support automated video review and preliminary symptom quantification in controlled monitoring settings. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/430f0cfe6e3562b2d81d7efe344ba71e" />

### [Nurses’ Attitudes and Experiences With Generative AI in Clinical Practice: Qualitative Systematic Review](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e99489)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-11-06` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e99489`

Background: The rapid advancement of generative AI (GenAI) holds transformative potential for clinical nursing practice, yet its integration also brings significant risks and uncertainties. Despite growing discourse on AI in health care, frontline nurses’ firsthand experiences, perceptions, and practical needs regarding GenAI remain largely under-synthesized in the existing literature. Objective: This systematic review aimed to synthesize qualitative evidence to clarify the core themes related to clinical nurses’ perceptions, experiences, obstacles, and requirements when using GenAI in real-world clinical settings. Methods: A systematic search was conducted across 10 electronic databases (PubMed, Embase, CINAHL, Web of Science, Cochrane Library, ProQuest Dissertations and Theses, China National Knowledge Infrastructure, VIP Information, SinoMed, and Wanfang) for records published between January 2018 and June 2026. Eligible studies included qualitative and mixed methods studies involving registered nurses that explored their perceptions and experiences of GenAI in clinical practice. Two independent reviewers (MMW and CYZ) performed study selection, quality appraisal, and data extraction, with disagreements resolved by consensus. Quality was assessed using the Joanna Briggs Institute (JBI) Critical Appraisal Checklist for Qualitative Research. Extracted data encompassed study characteristics, participant demographics, and GenAI-related contextual factors. Data synthesis followed Thomas and Harden’s 3-phase thematic synthesis: initial inductive coding generated descriptive themes, which were subsequently integrated with the Technology Acceptance Model for Artificial Intelligence in Nursing (TAM-AIN) framework to develop higher-order analytical themes. Certainty in the evidence was evaluated using the GRADE-CERQual (Grading of Recommendations Assessment, Development and Evaluation-Confidence in the Evidence from Reviews of Qualitative Research). Results: Of the 4350 initially identified records, 13 studies from 6 countries (China: n=6; Saudi Arabia: n=3; United States, Singapore, Australia, and South Korea: n=1 each) met the inclusion criteria, encompassing qualitative data from 234 registered nurses. Three main themes emerged: patterns of nurses’ engagement with GenAI (ranging from passive observation to proactive integration), multidimensional perceived threats to adoption (functional, professional, and accountability-related), and the moderating role of organizational readiness, which influenced whether these threats hindered or facilitated GenAI uptake in practice. The GRADE-CERQual assessment showed moderate to high confidence in all findings. Conclusions: Nurses’ engagement with GenAI ranges from passive observation to proactive integration, accompanied by legitimate concerns about functional reliability, professional role displacement, and accountability ambiguity. The moderating role of organizational readiness further highlights that training, tool optimization, and institutional governance remain critical yet often unmet. These findings necessitate coordinated efforts across workforce development, technological design, and regulatory frameworks. Future research should prioritize the development of clinically validated, context-aware GenAI systems that incorporate human-in-the-loop oversight, ensuring that efficiency gains do not compromise the delivery of compassionate, person-centered care. Trial Registration: PROSPERO CRD420251250664; https://www.crd.york.ac.uk/PROSPERO/view/CRD420251250664 <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/747940bd8a0ec5c7fa9e03e5e1df0318" />

### [Patient Engagement in a Virtual Cardiac Rehabilitation Platform: Secondary Analysis of the ERA-Nudge Trial](https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e64502)

- Source: `jmir_mhealth` · published `2026-11-06` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e64502`

Background: Mobile health interventions have the potential to motivate healthy behaviors. Optimal strategies for engaging patients in cardiac rehabilitation (CR) through mobile health interventions remain unclear. Objective: This study aimed to examine patient engagement in a mobile app platform for CR across patient characteristics and by the method of delivery of nudge messages over the 12 weeks of CR. Methods: The authors analyzed data from the ERA-Nudge (Enhancing Rehabilitation Adherence Through Optimized Nudge) study, a pragmatic randomized trial conducted at an urban safety-net hospital testing nudges (short messages designed using behavioral economics to elicit behavior change) to enhance CR adherence and log healthy activity. Trial patients were given access to a smartphone-based app. Nudges were initially sent as app notifications. Later in the study, patients received nudges as text messages. We assessed patient characteristics associated with app engagement, and we compared time spent in the CR app among patients receiving nudges as app notifications vs text messages. The trial enrolled patients who could engage with messages in either English or Spanish at an urban safety-net hospital with a qualifying CR diagnosis and a smartphone from May 2019 to May 2023. We describe minutes spent in the app across patient characteristics and by type of nudge delivery, and we compare the 2 approaches using unadjusted and multivariate negative binomial regression over the 12 weeks of CR. Results: In total, 223 patients received nudges: 74 via app notification and 149 via text message. Overall, patients spent an average of 219 (SD 328) minutes in the app, and 3 (1.3%) patients used the app for zero minutes. Patients receiving only app nudges spent a mean of 167 (SD 365) minutes in the app, and patients receiving text messages and app nudges spent 245 (SD 306) minutes in the app (unadjusted ratio 1.5, 95% CI 1.1-2.0; =.02). After adjusting for age category, sex, primary language, CR location (hospital vs home), and highest education achieved, Spanish-preferring patients, patients with some college education, and patients receiving text messages spent more time in the app. Patients receiving nudges as text messages and app notifications spent an adjusted ratio of 1.8 (95% CI 1.2-2.5; =.002) times as many minutes in the app compared to those getting nudges as app notifications. Conclusions: Among an urban safety-net population, text message nudges were associated with greater time in the app compared to nudges delivered as app notifications. These findings suggest that text messages may result in greater patient engagement for mobile health interventions. Trial Registration: ClinicalTrials.gov NCT03834155; https://clinicaltrials.gov/study/NCT03834155 International Registered Report Identifier (IRRID): RR2-10.1016/j.ahj.2025.05.010

### [Patient-Friendly Simplifications of Outpatient Reports Using Large Language Models via Few-Shot Prompting: Blinded Comparative Evaluation](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e101882)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-11-06` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e101882`

Background: Effective communication between clinicians and patients plays an important role in the successful delivery of health care. In geriatric medicine, where multimorbidity, polypharmacy, and functional limitations often make treatment plans complex, AI simplifications of clinical reports into patient-friendly language offer a promising way to improve communication. Objective: This study investigated whether AI could generate patient-friendly simplifications of outpatient reports that were comparable to those authored by human experts. Methods: Gemma-3-27B-it, an instruction fine-tuned open-source large language model (LLM) by Google, was used, and few-shot prompting was applied to enable the generation of patient-friendly simplifications of 64 deidentified geriatric outpatient reports written in German while preserving clinical meaning. LLM-generated simplifications were compared with those produced by 2 medical experts in a blinded evaluation. Quality was assessed using three approaches: (1) embedding-based semantic similarity to the original report, (2) blinded expert review by 2 clinicians, and (3) review by patient representatives, with the latter 2 using a standardized Likert-scale questionnaire. Results: AI-generated simplifications were comparable to geriatrician-authored simplifications regarding semantic similarity (=.02) and were preferred by patient representatives (&lt;.001) as well as one expert reviewer (=.001). Expert reviewers perceived physician-authored simplifications as more complete (&lt;.001), a finding that correlated with text length (&lt;.001). Conclusions: This study demonstrates the feasibility of using LLMs via few-shot prompting to simplify medical reports for improved perceived patient comprehension in geriatric care. Future prospective studies should examine whether such AI-driven simplifications positively impact objective health-related outcomes, adherence, and patient engagement. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/19189377aa08643c6a93037d087f53b4" />

Siemens Healthineers said Thursday it will rebrand its in vitro diagnostics business and establish the unit as a standalone organization by mid-2028. The rebranded business, Olyrian Diagnostics, will sell diagnostics for laboratory and point-of-care testing. Clients include Quest Diagnostics, which recently extended its contract by five years. Siemens Healthineers is rebranding its remaining business. Next year, the company will start a three-year transition to rebrand as Healthineers, dropping Siemens from its name. The rebrand coincides with a plan announced late last year for Siemens to give up its controlling stake in Siemens Healthineers. Shareholders will vote about the plan to spin off Siemens Healthineers shares at an annual meeting in February.

### [Cangrelor versus crushed ticagrelor in acute myocardial infarction complicated by cardiogenic shock (DAPT-SHOCK-AMI): a randomised, double-blind trial.](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0140-6736(26)00976-1)

- Source: `lancet_abstracts` · published `2026-11-07` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `new` · identity: `pmid:42998670`

Acute myocardial infarction complicated by cardiogenic shock (AMI-CS) can compromise the administration and absorption of oral P2Y12 inhibitors and carries high ischaemic and mortality risks. This trial assessed whether intravenous cangrelor was non-inferior to crushed oral ticagrelor for short-term clinical outcomes and whether it provided faster and more consistent platelet inhibition in patients with AMI-CS. DAPT-SHOCK-AMI was a double-blind trial conducted at 29 hospitals across five European countries (Czech Republic, France, Germany, Poland, and Slovakia). Patients older than 18 years with AMI-CS were randomly assigned (1:1 ratio, stratified by centre, age, sex, presence or absence of ST-segment elevation, and mechanical ventilation) to cangrelor (30 μg/kg bolus followed by an infusion of 4 μg/kg per min for at least 2 h) or crushed ticagrelor (180 mg loading dose). The primary laboratory endpoint was a platelet reactivity index of less than 50% at the end of primary percutaneous coronary intervention. The primary clinical endpoint was death, myocardial infarction, or ischaemic stroke at 30 days. The primary clinical endpoint was analysed for non-inferiority with a prespecified margin of 1 percentage point among all randomly assigned patients who received study treatment. The study is registered with ClinicalTrials.gov (NCT03551964) and EudraCT (2018-002161-19); recruitment and 1-year follow-up are complete, and 5-year follow-up is ongoing. Between Aug 1, 2018, and Jan 19, 2024, 605 patients (137 [23%] were female) were randomly assigned to cangrelor (n=298) or ticagrelor (n=307); no surviving patients were lost to the 1-year follow-up. The primary laboratory endpoint was analysed in 56 (19%) patients in the cangrelor group and 68 (22%) in the ticagrelor group, and was achieved in 56 (100%) of 56 patients receiving cangrelor and 15 (22%) of 68 receiving ticagrelor (p<0·0001). The 30-day primary clinical endpoint occurred in 112 (38%) of 298 patients receiving cangrelor and 126 (41%) of 307 receiving ticagrelor; non-inferiority was not demonstrated (difference -4 percentage points [90% CI -10 to 3]; Pnon-inferiority=0·13). Major bleeding at 30 days occurred in 19 (6%) of 298 patients receiving cangrelor and 16 (5%) of 307 receiving ticagrelor. To our knowledge, this is the first randomised trial to compare P2Y12 inhibitor strategies specifically in patients with AMI-CS. Cangrelor achieved effective platelet inhibition more frequently than crushed ticagrelor at the end of primary percutaneous coronary intervention. Non-inferiority of cangrelor versus crushed ticagrelor was not demonstrated for the primary 30-day clinical endpoint. These trial findings can inform future trials of P2Y12 inhibition strategies in patients with AMI-CS. Czech Republic Ministry of Health, Charles University (Czech Republic), and NextGenerationEU.

### [Long-Term Survival After Initial Invasive or Conservative Management of Stable Coronary Disease in ISCHEMIA-EXTEND.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.20375)

- Source: `jama_abstracts` · published `2026-11-07` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `new` · identity: `pmid:42998244`

It is unknown whether routine invasive management of chronic coronary disease improves long-term survival when added to guideline-directed medical therapy. To compare the effects of initial invasive and conservative strategies on long-term mortality in patients with chronic coronary disease. In this long-term follow-up study of the International Study of Comparative Health Effectiveness with Medical and Invasive Approaches (ISCHEMIA), an international trial of patients with chronic coronary disease, moderate or severe ischemia, left ventricular ejection fraction of 35% or more, and no left main coronary artery disease, unacceptable angina, or recent acute coronary syndrome, participants were randomized at 320 sites in 37 countries beginning August 2012 and followed up through December 2025. Data were analyzed using the intention-to-treat principle. ISCHEMIA randomized patients to an initial invasive strategy of guideline-directed medical therapy plus coronary angiography and revascularization (when feasible) or an initial conservative strategy with guideline-directed medical therapy alone, with invasive management for failure of medical therapy. All-cause, cardiovascular, and noncardiovascular mortality were analyzed using cumulative incidence estimates, Cox and Fine-Gray models, bayesian analysis, and restricted mean survival time. Among 5179 participants, median age was 64 (IQR, 58-70) years; 23% were female, 42% had diabetes, and median ejection fraction was 60% (IQR, 55%-65%). During 9-year median follow-up (range, 1 day to 13.4 years), 1071 deaths occurred (782 during extended follow-up): 672 cardiovascular, 359 noncardiovascular, and 40 unclassified. All-cause 11-year mortality was not significantly different between groups (25.8% invasive vs 26.9% conservative; estimated difference, -1.0 percentage point [95% CI, -4.2 to 2.1 percentage points]; adjusted hazard ratio [AHR], 0.97 [95% CI, 0.86-1.09]). By bayesian analysis, the posterior probability of more than 1 percentage-point survival benefit to invasive vs conservative was low (18%). Cardiovascular mortality at 11 years was 15.8% in the invasive group vs 17.1% in the conservative group (estimated difference, -1.4 percentage points [95% CI, -4.0 to 1.3 percentage points]; AHR, 0.88 [95% CI, 0.75-1.02]). Noncardiovascular mortality at 11 years was 9.1% in the invasive group vs 8.1% in the conservative group (estimated difference, 1.0 percentage point [95% CI, -0.9 to 2.9 percentage points]; AHR, 1.18 [95% CI, 0.96-1.45]). Eleven-year restricted mean survival was not significantly different (estimated difference, 14 days [95% CI, -41 to 70 days]). In this study, long-term all-cause mortality in patients with high-risk chronic coronary disease was not significantly different after routine invasive vs initial conservative management. ClinicalTrials.gov Identifier: NCT04894877.

### [MambaSleepCVD for prediction of long term cardiovascular outcomes from polysomnography](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03450-3)

- Source: `npj_digital_medicine` · published `2026-11-07` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03450-3`

Long-term cardiovascular disease (CVD) risk prediction remains a major global clinical priority. This study investigates how much prognostic information full-night polysomnography (PSG) carries after explicitly controlling demographic confounding, and whether combining specialized unimodal representations with continuous fusion mechanisms can effectively leverage it. We adopt a modular framework based on Coupled Mamba for simultaneous cross-modal and temporal fusion, capturing long-range dependencies and dynamic interactions between physiological modalities throughout sleep. With this capability, we assess whether specialized supervised representations from task-specific unimodal encoders offer prognostic value comparable to task-agnostic multimodal self-supervised pre-training. Validated on the Sleep Heart Health Study (SHHS) dataset, our approach achieves performance comparable to or better than current state-of-the-art architectures in the fully supervised, data-abundant regime, although the cardiac channel alone accounts for most of the discriminative signal. More importantly, we show that in the reference dataset chronological age strongly drives survival ranking; when provided as an explicit input, it can create a shortcut that obscures the contribution of PSG-derived physiology. Ultimately, our findings suggest that supervised unimodal experts integrated through a fusion engine provide a flexible pathway for prognostic risk ranking from sleep recordings, while reliable CVD screening will require independent external cohorts and explicit confounder control.

### [Wrist photoplethysmography encodes dietary information](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03425-4)

- Source: `npj_digital_medicine` · published `2026-11-07` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03425-4`

Whether wearable photoplethysmography (PPG) encodes dietary information remains unknown. We trained a language model on 1.1M meals to align PPG with meal descriptions. Near-meal PPG carries a meal-specific signal beyond individual traits; predictability decreases farther from meals. This transfers to dietary tasks: PPG increases AUC by 11% for a caloric-intake proxy and satiety across held-out and independent cohorts. Wearable PPG may augment text-based logging, compensating for brief descriptions.

### [Effects of semaglutide on cardiac structure and function in heart failure with preserved ejection fraction and obesity: a pre-specified cardiac magnetic resonance substudy of the CAMEO-SEMA trial](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04787-8)

- Source: `nature_medicine` · published `2026-11-08` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04787-8`

Glucagon-like peptide-1 receptor agonists improve symptoms in patients with obesity and heart failure with preserved ejection fraction (HFpEF). We recently demonstrated that semaglutide reduces exertional pulmonary artery wedge pressure (PAWP) in obesity-related HFpEF, but potential effects of semaglutide on cardiac structure and function that may underlie these hemodynamic improvements remain unclear. In this prespecified cardiovascular magnetic resonance (CMR) substudy of the randomized CAMEO-SEMA trial, 49 participants (33 semaglutide, 16 placebo, 28 female) underwent CMR at baseline and 52 weeks, within 1 day of invasive exercise hemodynamic assessment. The prespecified primary CMR endpoint was change in left atrial (LA) reservoir strain; other prespecified CMR endpoints included LV filling dynamics, which were assessed by early filling rate indexed to end-diastolic volume (eFR/EDV). Compared with placebo, semaglutide improved LA reservoir strain (+5.7% [95% CI, +0.3 to +9.7]; P=0.037) and eFR/EDV (+0.64 s−1 [95% CI, +0.42 to +1.00]; P&lt;0.0001). Improvements in LA reservoir strain and LV filling dynamics were associated with reductions in exercise PAWP (r=−0.46, P&lt;0.001 and r=−0.65, P&lt;0.0001, respectively). With respect to other prespecified CMR endpoints, semaglutide reduced LV mass, restored more physiologic LV geometry, and improved right ventricular function and biventricular myocardial mechanics. The results of this CMR substudy indicate that in patients with obesity-related HFpEF, semaglutide improves LA function and LV diastolic filling dynamics, identifying improvements in myocardial function that may contribute to semaglutide’s improvement of hemodynamics. ClinicalTrials.gov number: NCT05371496. As presented at the 2026 AHA Scientific Sessions, a cardiac magnetic resonance substudy of the CAMEO-SEMA trial showed that semaglutide significantly improved cardiac structure and function, including left atrial reservoir strain and left ventricular filling dynamics, in individuals with heart failure with preserved ejection fraction and obesity.

### [Hemodynamic effects of semaglutide in individuals with heart failure with preserved ejection fraction and obesity: a placebo-controlled randomized clinical trial](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04762-3)

- Source: `nature_medicine` · published `2026-11-08` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04762-3`

The glucagon-like peptide-1 receptor agonist semaglutide improves symptoms in heart failure with preserved ejection fraction (HFpEF) through unclear mechanisms. Here, in this double-blind, placebo-controlled trial, we randomly assigned 81 patients (47 women) with HFpEF and obesity to semaglutide (2.4 mg weekly) or placebo for 52 weeks. The primary outcome was the change in invasively measured pulmonary artery wedge pressure (PAWP), a direct measure of cardiac congestion, during low-intensity exercise. Additional mechanistic outcomes included other hemodynamic measures and hemodynamic HFpEF remission (defined as normal PAWP under conditions of both rest and exercise at 52 weeks). At baseline, PAWP was 17.5 ± 4.3 mmHg at rest and 31.7 ± 5.2 mmHg during exercise. After 52 weeks, the mean change in PAWP during exercise was −9.8 (95% confidence interval (CI): −11.3 to −8.2) mmHg with semaglutide and +0.9 (95% CI: −1.3 to 3.0) mmHg with placebo (estimated difference, −10.6 mmHg; 95% CI: −13.3 to −8.0; P &lt; 0.0001). As compared to placebo, semaglutide also decreased resting PAWP (estimated difference, −7.1 mmHg; 95% CI: −9.8 to −4.4; P &lt; 0.0001), and semaglutide-treated patients were more likely to experience hemodynamic remission (53.7% versus 7.4%, odds ratio = 14.1, 95% CI: 3.0−134.6, P &lt; 0.001). The results from this trial indicate that, in patients with HFpEF and obesity, treatment with semaglutide reduced left heart filling pressures during exercise and at rest and was associated with hemodynamic HFpEF remission in over half of treated patients. ClinicalTrials.gov identifier: NCT05371496 . As presented at the 2026 American Heart Association Scientific Sessions, in a placebo-controlled randomized clinical trial, semaglutide significantly improved pulmonary artery wedge pressure and other hemodynamic parameters during low-intensity exercise in individuals with heart failure with preserved ejection fraction and obesity.

### [An ulcerative colitis taxonomy defined by individual patient-reported response trajectories](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04741-8)

- Source: `nature_medicine` · published `2026-11-09` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `new` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04741-8`

A barrier to precision medicine for ulcerative colitis (UC) is the inability to predict and mechanistically explain treatment responses. Using patient-reported outcomes (PROs) from a phase 3b comparative trial of 768 participants, we identified four distinct patient response-trajectory groups for vedolizumab (anti-integrin) and adalimumab (antitumor necrosis factor). Validation in a second phase 3 trial of 576 participants linked PRO-defined vedolizumab trajectories to long-term disease complications. Bulk, single cell, and spatial transcriptomics and proteomics identified vascular inflammation as associated with vedolizumab nonresponse through interactions between SPP1+ macrophages and endothelial cells. In two separate real-world cohorts of 4,981 and 3,854 patients with UC, concomitant use of renin–angiotensin system inhibitors (modulators of vascular inflammation) was associated with reduced long-term complications for vedolizumab but not anticytokine therapies. These findings define a response-trajectory-based taxonomy that requires prospective validation for clinical utility and applications for selecting vedolizumab combination therapy in UC. An analysis of patient-reported trajectories of response to vedolizumab in ulcerative colitis defines disease endotypes and identifies vascular inflammation as a potential resistance mechanism.

### [Deep learning-based gait analysis predicts multisystem health phenotypes](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04725-8)

- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `new` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04725-8`

We developed a gait foundation model from three-dimensional skeletal motion data of 3,328 deeply phenotyped adults, recorded using a depth camera during five motor tasks. Embeddings significantly predicted 1,887 of 3,210 phenotypic targets and provided predictive gains across all 18 body systems in men and 17 out of 18 in women, while also improving prediction of clinical diagnoses and medication use.

### [FDA seeks input on telesurgery, autonomous robotics](https://www.medtechdive.com/news/fda-seeks-input-on-telesurgery-autonomous-robotics/874124/)

- Source: `medtech_dive_primary` · published `2026-11-09` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `new` · identity: `url:https://www.medtechdive.com/news/fda-seeks-input-on-telesurgery-autonomous-robotics/874124/`

The Food and Drug Administration on Friday issued a discussion paper on robotic medical devices with autonomous or remote teleoperation capabilities and is requesting feedback on the systems’ benefits and risks. Autonomous and remote capabilities bring complexity to the benefit-risk consideration, the FDA said. The agency wants input on factors including technical considerations, terminology, risk mitigation and how to develop policy that is responsive to innovation while maintaining device safety and effectiveness. The FDA is asking the robotics community to submit comments on the discussion paper by Jan. 5. Ahead of that deadline, a public workshop to discuss the topics raised in the paper is scheduled for Dec. 2-3.

### [Machine Learning Models for Glycemic Prediction in Type 1 and Type 2 Diabetes: Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e100653)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-11-09` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `new` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e100653`

Background: Machine learning models are increasingly used for glycemic event prediction and continuous glucose forecasting, but targets, populations, horizons, validation, and uncertainty reporting remain heterogeneous. Objective: This study aimed to evaluate machine learning models for glycemic prediction in type 1, type 2, and mixed diabetes populations while distinguishing the full evidence map from the quantitatively analyzable subset. Methods: We searched 9 English- and Chinese-language databases from inception to April 12, 2026, for studies predicting future glycemic events or glucose values in people with diabetes. Continuous forecasting and binary event prediction were analyzed separately. Root-mean-square error (RMSE) was the principal continuous end point; mean absolute error was supportive. Area under the receiver operating characteristic curve was synthesized using a Bayesian random-effects model because analyzable 2×2 data were sparse. Two reviewers (HY and DA) independently applied PROBAST (Prediction Model Risk of Bias Assessment Tool) to 19 quantitative-source reports (28 assessment units). Results: We identified 787 records, assessed 310 full texts, and included 299 reports representing 270 studies; 19 reports contributed to quantitative synthesis. The review included 128 type 1, 26 type 2, 78 mixed, and 38 unclear-diabetes-type studies. Pooled RMSE was 1.18 mmol/L (95% CI 1.01‐1.34; 37 effects), supportive mean absolute error was 0.90 mmol/L (95% CI 0.78‐1.02; 20 effects), and Bayesian pooled area under the receiver operating characteristic curve was 0.926 (95% credible interval 0.877‐0.957). Supportive sensitivity and specificity were 0.77 and 0.80. Of 28 PROBAST units, 24 were high risk, 2 unclear, and 2 low. Post hoc exploratory within-report analysis estimated an RMSE increase of 0.481 mmol/L per additional 30 minutes (95% CI 0.213‐0.749), but overlap sensitivity reduced certainty. In the 270-study clinical readiness audit, external validation occurred in 4.0% (10/251), prospective evaluation in 0.7% (2/270), calibration in 4.8% (13/270), decision-curve or net-benefit analysis in 2.6% (7/270), and implementation or human-factors evaluation in 2.6% (7/270). Conclusions: Glycemic prediction studies show forecasting and discrimination signals, but heterogeneity, high risk of bias, incomplete uncertainty reporting, and limited external and prospective validation preclude deployable benchmarks. Pooled estimates should be viewed as summaries of analyzable literature. Future studies need transparent horizon, threshold, calibration, validation, uncertainty, and implementation reporting. Trial Registration: PROSPERO CRD420261366978; https://www.crd.york.ac.uk/PROSPERO/view/CRD420261366978 <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/a33d89c528a74289a096a0840abba334" />

### [Novocure denied FDA approval for metastatic cancer treatment](https://www.medtechdive.com/news/novocure-denied-fda-approval-for-metastatic-cancer-treatment/874127/)

- Source: `medtech_dive_primary` · published `2026-11-09` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `new` · identity: `url:https://www.medtechdive.com/news/novocure-denied-fda-approval-for-metastatic-cancer-treatment/874127/`

Novocure has been denied Food and Drug Administration approval for its device intended to treat non-small cell lung cancer that has spread to the brain. The FDA’s not approvable letter cited uncertainty around the degree of clinical benefit provided by Novocure’s device, Optune Mya, the company said Monday. The system uses low-energy electrical fields to disrupt cancer cells’ ability to grow and divide. The regulator did not raise concerns about the device’s safety profile, Novocure said. The company intends to meet with the FDA to determine if there are other options that would support approval without additional clinical studies.

### [PancreaGuide for Computed Tomography Report Interpretation and Perioperative Consultation in Pancreatic Cancer: Development and Retrospective Evaluation of a Knowledge-Augmented Large Language Model Agent](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e101607)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-11-09` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `new` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e101607`

Background: Accurate interpretation of pancreatic cancer status and resectability-related information, together with effective perioperative communication, remains a major challenge in pancreatic cancer care. In routine practice, unstructured computed tomography (CT) reports and complex clinical information may limit the accessibility and consistency of clinical information delivery and increase the communication burden on clinicians. Large language models (LLMs) offer new opportunities for clinical information support; however, existing applications are often task-specific and rarely combine CT report interpretation with perioperative consultation within a unified patient-oriented framework. Objective: This study aimed to develop and retrospectively evaluate PancreaGuide, a knowledge-augmented LLM agent designed to support clinician-supervised CT report-based interpretation of pancreatic cancer resectability-related information and standardized perioperative consultation. Methods: We developed PancreaGuide, an agent-based framework integrating structured prompting, retrieval-augmented generation, and a domain-specific pancreatic surgery knowledge base to support clinical information interpretation and perioperative consultation. A retrospective cohort of 444 patients who underwent contrast-enhanced CT at West China Hospital between 2023 and 2025 was analyzed. Three LLMs, ChatGPT, DeepSeek, and Gemini, were evaluated in both base and PancreaGuide-integrated configurations. CT report-based interpretation of resectability-related information was evaluated using accuracy, sensitivity, specificity, positive predictive value, negative predictive value, -score, receiver operating characteristic curves (ROCs), and decision curve analysis (DCA) using a surgery-based proxy reference. Perioperative consultation quality was evaluated across 5 dimensions: accuracy, completeness, consistency, safety, and robustness. Results: Higher classification metric point estimates were observed after integration of the PancreaGuide framework across all 3 backbone models. In CT report-based interpretation of resectability-related information, Gemini+ achieved the highest accuracy of 87.4% (95% CI 0.840‐0.902) and a sensitivity of 95.9% (95% CI 0.927‐0.978). All models showed improved sensitivity and specificity estimates after PancreaGuide integration, with improved agreement between model-generated interpretations and observed surgical management status. ROC and DCA suggested improved discrimination and estimated net benefit using the surgery-based proxy reference. In perioperative consultation, PancreaGuide-integrated models received higher expert-rated scores than their corresponding base models across all 5 evaluation dimensions. Gemini+ achieved the highest overall score (mean 3.88, SD 0.38), followed by ChatGPT+ (mean 3.8, SD 10.36) and DeepSeek+ (mean 3.75, SD 0.35). The greatest improvements were observed in completeness and robustness, with consistent gains across clinical consultation domains. Conclusions: PancreaGuide provides a unified, knowledge-augmented framework for clinician-supervised CT report-based interpretation of resectability-related information and perioperative information support across multiple LLM backbones. The CT-based findings should be interpreted as agreement with observed surgical management status rather than validation against an independently adjudicated anatomical resectability. PancreaGuide may serve as an auxiliary information interpretation framework to improve the accessibility and consistency of clinical information delivery, while it should not replace formal radiological assessment, multidisciplinary evaluation, or clinician judgment. Trial Registration: Chinese Clinical Trial Registry ChiCTR2600119597; https://tinyurl.com/5n72btcb <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/18b6db060d3670d6ca66b33e40769eaa" />

### [Relating to AI in Spiritual Care](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e113467)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-11-09` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `new` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e113467`

In response to a recently published Delphi study on AI in spiritual care, this commentary focuses on ways to perceive the relationship between AI and human beings in the context of spiritual care. We explore this relationship by framing it within the philosophy of Martin Buber. Because AI has no lived experience or existential crises of its own, we question whether a truly fulfilling encounter between a human being and an AI model is possible when the relationship is inherently asymmetrical on an existential level. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/ee7842d03d5ed4988e561105946400c9" />

### [Resistance exercise to attenuate lean mass loss during tirzepatide therapy for obesity: a randomized controlled trial](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04752-5)

- Source: `nature_medicine` · published `2026-11-09` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `new` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04752-5`

Incretin mimetics as obesity therapy induce substantial weight and fat loss but also decrease lean soft tissue mass, possibly impairing skeletal muscle function. Here, in this two-arm randomized controlled trial of adding resistance exercise (REx) to tirzepatide (T), 120 adults with overweight or obesity were treated with tirzepatide and randomized to thrice-weekly resistance exercise training or usual activities for 40 weeks. Tirzepatide was safe, and most participants tolerated up-titration to 15 mg weekly. Adherence to resistance exercise was 91%. Weight loss achieved was similar (T = −22.61 kg, T+REx = −22.28 kg, P = 0.694). For primary outcomes, loss of total fat mass was similar (T = −17.19 kg, T+REx = −17.98 kg, P = 0.700; between-group difference = −0.77 kg, 95% confidence interval (CI): −2.14 kg to 0.60 kg), but resistance exercise reduced loss of total lean soft tissue mass (T = −4.67 kg, T+REx = −3.57 kg, P &lt; 0.001; between-group difference = 1.09 kg, 95% CI: 0.43−1.75 kg). For related endpoints, resistance exercise reduced loss of appendicular lean soft tissue mass (T = −3.55 kg, T+REx = −2.92 kg, P &lt; 0.001). Upper and lower body muscle strength decreased with tirzepatide (bench press −4.09 kg, lat pulldown −4.42 kg, knee extension −4.42 kg) but increased with T+REx (+4.68 kg, +8.58 kg, +5.88 kg, respectively, all P &lt; 0.001 versus tirzepatide). In conclusion, resistance exercise training attenuated loss of total lean soft tissue mass, representing an effective adjunct to tirzepatide anti-obesity therapy. Australian New Zealand Clinical Trials Registry identifier: ACTRN12623001236684 . Participants undergoing resistance exercise while receiving tirzepatide lost less lean mass than participants receiving tirzepatide alone.

### [Rural Health Transformation Program: Year 2 Awards](https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/rural-health-transformation-program-year-2-awards)

- Source: `cms_fact_sheets` · published `2026-11-09` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `new` · identity: `url:https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/rural-health-transformation-program-year-2-awards`

Rural Health Transformation Program: Year 2 Awards The Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS) announced $10 billion in this year’s awards to all 50 states through the Rural Health Transformation Program (RHTP), continuing this historic investment to strengthen rural healthcare across America. Established through the Working Families Tax Cuts Act, RHTP provides $50 billion over five years to help states improve healthcare access, quality, and outcomes in rural communities. Rural health challenges extend beyond geography and long travel distances. Many communities also face shortages of health professionals, high rates of chronic disease, limited specialty and behavioral health capacity, barriers to healthy living, aging infrastructure, workforce recruitment and retention challenges, and fragmented care delivery. RHTP gives states resources and flexibility to address these interconnected challenges while establishing clear expectations for implementation, accountability, and measurable progress. RHTP at a Glance $50 billion over five years for rural health transformation. Each year $10 billion is awarded from fiscal year (FY) 2026 through FY 2030. All funds must be expended by Sept. 30, 2032, or they will be returned to the U.S. Treasury. All 50 states participate in the program. At least $100 million per state, per year, is given through equal baseline funding. State-led transformation plans address the needs of each state’s rural communities. Annual reporting and funding determinations help CMS monitor implementation and progress throughout the five-year program. RHTP supports transformation across the rural healthcare delivery system. It is not limited to one type of provider or designed simply to provide short-term operating support to individual hospitals. States are pursuing strategies involving hospitals, clinics, emergency medical services, behavioral health providers, community organizations, workforce partners, technology, and other parts of the rural health ecosystem. What Does RHTP Support? RHTP is designed to address the interconnected challenges facing rural communities and strengthen healthcare delivery across five strategic goals: Make rural America healthy again: Support rural health innovations and new access points to promote preventive health and address root causes of diseases. Projects will use evidence-based, outcomes-driven interventions to improve disease prevention, chronic disease management, behavioral health, and prenatal care. Sustainable access: Help rural providers become long-term access points for care by improving efficiency and sustainability. With RHTP support, rural facilities work together — or with high-quality regional systems — to share or coordinate operations, technology, primary and specialty care, and emergency services. Workforce development: Attract and retain a highly skilled healthcare workforce by strengthening recruitment and retention of healthcare providers in rural communities. Help rural providers practice at the top of their license and develop a broader set of providers to serve a rural community’s needs, such as community health workers, pharmacists, and individuals trained to help patients navigate the healthcare system. Innovative care: Spark the growth of innovative care models to improve health outcomes, coordinate care, and promote flexible care arrangements. Develop and implement payment mechanisms incentivizing providers or Accountable Care Organizations (ACOs) to reduce healthcare costs, improve quality of care, and shift care to lower cost settings. Tech innovation: Foster use of innovative technologies that promote efficient care delivery, data security, and access to digital health tools by rural facilities, providers, and patients. Projects support access to remote care, improve data sharing, strengthen cybersecurity, and invest in emerging technologies. The five strategic goals set the overall direction for RHTP. States must use RHTP funds for at least three approved uses under the program. These include prevention and chronic disease management; rural workforce recruitment and retention; technology-enabled care and health IT; behavioral health and substance use disorder services; innovative care and payment models; and investments that help rural communities strengthen and right-size their healthcare delivery systems. How Does RHTP Funding Work? Congress established a two-part structure for distributing the $10 billion available each year: 50% — Baseline Funding Half of the annual funding is distributed equally among all approved states. With all 50 states participating in RHTP, each state receives $100 million in baseline funding each year. 50% — Workload Funding The other half is allocated using factors established in the RHTP Notice of Funding Opportunity (NOFO). These factors account for differences among states and include: Characteristics of a state’s rural population and healthcare facilities; Initiatives included in the state’s rural health transformation plan; Certain state policy actions; and Other technical, data-driven factors described in the NOFO. The baseline and workload portions together determine each state’s annual RHTP award. What Is Different About Year 2? The initial FY 2026 awards reflected the statutory funding structure and evaluation of states’ plans. Subsequent funding is also determined pursuant to statutory requirements and is informed by states’ progress implementing their approved plans. All 50 states submitted annual reports to CMS documenting their progress during the program’s first year. CMS uses annual reporting to monitor implementation and inform funding for subsequent budget periods. For initiative-based portions of the funding framework, CMS uses an established checkpoint model to assess implementation progress. Checkpoints measure concrete steps in implementation, such as establishing program governance, developing and receiving approval of project plans, launching initiatives, reaching milestones, establishing methods for measuring results, and planning for long-term sustainability. This approach recognizes that transformation occurs over time. Early implementation may focus on building the capacity needed to produce longer-term changes in healthcare access, delivery, and outcomes. Why Can State Awards Change From Year to Year? RHTP was designed so that a portion of the funding is competitively allocated and can change from year to year. The equal baseline portion remains the same for participating states. The workload portion can change as CMS applies the program’s annual funding methodology, including factors that reflect implementation progress and progress toward state policy actions. As a result, a state’s total award may increase, decrease, or remain similar from one year to the next. A decrease in one year does not determine a state’s funding trajectory for the remainder of the program; funding is dynamic as determined annually under the program’s established methodology. Annual funding determinations do not change the program’s underlying goal: supporting all participating states as they implement long-term strategies to transform rural healthcare. Year 1: Building the Foundation During Year 1, states worked to establish governance structures, build partnerships, hire staff, develop and approve project plans, establish contracts and subawards, purchase equipment and technology, launch initiatives, and begin implementing programs in rural communities. Because states entered RHTP with different rural populations, workforce capacity, disease burdens, health system designs, and implementation plans, progress does not look identical in every state. Some initiatives are already reaching patients and communities, while others have focused initially on building the infrastructure needed to deliver new or restored services and produce measurable results over the remaining years of the program. Learn more about how states are putting their rural health transformation plans into action through CMS’ state snapshots and additional state progress materials, available at https://www.medicaid.gov/resources-for-states/rural-health . Understanding RHTP RHTP addresses more than geographic barriers. Long travel distances can make accessing care difficult in rural communities, but rural health challenges also include health professional shortages, chronic disease burden, limited specialty and behavioral health services, aging infrastructure, workforce instability, and fragmented delivery systems. RHTP gives states tools to address these challenges within their states. RHTP is about more than hospitals. Rural hospitals are an important part of rural health systems, but RHTP supports broader transformation involving providers, workforce, technology, emergency services, prevention, behavioral health, community partnerships, and new care-delivery models. States are in the driver’s seat. Each state developed its own rural health transformation plan based on the needs of its rural communities. CMS establishes program requirements and provides oversight, while states determine how to implement their approved strategies within those requirements. RHTP is a transformation program, not a replacement for other federal healthcare funding. RHTP funds must support approved transformation activities and may not supplant existing government funding sources or programs. Awards come with ongoing accountability. RHTP is a cooperative agreement, meaning CMS remains substantially involved throughout implementation. States must report progress, comply with federal requirements, and use funding for approved purposes. CMS provides ongoing technical assistance and monitors implementation through dedicated Project Officers, reporting, financial oversight, and other program controls. Funding, obligations and accountability. States must obligate RHTP funds within the applicable budget period. Funds that are properly obligated within that period generally remain available for expenditure through the end of the following fiscal year, subject to program requirements. CMS has published state obligation data through July 31, 2026, as a point-in-time look at states’ use of Year 1 funding. These figures do not, by themselves, measure a state’s overall progress or determine its Year 2 award. Unexpended funds are subject to redistribution under the statute. This redistribution process is based on the same methodology used to determine each state’s allocation amount and would be allocated to the other states in a different distribution cycle. It is distinct from the annual process CMS uses to determine each state’s next RHTP award. What Happens Next? This year’s awards mark the next phase of implementation as states build on the groundwork and partnerships established during the first year. States will begin more detailed quarterly reporting in November 2026. States will continue submitting required quarterly and annual reports to CMS throughout the program. CMS will use information submitted by states to monitor implementation, provide technical assistance, assess compliance, and inform annual funding determinations. Over time, this data will help CMS assess progress toward the access, workforce, health, technology, and sustainability goals states committed to pursue through their transformation plans. New RHTP funding allocations continue through FY 2030, and all RHTP funds must be expended by Sept. 30, 2032, or they will be returned to the U.S. Treasury. Additional Resources • Rural Health Transformation Program Webpage • RHTP Notice of Funding Opportunity • September 2026 RHTP Post-Award Frequently Asked Questions • RHTP Reporting and Rescoring Webinar • State Program Snapshots • RHTP Obligation Funds Report, Year 1 • State Data Maps CMS News and Media Group Catherine Howden, Director Media Inquiries Form 202-690-6145

<p><a href="https://medcitynews.com/2026/11/simulation-guided-clinical-trials-is-eu-law-ready/"><img width="600" height="402" src="https://medcitynews.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/7/2020/08/GettyImages-480985277-600x402.jpg" class="attachment-large size-large wp-post-image" alt="" style="max-width: 100%; height: auto!important;" decoding="async" fetchpriority="high" srcset="https://medcitynews.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/7/2020/08/GettyImages-480985277-600x402.jpg 600w, https://medcitynews.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/7/2020/08/GettyImages-480985277-300x201.jpg 300w, https://medcitynews.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/7/2020/08/GettyImages-480985277-768x514.jpg 768w, https://medcitynews.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/7/2020/08/GettyImages-480985277-e1598369408885.jpg 700w" sizes="(max-width: 600px) 100vw, 600px" /></a></p><p>Simulations do not replace human judgment: they inform it, making transparent what would otherwise remain a “black box.” EU law does not require them today; but a proportionate embrace, in the right legislative vehicle, with clear methodological standards, and without crushing noncommercial research, could make trials safer, faster, cheaper, and more reliable, without sacrificing rigor.</p> <p>The post <a href="https://medcitynews.com/2026/11/simulation-guided-clinical-trials-is-eu-law-ready/">Simulation-Guided Clinical Trials: Is EU Law Ready?</a> appeared first on <a href="https://medcitynews.com">MedCity News</a>.</p>
